| P2-T11 | Rich text editor | done | task/p2-t11-rich-text-editor | 2026-08-10 | **A design document first, per the task's own card**: `docs/design/rich-text-editor.md` pins down what TECHNICAL-PLAN only named in the abstract ("editor JSON... a version integer... one shared core module") — the stored shape is TipTap's own document JSON directly, never a custom translation layer, restricted to an explicit node/mark allow-list this document defines from scratch (nothing prior specifies one). **`packages/core/src/rich-text/`** (`schema.ts`/`validate.ts`/`render.ts`/`excerpt.ts`/`extract.ts`), pure, no DB/network/DOM, exported from the package root: `parseRichText` (two-pass validation — attribute shape via Zod, then nesting via an explicit `NESTING_RULES` lookup, so a `tableRow` cannot sit directly inside a `paragraph` even though both are individually allow-listed types), `renderRichTextToHtml` (a fixed node-type-to-tag table, resolver callbacks for mention/entityLink/attachment that degrade to plain text with no resolver or a failed lookup, never a broken link), `excerptRichText`, and the decode-safe `extractMentionIds`/`extractAttachments` (TECHNICAL-PLAN §4.10's own line: malformed input returns `[]`, never throws). **The one real integration point today**: `people.updateOwnProfile`'s `bio` field, upgraded from P2-T03's own placeholder (`z.unknown()`) to a `.refine()` against `isValidRichText` — P2-T03's STATUS.md row named this exact module as what it was waiting on. No other rich-text-bearing table exists yet (comments/documents/goal descriptions are later phases), so every other consumer named in TECHNICAL-PLAN §1 (mail, exports, the inbox, the feed) has nothing to wire into yet — the functions exist and are tested against synthetic documents, not against a real caller. **A real architecture-boundary catch while writing the design doc, before any editor code**: TECHNICAL-PLAN §1's own package table restricts `packages/ui` to depending on `packages/method` only, never `packages/core` — where the validator lives. The design doc's first draft assumed the editor component would call `parseRichText` directly; caught and fixed before implementation by taking `validate` as an optional prop instead (the same dependency-injection shape already used for the member/entity search functions and the upload function, since `packages/ui` has no reach into either P2-T05's blob pipeline or a member directory). **`packages/ui/src/rich-text/`**: `RichTextEditor` (`@tiptap/react`), configured to exactly the design doc's allow-list — `@tiptap/starter-kit` with `dropcursor`/`gapcursor`/`undoRedo`/`underline`/`listKeymap` explicitly disabled (all bundled by default in the installed v3, none on the allow-list) and `heading`/`link` configured down to levels 1-3 and `http`/`https`/`mailto` only; `@tiptap/extension-table`; two separately-configured `@tiptap/extension-mention` instances (`@` for members producing `mention` nodes, `#` for entity autolink via `Mention.extend({name: "entityLink"})` producing a distinct node type with `shortId`/`label` attrs — one Mention instance cannot produce two node types even with multiple trigger characters, confirmed by reading the installed extension's own types before assuming otherwise); a hand-written `attachment` `Node.create()` with a React node view (upload spinner / filename+remove, never a link inside the editor itself); and a `/` slash-menu built directly on `@tiptap/suggestion` since it runs editor commands rather than inserting a node. A shared `SuggestionList` component and `createSuggestionRender` factory back all three popups (mention, entity link, slash) with one keyboard-navigation implementation. **Upload flow** (§7): an `attachment` node appears immediately with `status: "uploading"`, flips to `"ready"` with a real `blobId` once the caller's own `uploadFile` prop resolves, or is removed outright on failure — never left pointing at nothing. Submit gating (`hasUploadsInProgress()` on the component's imperative handle) is the literal acceptance line: a submit while an upload is in flight must wait or fail loudly, never drop the attachment silently — enforced here client-side, with the server-side write boundary (`bioInputSchema`'s `isValidRichText` call) as the actual backstop, the same "client warns, server enforces" split as the `validate` prop. **Draft autosave** (§8): `draft.ts` (pure: `fingerprint` — a cheap rolling hash, not a cryptographic digest, matching P2-T10's `avatarToneFor` reasoning — plus `createDraft`/`isDraftUsable`) and `use-draft-autosave.ts` (the `localStorage` wiring). "A draft against changed base content does not resurrect" is `isDraftUsable`'s own fingerprint comparison, not a merge or a warning; expiry (14 days) is checked identically. A dev-only preview page at `apps/web/app/dev/rich-text` (matching P2-T10's `/dev/components` precedent) exercises the whole thing end to end with a mocked member/entity search and a mocked (artificially delayed) upload, wiring the real `isValidRichText` in as the `validate` prop. **New dependencies**: `@tiptap/core`/`pm`/`react`/`starter-kit`/`extension-table`/`extension-mention`/`suggestion`, all pinned to the identical `3.29.2` (every `@tiptap/*` v3 package peer-pins its siblings to an exact version, not a range, confirmed by reading the published peerDependencies rather than assumed) — TipTap itself was already named and approved alongside P2-T10's UI dependencies; picking the exact packages and pins is this task's own implementation decision, the same status as P2-T10's Base UI package resolution. **Verified** under Node 22: typecheck (`packages/core`, `packages/ui`, `apps/web`), Biome, and the boundary gate (229 files) all pass; `packages/config`/`apps/web` suites re-ran clean. `packages/ui`'s own suite ran here (jsdom, no DB) with the new files included: 54 tests total, 13 of them new (draft fingerprint/expiry, the hook's localStorage wiring including the draft-resurrection case, and — genuinely uncertain going in — the TipTap editor mounting cleanly in jsdom with no crash, proven directly rather than assumed). `packages/core`'s own rich-text suite (29 tests: the golden round-trip fixtures, the malicious `javascript:` link and unknown-node/mark rejections, decode-safe extraction on malformed input) was run against a throwaway, uncommitted Vitest config with no `globalSetup` — the real committed `packages/core/vitest.config.ts` still opens a database for the whole package as every prior row notes, but this module is genuinely pure, so this is real, disclosed execution proof rather than "written, never run." The 5 new schema-level tests added to `packages/core/test/people.test.ts` (`bio`'s Zod input schema accepting/rejecting documents directly) could not be run the same way — that file's top-level `beforeEach` provisions a real workspace regardless of which test is selected, so it inherits the whole file's existing Docker/Postgres gap. Licence gate verified manually (`pnpm licenses list --json` diffed against the allow-list by hand, same method as every prior task): every new package and its `@floating-ui/*`/`linkifyjs` transitives are MIT, already allowed. Found and fixed one unrelated pre-existing defect while touching `people.test.ts` for the first time this session: two genuinely dead imports (`activities`, `eq`) Biome caught once run against this file — removed. **A real build-breaking bug, found after this row was first written, by actually running `pnpm build` rather than trusting `tsc`:** the `/dev/rich-text` preview page (a Client Component, needing `useState`) imported `isValidRichText`/`RICH_TEXT_SCHEMA_VERSION` straight from `@openokr/core`. `tsc` saw nothing wrong — both are pure, DB-free functions — but `@openokr/core`'s barrel (`index.ts`) also re-exports `workspaces/memberships.ts` and everything downstream of it, all the way to the Postgres driver, and importing *anything* from that barrel inside a Client Component pulls the whole graph into the browser bundle. The build failed outright: `Module not found: Can't resolve 'net'` (then `tls`, then `util/types`), Node built-ins no browser has. Fixed by moving the `@openokr/core` call into a Server Action (`apps/web/app/dev/rich-text/actions.ts`) and widening `RichTextEditor`'s `validate` prop from `(json) => boolean` to `(json) => boolean | Promise<boolean>` — a Server Action is the only shape any real host could ever have handed this prop anyway, since `@openokr/core` is unreachable from the browser by construction; the synchronous signature was never going to survive contact with a real caller. `docs/design/rich-text-editor.md` §9 corrected to match. Re-ran `pnpm build` after the fix: compiles and generates every route, including both `/dev/*` preview pages, cleanly. **Not verified, flagged rather than silently skipped:** (1) real-browser behaviour (drag-drop upload, the floating suggestion popups' actual position, `?` overlay-style focus trapping inside the editor) — this sandbox has no Docker to run the dev server or Playwright against; the jsdom mount proves the extension set assembles and renders without crashing, not that positioning or drag-and-drop work in a real browser. (2) Every consumer TECHNICAL-PLAN §1 names beyond `bio` (mail, exports, comments, documents) — none exist yet, so `renderRichTextToHtml`/`excerptRichText`/the extraction functions are proven against synthetic fixtures only. (3) `codeBlock`'s allow-list deliberately excludes syntax highlighting — not asked for by S-30, flagged as a natural future addition rather than built speculatively. **Three further real defects found by actually running the dev preview in a browser, not the jsdom mount**: `packages/ui/src/rich-text/suggestion-list.tsx` and `attachment-view.tsx` were both missing `"use client";`, a class of bug the jsdom harness cannot catch since it never distinguishes server from client rendering. Separately, `/dev/rich-text` passed `searchMembers`/`searchEntities` as inline arrow functions, recreated on every render; since `onUpdate` fires on every keystroke and the editor's extension set is memoised on those props by identity, typing anything tore down and rebuilt the whole TipTap plugin set mid-query and broke the mention popup. Fixed by hoisting both to stable module-level functions and wrapping `validate` in `useCallback`. A third, subtler bug in the same file: `createSuggestionRender`'s `onStart` was `async`, awaiting a dynamic `import("@tiptap/react")` before creating the popup's `ReactRenderer`; a synchronous `items()` result (the slash menu's, unlike the mention search's async one) could arrive through `onUpdate` before that import resolved, and `renderer?.updateProps(...)` silently no-opped against a still-null renderer, so the slash menu always opened to "No matches" and never recovered. Fixed with a static `import { ReactRenderer } from "@tiptap/react";` and a synchronous `onStart`. Re-verified with Playwright after each fix: typed `@Ada`, got and selected a real mention; typed `/` on an empty block, got all seven commands |
| P2-T12 | Data-change runner | done | task/p2-t12-data-change-runner | 2026-08-10 | `packages/db/src/data-change.ts`: `runDataChanges` against a plain client, mirroring `migrate.ts`'s own shape (a `_data_changes` ledger table it creates itself, an advisory lock distinct from the migration runner's so the two never contend, name-ordered scripts) but batched and resumable rather than one statement per script, because a backfill can be too large for one transaction the way a schema change never is. A script declares `expects`: the columns and `information_schema.columns.data_type` values its SQL depends on, checked before every run, not once at review time — this is what "a later schema change cannot silently alter what an old script does" (the task's own acceptance line) means as code rather than a comment. The ledger records a cursor and commits after every batch, so a crash between batches loses no progress; a completed script is skipped on every later run, which is the acceptance criterion itself ("run twice... does nothing... the ledger shows one completion"), proven in a test that forces a mid-run crash and confirms the resume neither re-runs the already-committed batch nor restarts from zero. One design choice worth naming: a migration's checksum covers its literal SQL text, but a script here is TypeScript code, not a static string, so its checksum instead covers `{name, expects}` — the declared, frozen contract — and changing that contract on an in-progress (started, not completed) script is refused rather than silently accepted, matching the spirit of "immutable once it has run" without needing to hash a function body. The one sample change (`data-changes/0001_backfill_member_timezone.ts`) sets a member's `timezone` from their workspace's own settings wherever it is still null, batched by a keyset on `id` (this schema's ids are time-ordered, so ascending order is a cheap, stable resume point) — flagged plainly in its own header as a worked example rather than a real gap this codebase has today, since every member the Operation pipeline provisions already gets a timezone at insert time; the importer (P6-T01/T02) is the real future case it is written for. `pnpm db:change` (`packages/db/src/bin/data-change.ts`, wired into the root `package.json`) is the new command CLAUDE.md's own "commands that do not exist yet" table named for this task; moved it into the main command list and removed the stale row. The conventions document is `docs/design/data-change-conventions.md`. **A stray finding, not chased further because it is outside this task's scope:** while editing that same CLAUDE.md table, `pnpm gen:contract`'s row cites "P2-T09" as its arrival task, but P2-T09's actual IMPLEMENTATION-PLAN deliverables (security baseline: rate limiting, lockout, CSP, sessions, the freeze overlay, placeholder secrets) say nothing about contract generation, and `packages/core/src/actions/registry.ts`'s own header comment names "P5-T07 onwards" for exactly this generated-projections work instead — the same shape of citation defect already known and flagged for P2-T16 (`AI-NATIVE-PLAN.md §1.7`). Left as found, for a human to correct the same way. **Verified** under Node 22: typecheck (`packages/db`), Biome, the boundary gate, the migration linter and the soft-delete lint all pass; `packages/config`'s own suite re-ran clean. **Not verified:** `packages/db/test/data-change.test.ts` — no Docker/Postgres in this sandbox, same gap as every prior Phase 2 row; this one is unusually self-contained (a scratch database per test, like `migrate.test.ts` already does), so `pnpm db:up && pnpm --filter @openokr/db test` alone would be enough to close it, without needing the wider suite the way the operation.ts-touching rows do |
| P2-T13 | AIProvider port + drivers | done | task/p2-t13-ai-provider-drivers | 2026-08-10 | The inherited port-lifecycle follow-up was already closed in the cross-phase gap review just above this table, ahead of this task needing it — every port including `AIProvider` already declared `stop()` before the first real driver was written. The port itself (`chat`/`stream`/`chatWithTools`/`embed`/`extract`/`capabilities`) was already built at P1-T04 with only the `off` driver behind it; this task adds the six real ones AI-NATIVE-PLAN §3.2 names. Human approved three new dependencies after being asked precisely which ones, not just "AI SDKs generally": `@anthropic-ai/sdk` (native, for prompt caching and because its Messages API is not OpenAI-shaped), `openai` (drives four of the six drivers — `openai`, `openrouter`, `ollama` and the fully generic `openai-compatible` all speak the identical chat-completions API, just a different base URL and key, so one client and one implementation, `OpenAiCompatibleProvider`, covers all four as thin presets or direct use), and `@google/genai` (native, for Gemini's own tool-calling and structured-output fidelity). Two packages needed an explicit `allowBuilds` decision under pnpm's script-gating (`@google/genai` itself and its transitive `protobufjs`); checked what each package's flagged lifecycle script actually does before approving both — neither runs anything against a published install. Exact SDK call shapes (constructor options, request/response field names, streaming event shapes) were verified by reading the installed `.d.ts` files directly rather than trusted from training data, since all three packages are recent enough that memorised shapes risked being stale; this caught real differences from what would have been assumed, most importantly: OpenAI's `max_tokens` is deprecated in favour of `max_completion_tokens`; Anthropic's `MessageParam.role` now type-checks a `'system'` value for an unrelated beta feature (mid-conversation system blocks) that must not be confused with a general system message — the top-level `system` parameter is still the only real one; Google's client has no `fetch` injection point at all, only `httpOptions.baseUrl`, so `GoogleProviderOptions.client` exists as this driver's own seam for a pre-built stand-in instead; and Gemini's own embedding response carries no token-usage field of any kind. Every driver ships its own `..._DEFAULT_TIER_MODELS` seed (AI-NATIVE-PLAN §3.4: "a driver added without a default tier map is incomplete"), read through `defaultTierModelsFor` — free-text seeds, not a catalogue, meant to be overridden the moment P2-T15 exists. `createAIProvider` (`packages/adapters/src/create-ai-provider.ts`) is the one exported entry point; every driver class itself stays private to this package, matching how `createMailer` already works and keeping "adding a provider is a new driver behind the same port, never a change to feature code" true of the export surface, not just the port's own shape. Not wired into `createAdapters()`: which provider and key apply is resolved by configuration that changes without a restart, which is P2-T14's precedence resolver (user key, then workspace, then deployment, then off), not something decided once at process start. The driver contract is written up at `docs/design/ai-provider-driver-contract.md`. **Verified** under Node 22: typecheck and Biome on every new file, the boundary gate (185 files). The licence gate's own wrapper script could not run in this sandbox (`spawn pnpm ENOENT` — the same PATH quirk documented all session, not a new one), so it was run manually instead: `pnpm licenses list --json --prod` against the full installed tree, diffed by hand against `scripts/check-licences.ts`'s own allow list — every licence across all three new packages and their transitive dependencies (including `protobufjs`, BSD-3-Clause) is already on it. **Not verified:** `packages/adapters/test/ai-{openai-compatible,anthropic,google,mock}.test.ts` and `create-ai-provider.test.ts` — no Docker/Postgres in this sandbox, same gap as every prior Phase 2 row touching this package; the streaming tests for the OpenAI-shaped and Anthropic drivers carry the most risk of the new files, since they depend on a hand-authored SSE fixture matching each vendor's real event framing exactly and could not be run-and-corrected here the way the non-streaming ones' simpler JSON fixtures could be reasoned through with higher confidence — read those two `describe("stream", ...)` blocks first when running the suite |
| P2-T14 | AI configuration, keys, encryption and rotation | done | task/p2-t14-ai-configuration-keys | 2026-08-10 | Two tables, migration 0015: `ai_providers` (a workspace admin's on/off switch per provider, plus whether members may add a personal key for it) and `ai_credentials` (the sealed key material, `owner_member_id` null for the workspace's own key or set for one member's personal key, one of each per provider enforced by two partial unique indexes). Both RLS-scoped to the workspace, `force row level security`, no exception. **Envelope encryption is reused, not reimplemented**: `packages/core/src/secrets/key-ring.ts`'s existing `encryptSecret`/`decryptSecret`/`rewrapSecret` (built at P1-T09 for mail, generalised at the cross-phase gap review for `instance_audit_events`) now also seals AI credentials, so a data key per credential wrapped by the same root key ring, the same rotation mechanics. `maskKeyHint` (`packages/core/src/ai/credentials.ts`) is the one new primitive: last four characters visible, the rest replaced. **The precedence resolver** (`packages/core/src/ai/resolve.ts`, `resolveAICredential`) implements AI-NATIVE-PLAN §3.3 exactly — user key, then workspace, then deployment, then off — for one already-known provider (which tier maps to which provider is P2-T15's own job). Deliberately a plain export, not a registered action, matching `resolveOwnWorkspaceAccessLevel`'s own precedent: a decrypted key must never reach a surface the action registry projects onto REST/MCP/CLI/chat, only a host-side caller already trusted to make the real provider call (`apps/web`, or the P2-T17 agent runtime later). Returns plain data rather than a configured `AIProvider`, because `packages/core` may not depend on `packages/adapters` (TECHNICAL-PLAN §1) — the host takes this result and calls `createAIProvider` itself, the same reasoning `resolveMailSettings` already established. The deployment level reuses the exact instance-settings machinery mail already has (three new registry entries, `ai.deployment.{provider,apiKey,baseUrl}`, env-bootstrapped via `OPENOKR_AI_{PROVIDER,API_KEY,BASE_URL}`) — one single-provider fallback for the whole instance, matching §4.14's own line naming "the deployment-level AI key" under the Instance scope, not a per-provider list. **Seven actions** in `packages/core/src/actions/ai.ts`: `updateProviderConfig`/`setWorkspaceCredential`/`removeWorkspaceCredential` at `full` (admin), `setPersonalCredential`/`removePersonalCredential` at `edit` (self-service, additionally gated on the provider's own `allowUserKeys`), `readProviderConfig` (admin, masked hints only) and `readOwnCredentialStatus` (self, `view`). `ActionCallContext` (`actions/define.ts`) gained an optional `ring?: KeyRing` field — the first thing any action has needed a key ring for; every other action's context is unaffected. **Rotation is deliberately per-workspace, not a second instance-wide sweep**: `ai_credentials` is ordinary workspace data with `FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` and no `app.instance_admin`-style exception, unlike `system_settings`/`instance_audit_events` which sit above the tenant floor by design — extending the instance-admin bypass to reach workspace data would be exactly the "row-level security is the tenant floor, not replaced" hard rule this task should not quietly cross for its own convenience. `ai.rotateCredentials` (full access) re-wraps one workspace's own rows inside that workspace's own transaction instead, "one command" from a workspace admin's own seat. `pnpm keys:rotate` is unchanged and still covers instance secrets only. **No live connection test built**: AI-NATIVE-PLAN §3.3 asks for one, but making a real vendor network call cannot run inside the Operation pipeline (`packages/core` cannot reach `packages/adapters`, and a write path may not call a driver directly per CLAUDE.md's own hard rule) — it belongs in the host layer (`apps/web`, calling `resolveAICredential` then `createAIProvider` then a cheap real call, before writing the resulting status back through a future thin write action), which has no real caller yet since no admin console screen exists before P2-T16's own S-37. Flagged as a follow-up for whichever task builds that screen, not built speculatively against nothing. **Verified**, for the first time against a real database rather than typechecking alone (this machine's own native Postgres, no Docker): `pnpm typecheck`, Biome on every new/touched file, the boundary gate (236 files), the migration linter (17 files) and the soft-delete lint (15 tables, 149 files) all pass. `packages/core`'s full suite, 380/380 (10 new: mask-hint formatting, off/workspace/user/deployment resolution in isolation, the acceptance criterion itself — a workspace key and a personal key, the member's own key wins for them and only them — a provider that disallows personal keys refusing one, the deployment fallback only firing for its own named provider, an admin's read of the provider config never containing a workspace or personal key as a raw string anywhere in the response, and rotation re-wrapping a real credential then leaving it decryptable both before, during and after, with a second pass re-wrapping nothing). A real bug caught before it shipped: `updateProviderConfig`'s first draft used `onConflictDoUpdate` against `ai_providers_workspace_provider_idx`, a *partial* unique index (`where deleted_at is null`) — Postgres refuses to infer a partial index as an `ON CONFLICT` arbiter without repeating its own predicate, so every upsert failed outright the first time this ran against a real database; rewritten as an explicit branch on the row `load` already fetched, avoiding the partial-index arbiter question entirely. **Not verified:** no UI exists yet (the AI console is S-37, P2-T16's own card), so every action here is proven against `callAction` directly in tests, not through a running screen |
| P2-T15 | Model catalogue, tier routing, structured output and prompts | done | task/p2-t15-model-catalogue-tier-routing | 2026-08-10 | Four workspace-scoped tables, migration 0016: `ai_models` (custom entries only — see below), `ai_model_policies` (one tier-to-model mapping per workspace per tier, an override), `ai_feature_settings` (a switch and an optional tier override per §2 capability; quota is P2-T16's own column, not reserved speculatively here), `ai_prompts` (append-only versions, never an update to one already written, so "reversible" means the old text actually still exists). **The seeded model catalogue is code, not a table** (`packages/core/src/ai/model-catalog.ts`, `SEEDED_MODELS`), the same "registry plus database overrides" shape `INSTANCE_SETTINGS` already uses — seeding a `FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` table with rows belonging to no workspace has no clean owner to write them as, the identical reasoning P2-T14 already used for the deployment AI key. `ai_models` therefore holds only what an admin adds themselves, real workspace data with no such ambiguity; a workspace's effective catalogue (`ai.readModelCatalog`) is the seeded list plus its own custom rows. Cost and context-window figures in the seed are a working default an admin is expected to confirm or refresh (§3.4's own wording), not a pricing guarantee. Likewise `ai_prompts`' built-in default text is code (`packages/core/src/ai/prompts.ts`), seeded for two representative §2 capabilities today (`draft.objective`, `rewrite.failing_rule`) rather than the whole catalogue — neither has a real caller yet, the same scope P2-T13/P2-T14 already carry, and a default prompt for a capability nothing calls would be invented rather than built. **Tier routing** (`packages/core/src/ai/tier-routing.ts`): `resolveTierRoute` checks `ai_model_policies` first, then falls back to the seeded catalogue's own default for whichever provider this workspace has enabled (`ai_providers`, P2-T14) — "a workspace that has supplied only a key resolves every tier through the driver's seeded map" holds with zero policy rows written. `resolveFeatureTier` layers a feature's own admin-set override (or lack of one) on top of whatever tier the feature's own code asks for by default, and a missing settings row means enabled (§4: "on by default where a provider is configured"), never disabled by omission. **The context-window guard** (`packages/core/src/ai/context-guard.ts`, `guardContextWindow`) is a pure function reserving a fraction of the window for the reply itself by default, not just checking the raw token count against the limit — a request that exactly fills the window leaves no room for a model to answer at all. **Structured extraction with one repair attempt** (`packages/agents/src/structured-extraction.ts`, `extractStructured`) lives in the new `packages/agents` package, not `packages/core`: it calls a real `AIProvider`, and `packages/core` may not depend on `packages/adapters` (TECHNICAL-PLAN §1) — `packages/agents` is the one package meant to hold logic spanning both, and this is its first real content, ahead of the Coach and Champion runtimes that will call it (P2-T17). One repair call carries the first (invalid) reply back to the model with a correction instruction; a second failure throws a typed `StructuredExtractionError` carrying both raw attempts rather than returning anything Zod rejected. Fourteen actions across `packages/core/src/actions/ai.ts` (unchanged from P2-T14) and the new `actions/ai-models.ts`, all `full`-access admin governance, matching every other S-36/S-37 card — routing and prompts are not self-service the way a personal AI key is. **Verified**, against a real database: `pnpm typecheck`, Biome on every new/touched file, the boundary gate (243 files), the migration linter (18 files) and the soft-delete lint (19 tables, 155 files) all pass. `packages/core`'s full suite, 392/392 (12 new: the context guard blocking an oversized request and allowing a normal one; the seeded catalogue actually covering fast/balanced/deep per provider except anthropic's own documented embed gap; `readModelCatalog` combining seeded and custom without duplicating the seeded list; a custom model metering cost from its own figures; tier routing resolving through the seeded map with only a key supplied, an explicit policy overriding it, and reverting cleanly; a feature's tier override affecting only that feature; a disabled feature reporting disabled regardless of tier; the prompt registry serving the built-in default until a version exists, recording a change as a new version with the old text still readable, and restoring cleanly). `packages/agents`'s suite ran for the first time with real content, 5/5 (a first-attempt success never triggering a repair call; a repair succeeding on malformed JSON and on the wrong shape; a second failure throwing `StructuredExtractionError` rather than returning anything). **Not verified:** no UI exists yet (S-37 is P2-T16's own card); every action here is proven against `callAction` directly. The live provider call inside `extractStructured` is proven against a queued stub, not a real vendor — the same "mechanism proven, no live vendor smoke test" gap P2-T13's own streaming tests already carry |
| P2-T16 | Usage metering, quotas and hard caps | done | task/p2-t16-usage-metering-hard-caps | 2026-08-10 | The blocking doc defect (IMPLEMENTATION-PLAN.md's stale `AI-NATIVE-PLAN.md §1.7` citation, corrected to §4) was closed at the cross-phase gap review, ahead of this task needing it. Two workspace-scoped tables, migration 0017: `ai_usage_events` (per call: member or agent, feature, source, provider, model, tokens, cost, latency, status, flagged) and `ai_budgets` (one row per workspace/scope/scope-ref/metric/period). `ai_usage_events` carries no `deleted_at` — `openokr:hard-delete`, since a usage event is a fact about a moment metered against a real cost, and a hard cap computed from a partially-hidden total would be the wrong number. `agent_id` on both new tables carries no foreign key yet: `agents` is P2-T17's own deliverable, and the column exists now so a usage event or budget recorded before that table lands needs no later migration to reference it. **`recordUsageEvent` is a plain function, not a registered action** (`packages/core/src/ai/usage.ts`) — the reason is trust, not access level: the tokens, cost and latency it writes are facts about a call that already happened, known only to whichever host-side code actually made that real call. Exposing this through the action registry would let any caller self-report a number, understating their own usage or inflating someone else's toward a shared budget. It still runs through `runOperation` (every write does; the boundary gate enforces it), with `bootstrap: true` doing the same job it does for `invitations.acceptLink`: there is no resource-level access question to ask, so the function existing at all, reachable only from trusted server code, is the whole of what authorises it. Anomaly flagging is a deterministic heuristic against the same table: a call flags when its cost exceeds 5x the trailing average of the last 20 calls for that feature, with at least 5 prior calls to have a baseline — a workspace's first few calls are never anomalous relative to nothing. **Budgets and the hard cap** (`packages/core/src/ai/budgets.ts`): `checkBudget` sums usage since the current UTC day or month's start and compares to a configured limit, `configured: false` (not an error) when nothing is set, matching "nothing must be configured before the product works." `checkFeatureAvailability` layers P2-T15's own feature switch, then the workspace's own budget, then the calling member's or agent's own, returning the first refusal with a clear message — a feature switched off never even reaches a budget check. **The workspace's own scope doubles as the hard cap** the task card names, rather than a second column: crossing it is what disables every feature and (once P2-T17 exists) what its agent loop checks between tool calls to halt a run mid-flight; crossing a `user`- or `agent`-scope budget is a lighter, narrower consequence, not a second hard cap. `isOverHardCap` is the one function P2-T17 will call for that halt check. Four admin actions (`packages/core/src/actions/ai-usage.ts`, all `full`): `readBudgets`, `setBudget`, `removeBudget`, `readUsageSummary`. **The AI console itself (screen S-37) is not built**, the same scope boundary P2-T13/T14/T15 already carry for every UI they enable: no design gate has reviewed a 7-card admin screen (provider, models, features, budgets, prompts, privacy, usage), and building one without that review would be significant unreviewed UI in a task that is fundamentally about data and enforcement logic. Flagged as a follow-up needing its own design pass, not built speculatively. **Verified** against a real database: `pnpm typecheck`, Biome on every new/touched file, the boundary gate (247 files), the migration linter (19 files) and the soft-delete lint (20 tables, 158 files) all pass. `packages/core`'s full suite, 403/403 (11 new: usage recorded and summed accurately across calls; a real cost spike flagged against its own feature's baseline, a workspace's first few calls never flagged with no baseline yet; a budget reporting unconfigured-and-within-limit with nothing set, then crossing once usage exceeds it; a feature disabled with a clear message once its workspace budget is crossed, staying available with nothing configured, and refused by its own switch before any budget check runs at all; the hard cap reporting over only once the workspace's own configured metric is actually crossed; the admin actions listing and removing a budget). One caught during writing, not after: the marker comment for `ai_usage_events`' hard-delete exception has to sit on the line immediately above `create table`, not merely nearby — the same placement rule the migration linter's own header already documents, re-learned by tripping it |
| P2-T17 | Agent runtime: agents, runs, sandbox, proposals | done | task/p2-t17-agent-runtime | 2026-08-10 | Three tables, migration 0018: `agents` (one row per agent, each owning its own `workspace_members` row with `kind = 'agent'` — least-privilege bindings need no new mechanism, they are ordinary `access_bindings` rows on that member's own `access_groups` entry, and the cross-phase gap review already excludes the blanket `workspace_standard`/`space_standard` tiers for agent-kind members, so an agent reaches nothing until `agents.bindScope` names a resource), `agent_runs` and `proposed_changes` (both `openokr:hard-delete` — a run's log and a proposal's decision are facts about what happened, the same "fact about a moment, nothing to soft-delete-recover" reasoning `ai_usage_events` already established). Ten actions in `packages/core/src/actions/agents.ts`, all `full`-access admin governance: `agents.list/create/setEnabled/bindScope/startRun/readRun/cancelRun`, `proposals.list/bulkApply/bulkDismiss`. `agents.startRun` accepts an already-decomposed task list rather than calling a real model to plan one — real planning is `extractStructured` (P2-T15) against the agent's own `planningInstructions`, still with no live feature caller, the same scope every AI task since P2-T13 carries. **The run state machine itself lives in the new `packages/agents/src/run-executor.ts`**, not `packages/core`, per TECHNICAL-PLAN §1's own package table ("run state machines" belong there); `processNextTask` handles exactly one task per call and returns rather than looping, so a run resumes correctly from persisted state alone — proven by a test that calls it twice, independently, with nothing carried over in memory between calls. Every dispatch mode shares one pre-check first: the agent's own binding on the task's named target context (`resolveSubjectContext` + `resolveMemberAccessLevel`, the same not-found-shaped refusal `getAccessScoped` uses), logged as `denied` and never attempted if it fails — sandbox included, since simulating honestly means simulating what would actually be denied. Past that check, `sandbox` simulates and writes nothing; `propose` inserts one `proposed_changes` row and calls nothing real; `scoped_direct` calls the real action through `callAction`, deliberately outside the run's own transaction (the same reasoning `proposedChanges.bulkApply` — now `proposals.bulkApply` — already established: a dispatched action is a real, independent write with its own audit and outbox rows, and its failure must not roll back the run's own progress past that task, only log it as `error`). `isOverHardCap` (P2-T16) is checked before touching a task at all; crossing it halts and fails the run with the reason recorded on the row, not silently. When tasks remain, the run self-reschedules by inserting one outbox row (topic `agents.run.continue`), never a direct `JobQueue.enqueue()` call — the boundary gate enforces this, and it caught a false-positive against this same rule firing on a doc-comment's own prose, reworded to stop naming the method literally. No relay consumer for that topic exists yet in `apps/web`, the same "mechanism proven, no live caller" scope every AI task since P2-T13 carries, so a run only actually advances when a call (today, a test; later, a worker) invokes `processNextTask` again. **Two real bugs caught before any test ran**, by re-reading the code rather than by a failure: `proposedChanges.bulkApply`/`bulkDismiss`'s final status update was scoped only to `workspaceId` and `status = 'pending'`, which would have marked *every* pending proposal in the workspace decided, not just the ids the caller actually named — fixed by adding `inArray(proposedChanges.id, ...)` to both. **One caught by the registry's own convention test**: `proposedChanges.list/bulkApply/bulkDismiss` failed `registry.test.ts`'s `domain.verb` naming check, because the domain segment must be all-lowercase and `proposedChanges` is not — renamed to `proposals.*` throughout (the exported function names and the `proposed_changes` table itself are unchanged; only the three action-registry strings moved). `agent_id` on `ai_usage_events`/`ai_budgets` (P2-T16) still carries no foreign key to `agents` — flagged then as a gap this migration could close and deliberately left open here too, since retrofitting it is a schema change with no behavioural need driving it yet. **Not built:** the run history screen (S-38), the same UI-deferral precedent S-37 already carries; the outbox relay's own consumer wiring for `agents.run.continue` in `apps/web`; a real planning call from `agents.startRun` into `extractStructured`. **Verified** against a real database: `pnpm typecheck`, Biome on every new/touched file, the boundary gate (251 files), the migration linter (18 files) and the soft-delete lint (21 tables, 161 files) all pass. `packages/core`'s full suite, 412/412 (9 new). `packages/agents` gained its first database-backed suite (its own `vitest.config.ts` and a `@openokr/test-support` dev dependency, mirroring `packages/core`'s own harness rather than needing the alias workaround `packages/core` uses, since `packages/agents` already depends on `packages/core` and test-support depending on core too is not a cycle): 12/12 (7 new — sandbox commits nothing; propose commits nothing until `proposals.bulkApply` runs, with a real audit row as proof; `scoped_direct` calls the real action immediately within its bindings, and logs `error` without stopping the run when the dispatched action itself refuses; a task outside the agent's bindings is denied and never attempted; a two-task run resumes correctly across two independent calls, with the outbox row visible after the first and gone after the run completes; a non-running run refuses `processNextTask` outright). `packages/db`'s suite, 97/101, unchanged from the baseline the cross-phase gap review already established — the four failures are the pre-existing PgBouncer-not-running gap (no Docker in this environment), not a regression. Migration 0018 applied to the dev database. Carries forward the standing P1 hardening follow-up this row previously held on its own: `PgBossJobQueue.stop()` clears `#running` but not `#started`, so a `start()` after a `stop()` skips `createQueue` for every queue it has already seen and then sends into queues pg-boss no longer knows about — the first component likely to cycle the queue inside one process |

## Phase 2 exit, and a change of branch policy

**2026-08-11.** The Phase 2 exit checklist is
`docs/design/p2-exit-checklist.md`, written before P3-T00 as EXECUTION-GUIDE.md
§4 requires. All fifteen criteria on IMPLEMENTATION-PLAN.md's Phase 2 exit line
are met. The checklist records **how** each one was verified, because that varies
far more than the verdicts do: three were exercised against a real database, one
in a real browser, nine by a database-less suite, and two (real mail delivery,
real AI provider requests) by nothing at all.

Two findings came from running the gates rather than reading the code, which is
the pattern this repository keeps repeating:

1. **`pnpm check:licences` had never executed on Windows.** `execFile` cannot
   resolve `pnpm.cmd`, and Node 22 refuses to spawn it directly under the
   `EINVAL` guard from CVE-2024-27980. Green in CI, broken on every Windows
   machine. Fixed with `shell: true` on win32 only.
2. **`pnpm lint` cannot be read on a Windows checkout.** All 232 diagnostics are
   CRLF-against-LF formatter noise across 20 files, zero rule violations, caused
   by `core.autocrlf = true` with no `.gitattributes`. **Answered the same day:**
   `.gitattributes` added with `* text=auto eol=lf`. Lint went to zero and
   immediately surfaced two real findings the noise had been hiding.
3. **A third instance of the same Windows defect, found 2026-08-12:**
   `packages/test-support/test/flaky-reporter.test.ts` spawned `pnpm` the same
   way `check-licences.ts` did, so the flakiness reporter's own suite had never
   run on Windows either. Same fix. Worth assuming there are more: the pattern to
   look for is `execFile`/`spawn` with a package-manager name and no
   `shell: true`.

**The `.gitattributes` change had a consequence worth recording, found on
2026-08-12.** The migration runner hashed raw file bytes, so flipping every
migration in the working tree from CRLF to LF made it refuse to run at all:
"Applied migration 0001_outbox.sql was edited after it ran", against files
nobody had touched. Locally that is an annoyance. Outside this repository it is
an upgrade-breaking bug: an instance deployed from a Windows checkout records
CRLF checksums, and its next upgrade from an image built on Linux would refuse
to migrate. `packages/db/src/migrate.ts` now normalises line endings and trailing
newlines before hashing, so the checksum describes the SQL rather than its
encoding, which is what the gate always meant. A ledger row written by the old
runner is still recognised, **only** when the file's normalised content is
unchanged, and is then rewritten once so each database heals itself. Five new
cases in `migrate.test.ts` cover it, including the one that matters: a real edit
that also changed the line endings is still refused.

**Branch policy changed, at the human's instruction (2026-08-11).** The
one-branch-per-task stack is abandoned: twenty-two branches became unmanageable.
Every Phase 2 commit now sits on the single branch **`agung`**, cut from
`phase2-part2`, and every Phase 3 task adds **one commit** to that same branch.
The audit trail moves from the branch name to the commit message, which still
carries the task identifier. `main` still ends at Phase 1 and no Phase 2 work
has been reviewed or merged by anyone. That review debt is the largest risk
crossing into Phase 3 and is recorded in full in the exit checklist.

## The reference mockups, and when each one arrives

**Asked and answered on 2026-08-12.** "Make the UI match the mockups" was put
directly, and the answer is that it cannot be done ahead of the tasks: all eleven
mockups in `docs/stakeholder/mockups/png/` map to screens that do not exist yet,
and UIUX-PLAN.md §10's own table names the owning task for each. A Work Map row
needs goals, key results, initiatives and KPIs, which arrive at P3-T04, P5-T01
and P3-T12.

**The decision: follow the plan order.** Each mockup is opened at the start of
its own task, as CLAUDE.md already requires, and the screen is built when the
model behind it exists. Building the eleven now against mock data was considered
and rejected: it would be torn out again when the real task ran.

| Mockup | Arrives with |
|---|---|
| 02-cycle-workspace | P3-T03 |
| 10-review-inbox | P3-T08 |
| 05-alignment-studio | P3-T10, with P3-T09's findings |
| 01-work-map | P3-T11 |
| 06-kpi-recovery | P3-T14 |
| 04-gates-capacity | P4-T03 |
| 03-draft-coach, 03b-rule-card | P4-T02 |
| 07-weekly-session | P4-T07 |
| 08-quarterly-review | P4-T10 |
| 09-channels | P5-T02 to P5-T05 |

Five of the eleven are satisfied by the end of Phase 3. The rest belong to
Phase 4 and Phase 5.

**Three shell gaps found while comparing, each recorded against its owner
rather than fixed on the spot.** They are gaps against UIUX-PLAN.md §3, the
specification, not against the picture:

| Gap | Owner | Why not now |
|---|---|---|
| The sidebar lists one `Spaces` link; §3 asks for the spaces themselves nested under it | P3-T11 | Spaces exist, so this one is buildable today. Deferred because P3-T11 replaces the `/` route and reworks the primary nav anyway, and touching the shell twice for it is worse than once |
| §3 shows three separated nav blocks; the module registry (P2-T08) has `section` but no grouping, so the structure cannot be expressed | P3-T11 | Same reason. It is a registry change plus a shell change, and P3-T11 is where the nav is next opened |
| The topbar shows the workspace name; §3 asks for a breadcrumb | P3-T11 | Same |

The cycle strip is **already** on P3-T03's card ("the cycle strip in the shell").
Its component exists in `packages/ui` from P2-T10 and its data exists from
P3-T02, so only the wiring is left, and "what is blocking it" needs P3-T03's own
gate state.

Every function in it is a METHOD.md §3 rule taking a §11 threshold as an argument, and the repository rule puts those in the method package and nowhere else; the same code has to run in the browser as somebody types, on the server before a write and inside the agents. `packages/core/src/scoring/recompute.ts` is the half that needs rows. **The golden masters are read out of the design document at run time**, not retyped beside the test. `packages/method/test/scoring.test.ts` loads all ten matrices through the P3-T02 golden-table helper, so changing a number in `docs/design/p3-t00-scoring-and-health-engine.md` changes what the suite asserts and deleting a table breaks the build. **Three defects the golden masters caught, each fixed in the engine rather than in the test.** The weighted average was trusting its input, so a row carrying weight 150 dominated a company figure; weights are now clamped inside the average as well as on write, because the imported row is exactly the one the write path never saw. The cycle-breaking pass was quadratic and the 1,000-goal budget measured it at 374 ms against decision D-13's 200 ms ceiling; it is a single three-colour walk now, and the second pass became iterative because a thousand-deep chain of recursive frames is somebody's outage. In-transaction is also the stronger guarantee: there is no window where a page shows a number the rows no longer support. The same function is what the job will call when a host exists. | P3-T06 | Cadence engine + staleness | done | agung | 2026-08-12 | **Every step of the arithmetic runs on the calendar, and the conversion to an instant happens once, at the end.** That is the whole reason this task exists as its own engine: two Berlin Mondays a week apart are 167 hours apart in absolute terms, not 168, and arithmetic done on instants would have produced a Sunday deadline for half the year. Twelve golden-master instants cross both European and North American transitions in 2026, a zone that never shifts, and both ends of the offset range. **The stored instant is the local end of the due date**, `23:59:59.999` in the workspace timezone, so a check-in posted at any hour of the due date is on time, which is what a human means by "due Monday". It also avoids the local midnight that does not exist on some transition days. Month clamping is not sticky: `advance` recomputes from the anchor every month, so anchor 31 reads 31 Jan, 28 Feb, 31 Mar rather than silently rewriting itself to 28. Weeks step by seven days rather than searching for the next anchor weekday, because the weekday is already baked into the first due date and addition cannot drift. After a publication, two rules in order: advance one period from the **due date**, never from the publication date, then keep advancing while the result is on or before it. The first is why a Tuesday check-in does not move a Monday rhythm; the second is why a champion who vanishes for a month does not return to a backlog of four overdue periods. The tolerance changes no dates at all: it only decides whether the period that just ended was met or missed. **Two corrections to the design document, made in the same change.** The escalation ladder went to `packages/method/src/escalation.ts` rather than into the core engine, because which roles it widens to is §11 practice; the date arithmetic stayed in core where the local-date primitives already were. And the §5 sweep is a command, `pnpm cadence:sweep`, not a job: no scheduler host runs in the application yet. It goes through the Operation pipeline per workspace, so a health flip nobody triggered is still audited. Four of §8's seven reset events are wired: creation stamps the first due date, a frequency change restarts from today so it can never make a goal instantly overdue, a close clears the date because a closed goal is never due, and a reopen restarts it. Publication and deleting the latest check-in are the other two and need check-ins (P3-T07). Moving a goal between cycles deliberately changes nothing, and changing the workspace default rewrites nothing, because one setting change silently moving thousands of deadlines is not a setting change anybody would expect. **The scoring recompute's staleness became timezone-aware in the same change.** It was dividing milliseconds, which is wrong for a goal due at 23:59 local; it now counts local days through the cadence service. **Verified: `packages/core` 626/626** against a real database (74 of them cadence golden-master rows read out of the design document, plus five that drive the four reset events and the sweep through real rows) **and `packages/method` 234/234.** The command was also run against the development database: it examined one neglected goal, flipped it to `outdated`, wrote the audit row, and a second run flipped nothing. Every static gate clean, including a boundary violation it caught first: the command enumerated workspaces outside the access getter, which is now marked with the reason a maintenance command has no acting member to scope by. The goal page shows when the next check-in is due and how many days overdue it is, read in the workspace calendar rather than the reader's. **Still to do here:** `nextAfterPublication` is written, golden-master tested and unreachable until check-ins exist at P3-T07, which is the task that calls it. The nudges the ladder feeds are P4-T05; this computes the step and the targets with no channel, no queue and no clock. |
| P3-T07 | Check-ins: snapshots, publication, acknowledgement, voting | done | agung | 2026-08-12 | Migration 0023 adds `check_ins`, `check_in_snapshots` and `check_in_votes`, and closes the three columns earlier tasks left pointing at nothing: `key_result_values.check_in_id`, `goals.last_check_in_id` and the votes' own key result. **The snapshot has its own table because §6.2 calls it immutable.** One column cannot hold a history, and an edit inside the window has to leave the old snapshot intact: the difference a reviewer already read must not change under them. TECHNICAL-PLAN §4.4 and the §7.2 mapping are updated in the same change. **A draft is completely silent** and the test proves it against rows rather than intent: no value history, no cadence movement, no health change, no notification. An unchanged value writes no history row, because a sparkline should show movement rather than the heartbeat of somebody opening a form. **Three gate findings, all fixed rather than argued with.** The registry naming test caught `checkIns.*` breaking `domain.verb` for the same reason `keyResults.*` did at P3-T04, so the nine actions moved under the `goals` domain, which is also where their authorisation resolves. The registry's "a write needs edit" rule caught `acknowledge` asking for comment. And the soft-delete lint caught two reads written with `and(eq, isNull)` instead of `activeOnly`. **A decision that widens a role, recorded rather than settled:** the reviewer now holds `edit` on the goal they review rather than `comment`. Acknowledging is a write, §14 requires edit for every write, and a reviewer who could only comment could not do the one thing the role exists for. The side effect is that a reviewer can also edit the goal, which is more than the role needs. The alternative was an action that writes on view-level terms, and that rule exists to stop exactly that. **Verified: `packages/core` 640/640** against a real database, 14 of them new and covering every line of the task's test plan: the silent draft, publication with its snapshot and its obligation, the edit window refusing a finished period, the re-snapshot keeping both, deletion rolling the value and the pointer and the health back, an administrator refused where the reviewer is not, idempotent acknowledgement, and votes hidden until a reveal that is one write. Every static gate clean. **The surfaces landed after the domain, at `/check-in`.** Without a goal in the query string it is the session walker S-15 asks for: every goal the reader champions that is due or within two days, soonest first, with the days overdue and whether a draft is already open. With one it is the composer: status, a confidence dial in tenths because the §3.2 bands are read at 0.3, 0.4 and 0.7, a required narrative, and every key result pre-filled with the value it holds so the difference is visible before anything is typed. `goals.due` is the read behind it, and it offers a goal only to the member who champions it. METHOD.md §2.5 puts the check-in on the champion, so offering it to a reviewer or an administrator would be asking the wrong person however much access they hold. **A gap the browser found, not the tests.** Publishing advances the cadence, so the goal immediately leaves the due list, and the walker dropped the reader onto an empty page without ever showing the card they had just written. A goal named in the query string now stays on screen after it leaves the list, says it is no longer due, names its next date, and offers the next goal in the walker. The draft is also only opened for a goal that is actually due now, so reading the page of a goal already reported on no longer leaves an empty draft behind. The check-in card reads its differences from the snapshot rather than from the key results as they stand, which is the whole reason a snapshot is stored: a card read three weeks later shows what changed then. It states `awaiting the reviewer` as a first-class state (REQUIREMENTS §51), and carries the edit form for a check-in still inside its window, because a window nobody can use is a promise with no button. **The ✨ narrative draft S-15 asks for is deliberately absent.** It needs the provider, the prompt registry and the usage accounting, all Phase 4, and the rule is that every AI affordance is hidden when the provider is off. **Verified: `packages/core` 772/772** against a real database and **the end-to-end suite 21/21** from a clean build. The whole loop was also driven by hand in a browser: the walker listed one due goal, the composer published `caution` moving a key result from 41 to 55, the card rendered `41 → 55 (+14)` from the snapshot, the cadence advanced to the following week, and the walker correctly emptied. Every static gate clean. **The publish path is not driven from the end-to-end suite, on purpose.** Making a goal due needs the database, which that suite has no access to by design, and a goal created today with a Monday anchor is due next Monday, so the assertion would pass or fail depending on the day it ran. The core suite covers it against real rows instead; the browser tests cover what only a browser can settle, which is that the walker reports honestly and a goal reached directly shows its own history. **Still to do:** the reactions and comments the card carries, which need the comment tables from P3-T16. The realtime half of "the reveal is atomic for every connected client" needs the realtime port, which no host runs yet; the write is already one statement, so what is missing is the push rather than the atomicity. |

Storing the reviewer per check-in satisfies both, because the two rules act on different rows: open ones move, acknowledged ones keep whoever closed them, drafts stay null. The design document is corrected in the same change with the table of the three cases. `goals.reviewer_id` still answers "who reviews this goal" and no longer answers "who owes this acknowledgement". **`goals.acknowledgeCheckIn` now reads the check-in rather than the goal**, which also let it stop touching the `goals` table at all. Blockers, commitments, sessions and agent proposals return as named rows carrying the task that fills them (P3-T09, P4-T07, P4-T04, P4-T05) and the screen lists them, because a page showing two of six sources without saying so would look complete while failing to mention a blocker somebody owns. A test asserts all four are present. **No new threshold was invented for ageing an acknowledgement.** It is owed from publication, so publication is its due date; whether it reads as late comes from `cadence.acknowledgementLadderDays.escalate`, the §11 parameter whose own reason is "a check-in nobody acknowledged is a loop left open". **The badge is the same read as the list**, so the two cannot drift, and it counts overdue plus due today rather than everything: a badge that includes next week never reaches zero, and a badge that never reaches zero stops being read. Zero draws nothing. **Verified: `packages/core` 786/786** against a real database (14 new, covering every line of the task's test plan plus §4.4's Given / When / Then), `packages/ui` 141/141 (3 new on the badge), `apps/web` 16/16, **and the end-to-end suite 25/25 from a clean build** (2 new). `packages/db` 101/105, the four failures the pre-existing PgBouncer suite that needs Docker. Every static gate clean. Migration 0024 and data change 0005 both ran against the dev database, and the whole loop was driven by hand at `openokr.test`: the inbox listed one acknowledgement due today and one check-in due this week, Acknowledge removed the row, the Today count went 1 to 0 and the badge disappeared. **Three findings the browser produced that no gate did.** The badge was invisible between 1024 and 1279 pixels, where the rail collapses to icons and `hidden xl:inline-flex` took the chip with the label, so a badge failed exactly the laptop widths it is for: there is a dot on the icon below `xl` now, and an `sr-only` count at every width. P3-T07 left `<span className="sr-only">Reviewer of record: {uuid}</span>` in the check-in timeline as an explicit placeholder "before P3-T08 builds the inbox that lists it"; that inbox exists, so it is gone rather than reworded, and reading a bare UUID to somebody on a screen reader was never useful. And the end-to-end suite caught a locator collision: the sidebar item and the row action are both called "Check in", both correctly, so the specs scope to `main`. **DATABASE.md was stale from P3-T07** and is fixed here too: it still described `snapshot jsonb` on `check_ins` and had no `check_in_snapshots` section, three tasks after the table shipped. **Not built, each with its reason:** "Why you got nudged" needs a nudge to be about (P4-T05) and "Your week" needs the blocker clock and the session streak (P3-T09, P4-T04), so neither is drawn rather than drawn with invented numbers. "Live" means recomputed on navigation and after publication, acknowledgement and deletion, not pushed: the realtime port still has no host, the same gap P3-T07 recorded for the vote reveal |

TECHNICAL-PLAN §4.5, DATABASE.md and the §7.2 importer mapping are all updated in the same change; the three new tables are marked as having no legacy source, and `alignment_findings` is marked never-imported because it is derived and the first recompute after an import would replace anything written there. **Finding identity is a unique index that coalesces the two nullable goal columns**, because two nulls read as distinct to a unique index and the anchor finding would otherwise be inserted once per recompute forever. **Recompute runs in the writing transaction, not from the outbox**, because no relay host drains it: a topic with no consumer is a pending row nobody reads. Same call as P3-T05, and the stronger guarantee besides. One `realign` helper is the only path, and it is deliberately called from fewer places than the scoring `recompute`: creation, update, close, reopen, a cycle move (both cycles, and the old cycle id is read **before** the update because `returning` gives the new values) and the first key result only. §5.2 measures structure, so publishing a check-in and recording a value do not call it and a test proves the score does not move when one does. **Publish gate 4 became evaluable**, which is what P3-T04 was waiting for: the workflow snapshot now carries each key result's register entries, and an empty register is a real answer rather than `undefined`. P3-T04's own test asserting gate 4 was blocked "until P3-T09" was updated, which is that task's promise falling due rather than a regression. **Two refusals that are rules, not validation.** A goal dependency needs edit on **both** ends, because a link the other side cannot see is an assumption. And only the providing space confirms (§5.4): a depending team that could confirm its own dependency would turn the register into a wish list, and a provider named as free text cannot be confirmed at all, so that path says to name a risk owner instead. A risk owner clears gate 4 without claiming a confirmation, and the register keeps saying the dependency is unconfirmed. **Verified: `packages/core` 836/836** against a real database (20 new in `test/alignment.test.ts` covering the task's test plan and all five of the design document's acceptance criteria, plus 30 golden-master rows), `packages/method` 105/105, `packages/ui` 141/141, `apps/web` 16/16, end to end 25/25 from a clean build. Every static gate clean, including a boundary violation it caught first: the register's space-name lookup read a protected table outside the getter, now marked with the reason (every human member already holds `view` on every space through the `workspace_standard` binding P3-T01 gives them, so it discloses nothing, and a register that said "a team" instead of "Product" would be useless in the room it is read in). Migration 0025 ran against the dev database. **No screen ships here, on purpose.** The task card lists no surface, and UIUX-PLAN §10 attributes S-10 to P4-T03 and P3-T09 and S-16 to P3-T10, P3-T09 and P4-T06: this task delivers the data those screens read, and `alignment.read` returns the score, the findings with the goal each one opens, and the whole register with the provider and risk owner resolved to names. **Still to do here:** the §5.3 semantic findings are the Coach's at P4-T03, which is why the table has a `source` column from the start and why this engine filters on `source = 'engine'` before it touches anything. A structural recompute that cleared by scope rather than by source would delete the Coach's work every time somebody edited a weight |
| P3-T10 | Goal surfaces: explorer, detail, alignment studio | done | agung | 2026-08-13 | **Two of the three screens ship here. S-14 does not, and that is the honest state of this row.** S-13 the explorer is at `/goals`: cycle switcher, level filter, tree and list modes, closed-goal toggle, and per row the health word, the progress bar, the confidence band derived from the key results that carry one, the key result count and the days overdue. Every control is a link rather than client state, so a filtered view is a URL somebody can send to the person who needs to see it. **Tree mode indents by the parent pointer, not by level**, because those two disagree exactly where it matters: a team goal aligned straight to a company goal is a level skip, and drawing it at team depth would hide the very thing the alignment score penalises. A goal whose parent is outside the filter is drawn at the root and labelled, rather than dropped. S-16 the alignment studio is at `/goals/studio`, on the new `alignment.graph` read: one row per level, vertical connectors for the parent pointer, dashed ones for dependencies, pan by dragging, zoom, link mode that connects two goals into a dependency by clicking each in turn, and the three-tab panel of details, health and review. The review tab is empty and says why: the Coach's semantic findings need the provider and arrive at P4-T03, while the structural gaps in the health tab work with it off and always will. **Layout is computed, never stored.** A saved node position is a second thing to keep in step with the tree and goes stale the moment somebody re-parents a goal. **Virtualisation is by viewport intersection rather than list windowing**, because a canvas has two axes and a row window would still draw every card in a wide level; a connector is drawn when either end is visible, so a line never stops in mid-air. **Keyboard traverse walks the full ordered list, not what happens to be drawn**, and selecting scrolls the card into view and moves focus to it. The container carries no `tabIndex`: the cards are real buttons, so Tab already reaches the cascade and key events bubble from whichever card holds focus, and a focusable wrapper around focusable children is a second tab stop that does nothing. **The thousand-node budget is measured, not claimed.** The geometry is a pure module with its own suite: every node placed exactly once, an unused level taking no vertical band, children ordered under their parent, the traverse order containing all 1,000 ids, virtualisation drawing strictly fewer cards than exist and all of them when zoomed out, and layout plus filter for 1,000 nodes inside 100 ms. **One real defect the browser found that no gate did, and it is worth remembering:** the geometry module was first called `layout.ts` and sat inside a route directory, where the App Router reserves that name for a route layout and demands a default-exported React component. Typecheck, lint and seven passing unit tests all said fine; the page returned 500. Renamed to `geometry.ts`. **Verified:** `apps/web` 23/23 (7 new), typecheck, lint, dead code and boundaries clean, and both screens exercised at `openokr.test`, including the health tab reading 100 against the 75 threshold with no structural gaps. **Still to do, and this row stays open until it lands:** S-14, the goal detail's enrichment. `/goals/[id]` exists from P3-T04 with the fields, the role reassignment and the close and reopen transitions; what it still needs is the right rail (alignment parent, horizontal dependencies, rolled-up child goals, watchers), the per-key-result sparkline with its forecast, and inline value and confidence editing. The explorer's own inline weight and confidence editing and its quick check-in are part of the same piece of work and are not built either. **S-14 landed after the row above was written, and the task is still not done.** The right rail is on `/goals/[id]`: what this goal supports, what hangs off it, its horizontal links and its own register entries, from one new read `goals.relations`. Every block says something when it is empty rather than disappearing, and the orphan case names the 12-point cost instead of showing a blank panel. Each key result now carries a sparkline of its value history with the §3.6 forecast beside it, and inline value editing through `goals.recordValue`, proven in a browser: 55 to 58 moved the goal from 74% to 89%. **Confidence is deliberately not editable here, and that is the method rather than a missing parameter.** §3.2 puts confidence on the check-in, where it arrives with a narrative and a status; an input that let somebody drop a key result to 0.2 with no sentence attached would be the one number in the product that can change without anybody saying why. The card asks for "inline value and confidence editing" and this is a deviation, recorded rather than quietly satisfied by adding a parameter to `recordValue`. **The forecast horizon is the cycle end read from the cycle row**, not a day count: the scoring recompute prefers the cycle end and falls back to the key result due date, and a chart projecting to a different horizon than the stored forecast would be two answers to one question. **Two things this leaves open, both found in a browser.** The forecast is faithful arithmetic and poor advice on sparse data: three values inside two days, fitted by ordinary least squares and projected six weeks out, reads "672.02 by 2026-09-30" for a key result whose target is 60. Nothing here adjusts it, because §3.6 and decision D-5 are the method's; **it is worth asking whether §3.6 should require a minimum span before a forecast is shown at all.** And the sparkline overlaps its own row visually rather than sitting on its own line, which is a layout fix nobody should have to read a number through. **Both are closed now (2026-08-17), and the second turned out to be worse than a layout bug.** The row gained the gap it was missing, but the real defect was that `goals.keyResultHistory` answers newest first, which is right for a table and backwards for a chart: the sparkline plotted in array order, so the line ran right to left and a key result that climbed from 41 to 60 **drew as a falling line**. The chart was lying about direction. The component sorts oldest-first for itself; the read is unchanged, because the table wants what it returns. Verified in a browser at `openokr.test`: the same key result now rises. **Still to do, and why this row stays open:** the explorer has no side panel and no quick check-in, so the task's acceptance criterion, "when a key result is checked in from the side panel, then progress, RAG and health update live in both the list and the canvas", was **not met when this row was written, and is met now**: P3-T11 lists `side-panel opening` among its own deliverables, so the control landed there, and recording a value from the map moved the key result and its goal to 100% live in the list. The explorer still has no panel of its own, and does not need one now that the map has it. |
