| P3-T12 | KPIs: categories, records, grid | in_review | agung | 2026-08-13 | **The engine and the schema ship here. The actions and the S-20 grid do not, and this row stays open until they do.** Sections 1 to 3 of the approved KPI design are implemented in `packages/method/src/kpi.ts`, in the method package for the reason the scoring and alignment engines are there: every function is a METHOD.md §6 rule taking a §11 threshold as an argument, and the same code has to run in the grid as somebody types, on the server before the write, and in the importer normalising a legacy period key. **46 golden-master rows passed on the first run**, read out of `docs/design/p3-t00-kpi-engine.md` at run time: twelve period cases across all five frequencies, nineteen achievement cases in both directions, fourteen corridor states, plus an idempotence check the matrices do not contain. That last one guards the write path: normalisation runs before the unique index sees a value, so a weekly rule that stepped back a week when handed a Monday would silently merge two periods. **Migration 0026** adds `kpi_categories`, `kpis`, `kpi_records` and `kpi_shares`, with invariants as check constraints rather than application code: an owner that agrees with its kind, a KPI that is not its own parent, a corridor whose watch band is not above its healthy band, and a formula that belongs to a calculated KPI and only to one. **The period uniqueness index is deliberately not partial on `deleted_at`.** Every other index in this repository excludes soft-deleted rows; this one must not, because a soft-deleted record still occupies its period, and an index that ignored it would let a second row be inserted for the same period and both be live the moment anybody restored the first. **`key_results.kpi_id` finally got its foreign key**, which had been a plain uuid column since P3-T04 waiting for this table. It immediately caught a P3-T04 test inserting a synthetic uuid with a comment reading "KPIs arrive at P3-T12", exactly as the `spaces` foreign key caught two at P3-T01. That test now creates a real KPI, which is the stronger assertion. **The soft-delete lint also caught two unscoped updates in the P3-T09 alignment service**, in the finding upsert and the stale soft-delete. They were there when P3-T09 was committed and the gate did not report them until 0026 raised the soft-deletable table count from 43 to 50; both are scoped now. **Verified:** `packages/core` 882/882 against a real database, `packages/method` 105/105, migration 0026 applied to the dev database, and typecheck, lint, dead code, boundaries and both database lints clean. **Still to do:** the actions (categories, KPIs, and the record upsert that normalises the period before it writes), the keyboard-first grid at S-20 with grouping and sparklines, sharing, and the database test for uniqueness under concurrent writes. The acceptance criterion, a monthly KPI at eighty percent of target showing the watch state and re-recording updating rather than duplicating, is **not met yet**: the engine returns `watch` for it and the unique index is in place, but nothing writes a record through them **Part two landed after the row above was written, and the acceptance criterion is met.** The actions are `kpis.createCategory`, `kpis.create`, `kpis.record` and `kpis.grid`. **The record write is an `on conflict` upsert on the unique index, not a read-then-write**, because a read-then-write is exactly the race two people typing in the grid would lose: both read nothing, both insert. Letting Postgres settle it means the second writer updates the first writer's row, which is what design §1 promises, and a test drives two concurrent writers to prove neither fails and exactly one row survives. **Achievement reads the newest period that has an actual value, not the current calendar period.** A monthly KPI on the third of the month has no value for this month yet, and reading the empty period would flip every KPI in the workspace to `no_data` at every month boundary. The target comes from that record, falling back to the KPI's standing default. **The recovery link is hardcoded to `none`**, so `recovering` is unreachable, which is honest: no recovery goal can exist until P3-T14. **The grid is at `/kpis`**: KPIs as rows, periods as columns, corridor-coloured cells, grouped by category, Enter commits and moves down, the arrows move without committing, Escape restores, and a calculated KPI is read-only. **A cell is coloured by its own corridor rather than by the KPI's stored state**, because the stored state is the KPI's standing now and a cell three months back has its own band; colouring every cell by the current state would say last quarter was healthy because this one is. **Two defects the browser found within a minute, neither visible to any gate.** The columns were derived only from records that exist, so a brand new KPI had no columns, so there was nowhere to type its first value and the screen was unusable; the current period is always a column now, and which bucket today falls in is the engine's answer rather than the component's. And `today` comes from the server in the workspace timezone, never from `new Date()` in the browser: a value typed at 23:00 in Jakarta would otherwise land in yesterday's bucket for a workspace on UTC. **Verified:** `packages/core` 894/894 against a real database (12 new, covering the acceptance criterion, all five frequencies on the write path, both directions, the concurrent race, the negative-target diagnostic and both refusals), `apps/web` 23/23, every static gate clean, and the whole loop driven by hand at `openokr.test`: a KPI created, 80 typed into the August cell, Enter committing it, the cell turning amber and the standing figure reading 80% in the watch colour. **Still to do:** S-20 wants one column set per frequency, which strictly means one table per frequency; today there is one set with a dot marking the cells a KPI's frequency does not have, and the restructure is recorded rather than pretended. Row sparklines and category subtotals need the §6 aggregate rules from P3-T13. The filters and `kpi_shares` are unbuilt: the table exists with its policy, and nothing writes to it. **Navigation, added after review:** the sidebar carries one entry per module by UIUX-PLAN.md §4, which left `/kpis/trees`, `/kpis/recovery` and `/goals/studio` reachable only by typing the URL. `apps/web/lib/section-tabs.tsx` puts a link strip at the top of every screen in a module, and the two hand-written cross-links it replaces are gone. Links rather than client state, so a tab is a URL somebody can send. Driven in the browser across all five screens |
| P3-T13 | KPI formula engine | done | agung | 2026-08-13 | **A typed expression tree validated with Zod. No string parsing at evaluation time and no dynamic evaluation, ever.** A formula arrives from the builder as a tree and is stored as one, so there is no second representation for the two to disagree about, and nothing in the evaluator can be talked into running code. Parentheses do not exist as syntax, because a tree already carries precedence. **43 golden-master rows passed**, read out of `docs/design/p3-t00-kpi-engine.md`: fourteen formula cases, fourteen aggregation cases across every frequency pairing, eight cascade cases, plus checks the matrices do not carry. **The depth, node and reference bounds are deliberately not §11 parameters.** §11 holds the numbers the practice fires on; these are limits on an evaluator, and a workspace has no business raising them, because a deeper tree is not a different management style, it is a formula nobody can read. **One real defect my own test found.** The measurement walk was iterative, and `formulaNodeSchema.safeParse` in front of it was not: Zod's lazy recursion threw a `RangeError` on a 20,000-deep tree rather than returning a failure, so an imported or hostile formula could have taken the process down. There is now an iterative depth pre-check on the raw value before Zod sees it, and the comment claiming safety is true rather than half-true. **Null propagates and never becomes a number.** `0 × null` is null, not 0: short-circuiting would be arithmetically defensible and reporting-wise wrong, because the figure would look measured when nothing was measured. Division by zero is null with its own diagnostic rather than 0, which would read as a real `unhealthy` (decision D-9). A null result writes **no actual value** and records the diagnostic on the record, so the KPI reads `no_data` and the grid can say which source is missing instead of showing an empty cell that looks like nobody got round to it. **Cross-frequency references fold with the source's own aggregate.** A coarser source broadcasts down rather than being divided: nobody said the quarter was evenly spread. An empty span yields null except `count`, which yields 0, because zero records is a real count and zero as a sum of nothing is a fabrication. **Migration 0027** adds `kpi_dependencies`, one row per formula edge, with a self-reference check constraint, and `kpi_records.diagnostic`. The table exists rather than being derived from the formulas on every read because the cascade walks it the other way, from a changed source to everything downstream, and answering "who depends on this" from the formulas would be a table scan per keystroke in the grid. **A cycle is refused before the insert, against the graph as it would be**, so a cycle never reaches the table and no read has to defend against one forever. A test proves only the first edge survives the attempt. **The cascade runs in the writing transaction**, the third time this repository has made that call (P3-T05, P3-T09, P3-T12) and for the same two reasons: no relay host drains the outbox, and in-transaction leaves no window where a dependent shows a number its source no longer supports. The design document drives it from the outbox and `cascadeFromKpi` is what the relay will call. **Verified:** `packages/core` 946/946 against a real database (9 new, including the acceptance criterion end to end: a monthly KPI as the sum of two others, a source changing, both the dependent and its own dependent following, and the self-referencing formula refused), `packages/method` 105/105, every static gate clean, migration 0027 applied to the dev database. **Still to do:** there is no formula builder in the interface, so a formula is set through the action and not yet through a screen; the grid already renders a calculated KPI read-only and P3-T12's row records the formula chip as outstanding. Row sparklines and category subtotals now have their aggregate rules and are worth doing beside the builder |

Breadth-first is the rule rather than an implementation detail: it is what puts a leading child of the root ahead of a leading grandchild reached through a lagging one, which is §6.3's reading rule. Actions: `kpis.createTree`, `kpis.launchRecovery` and `kpis.recoveryDraft`. **The §10 key result link is real now**, which is what P3-T05 was waiting for: a KPI-linked key result reads the KPI's **real** achievement clamped to 0 to 100, never the effective figure, because a recovery key result reading its own KPI's effective health would feed its own progress back into itself. A KPI with no achievement at all leaves the key result where it was rather than dropping it to zero, since a KPI nobody has recorded is unmeasured rather than failing. **There is deliberately no `kpis.closeRecovery` action.** Closing a recovery is closing its objective through the ordinary `goals.close` path, retrospective and all, and the KPI follows on the next recompute because a closed goal resolves the link to `closed` and the corridor precedence hands the KPI back to its real band. A second action would be a second way to close a goal. Launching twice is refused only while the existing recovery is **open**: a KPI that fell over twice is entitled to a second recovery. **Two screens ship: S-19 at `/kpis/recovery` and S-18 at `/kpis/trees`.** The board is one list across every tree, unhealthy or recovering only, each card carrying achievement, the corridor, the objective with its progress and key result count, or a one-click launch. A node whose parent sits in another tree is drawn at the root and not dropped. **Verified:** `packages/core` **984/984** against a real database, including six new in `test/kpis.test.ts` covering every line of the task's test plan (three leading drivers becoming three key results with the right baselines, targets and directions; the descent through a lagging child; the placeholder; the refusal of a second open recovery; effective health reaching 70 while the real number stays at 60; and the closure stamp written once and not again). `packages/method` 105/105, `apps/web` 23/23, 27 golden rows, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries (347 files), dead code, and both database linters. | P3-T16 | Comments, reactions and discussion wiring | in_progress | obed | 2026-08-14 | **Committed at `f7b9b5d` with three follow-up fixes, and the row was never updated, so this entry is written by the other lane after reviewing the merge.** Migration 0032 adds `comments` (rich text on goals, key results, check-ins, cycles and documents) and `reactions` (one emoji per member per subject, unique across soft-deleted rows so a restore cannot collide). **Four defects the repository's own gates caught after the merge, three now fixed:** the two tables shipped with `enable row level security` but **without `force row level security`**, so the table owner, which is the role migrations run as, bypassed the tenant floor entirely; their policies had **no `with check`**, so the floor constrained reads and left writes open to carry another workspace's id; and they used the bare `current_setting('app.workspace_id')`, which raises when the setting is absent instead of matching nothing. All three are corrected in 0032 itself with the human's approval to edit a migration in place, because the correct history for a tenant hole is one where the table never existed without FORCE. Anyone who applied 0032 before this fix has to recreate their development database: the migration runner hashes content and will refuse the changed file. The soft-delete lint also caught three unscoped queries in `comments/service.ts`, and the boundary gate caught three writes whose `openokr:allow-mutation` markers had no colon and no reason, so the gate never recognised them. Both are fixed. **The fourth was the important one, and it is fixed: `comments.test.ts` had never passed against a real database.** Two defects sat behind it. Its fixture created a `member`-owned goal with no member on it, which `goals_owner_matches_kind` refuses. Correcting that exposed the real one: **both read actions took the acting member from `context.actor.memberId`, which a read never has.** Only `runOperation` resolves an actor, against rows loaded inside the writing transaction; a read's context carries the user id and nothing else, so `comments.list` and `reactions.list` refused every caller with "a system actor cannot do this". A `readingMember` helper now resolves it the way every other read action in the repository does, and refuses an unknown user with the same not-found an outsider gets, so membership is not disclosed. **`packages/core/test/comments.test.ts` 5/5.** **A fifth defect, found by the registry's own invariant once it finally saw these actions: `reactions.add` and `reactions.remove` declared `view`.** A write reachable at view is exactly the silent escalation that invariant exists to stop, and it meant anybody who could merely read a goal could attach something to it. Both are `comment` now, which is what the level is for. **A rule question came with it, and it is recorded here because a human may want to reverse it.** The invariant read "every write needs at least `edit`", which would have forced `comments.create` up to `edit` and handed every commenter the right to rewrite the objective. That rule is not in TECHNICAL-PLAN; it lives only in `registry.test.ts`, while §4.1 does define four levels with `comment` at 40 sitting between view and edit for exactly this purpose. The invariant now keeps `edit` as the floor everywhere except the `comments` and `reactions` domains, and asserts separately that **no write anywhere is reachable at `view`**, which is what its own comment said it was protecting. P3-T07 hit the same rule from the other side and widened `acknowledge` to `edit`; that was right, because acknowledging a check-in changes the goal's state. Writing a comment does not. The lesson underneath all of this is the one this repository keeps relearning: the commits that landed this task reported `pnpm typecheck` and `pnpm lint`, and neither can see a write that refuses at run time, a tenant floor left open, or a write reachable at view. **The surface is wired now, and it was worse than the dead-code report suggested.** `pnpm dead-code` flagged three uncalled actions; the real state was that `page.tsx` never rendered `CommentThread` at all, so the whole discussion was unreachable rather than half built. `goal-comments.tsx` binds the four writes to the thread, the page reads `comments.list` and `reactions.list` beside its other server reads, and reactions render as chips with the reader's own marked, because a count with no "did I" in it makes somebody click again to find out. **A second defect the wiring exposed: every colour class in the component was invented.** `fg-secondary`, `fg-tertiary`, `fg-primary`, `bg-bg-primary`, `border-border`, `destructive` and `accent` are not in `tokens.css`; the real names are `ink`, `ink-2/3/4`, `line`, `surface`, `bad` and `brand`. The thread would have rendered with no colour at all, which is the same class of defect P2-T10 hit when the app shipped with no Tailwind output. **Driven by hand at `openokr.test`:** a comment posted and rendered with its author and timestamp, and the reaction came back as `👍 1` in the own-reaction style. `apps/web` 23/23, typecheck 10/10, biome clean. **One dead-code finding remains, and it is not this task's:** `packages/core/src/embeddings/service.ts` has no caller, because P4-T13's outbox worker is the piece that would call it. Recorded on that row rather than hidden behind an ignore entry. **Decided by the human on 2026-08-18, and done:** migration 0032 carried 123 box-drawing characters (U+2500) in two decorative comment rules, at lines 14 and 57. No other migration in the repository uses a character WIN1252 cannot represent; the section sign and the em dash both exist there. Postgres refuses the whole migration on a WIN1252 cluster, which is what the Windows installer creates by default, so a self-hosted install on Windows cannot migrate. CI is UTF8 and stays green. Editing the file is not the fix on its own: `_migrations` keeps a checksum per file and an edit raises "Applied migration 0032 was edited after it ran" on every database that already applied it, which is both developer machines. Of the three ways out, the human chose the first: edit 0032 and rebuild the dev databases, since nothing is in production. The two rules are now plain ASCII headings, the schema is byte-identical, and the local dev database was dropped, migrated and re-seeded to prove it. Obed's needs the same, and `pnpm db:seed` fills it back after the wizard. **The rule is mechanical now rather than remembered:** `pnpm db:lint` refuses any migration carrying a character WIN1252 cannot store, naming the character and its count, so migration 0045 cannot repeat this. The test harness half of the same problem is fixed and needed no decision: two `create database` calls named no encoding and so inherited the cluster's, which is why this only ever failed on a developer's machine |

## Phase 3 task notes

The rows above carry a one-line summary and what is still outstanding, which is
what somebody signing them off needs. The reasoning behind each decision lives
here, because a table cell holding ten sentences is a table nobody reads.

### P3-T11 Work Map

**The front door at `/`, replacing the proving dashboard P1-T08 put there.**
That page was scaffolding and said so, and this row has carried the follow-up
since P3-T01. One tree over goals, sub-goals and key results with S-01's uniform
node contract: health including staleness, progress, confidence, owner,
timeframe and next step. Initiatives and tasks join the same contract in Phase 5
without the row component changing.

**The tree is flattened on the server**, parents before children, with depth as
a number rather than nesting. A nested render would put DOM depth at the mercy
of how deep somebody aligned their goals, and would make virtualisation
impossible later, because a virtual list needs siblings.

**Every node is a deep link** (`?node=<id>`), so a row is a URL somebody can
send, and the panel opens without the list losing its place because the list
never re-orders.

**The side panel carries the quick check-in**, which is what closes P3-T10's
acceptance criterion: recording 60 from the panel moved both the key result and
its goal from 89% to 100%, live in the list, with the panel still open on the
same node. The write revalidates the map, the explorer, the goal page, the
studio and the cycle together, which is what "in both the list and the canvas"
means.

**A value, and not a status.** Health follows §3.5's precedence, and the one
path that lets a human set it is the check-in, which needs a narrative. A panel
that flipped a goal to green in one click would be the shortcut around the
ritual the product exists to keep. That is visible on screen: the goal reads
100% and `caution` at the same time, because the last published check-in said
caution.

**Three end-to-end specs and three files were left stranded by the
replacement**, and both are fixed here rather than left to rot. The wizard and
registration specs asserted the old dashboard's "Signed in as" panel, its
definition list of §4.14 defaults and its workspace rename; they now assert the
Work Map, the provisioned cycle nobody chose, and a real write on admin general.
`workspace-overview.tsx`, `rename-workspace.tsx` and `overview-skeleton.tsx` are
deleted, and the dead-code gate is what found the third.

**Verified:** `apps/web` 23/23, end to end 25/25 from a clean build, every static
gate clean, and the whole loop driven by hand at `openokr.test`.

**Still to do.** The tree is flattened for virtualisation but not yet
virtualised, which is honest rather than done: the budget matters at the scale
P3-T17's seed will produce, and `@tanstack/react-virtual` is in the locked stack
for it. Scope tabs are a cycle switcher only; company, my spaces and one space
need the space filter the explorer already has. KPI tiles are P3-T12.

| P4-T13a | The embedding table and the outbox worker | in_review | agung | 2026-08-26 | **The row was `in_progress` since 2026-08-14 and its own note was wrong.** It said "Migration 0028 adds the `embeddings` table"; the file is 0031, and 0028 is `kpi_trees_and_recovery`. The revert at `7cb0969` left the migration, the Drizzle schema, `EmbeddingService` and a ten-test chunker in place, so what was actually missing was narrower than the row implied: nothing enqueued an embedding job and nothing drained one. A grep for outbox topics across `packages/core` found five, none of them about embedding. **The enqueue is in the Operation pipeline, one place, at Agung's direction on 2026-08-26.** Two ways in, and the second is the shape `ActivityInput.notify` already set: either the activity's own subject is embeddable, or the write names the content on `activity.embed` because its activity points at a container. A retro note's activity names the space and a narrative's names the goal, so embedding the subject would embed the wrong thing. **I told Agung this cost nothing per action and that was only half right**, and the correction is recorded: goals, key results, check-ins, blockers and comments come free because their activity subject is the content; five session-internal writes declare it in one line each. **The idempotency key carries a timestamp, and coalescing was the first idea and wrong.** One pending row per entity looked tidier until the collision case: a second edit arriving while the relay holds the first row would collide on the unique key, and a failed enqueue inside the write transaction would roll back a legitimate domain change. Duplicate rows are the cheaper mistake because the hash check makes the second a no-op, which is what the acceptance criterion measures. **The worker reads the text rather than carrying it.** A row written three edits ago would otherwise embed text the product no longer holds. It reads inside the tenant setting like every other read, holds no ambient authority, and drops an entity type it cannot read with a reason instead of throwing, because a row that retries until it dead-letters is an alert about a queue rather than about the product. **A draft check-in is never embedded.** P3-T07 makes a draft visible only to its author, and embedding one would put unpublished text into an index everybody's retrieval reads. Not asked for by the test plan, asked for by a promise the product already made. **A cycle is in neither list.** It is a period with a name and no prose, so embedding "Q1 2026" would add a retrieval hit that answers nothing. The plan's "cycle artifacts" is read as the learnings and the next-cycle drafts, which are both here. **Two test-plan lines are measured differently on this machine, and that is stated rather than hidden.** `EmbeddingService.index()` only asks a model when pgvector is present: without the extension it stores the chunk text and leaves the vector null, which is migration 0031's documented fallback. This Postgres has no pgvector, so a model-call count of one is unreachable here. The tests guard the call-count assertions on the extension and also assert the observable that holds either way: an unchanged hash takes the skip path, which writes nothing, so the row's `updated_at` does not move. **The vector path itself is therefore not exercised on this machine**, and a run with pgvector is what would prove it. **Two gate findings, both real.** The dead-code gate had `src/embeddings/service.ts` on knip's ignore list because nothing imported it; the exemption is gone now that the worker does, and removing it immediately found two exports that only their own file reads. That exemption would have hidden a dead service indefinitely. **Verified:** embeddings-worker 10/10, embeddings-chunker 10/10 unchanged, the full `packages/core` suite after the pipeline change, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 448 files, dead-code with the exemption removed, both database linters. **The pipeline change touches every write in the product**, which is why the full suite ran for this row rather than at the end of a batch. **What this row said on 2026-08-14, kept because it records what was believed then, including the migration number that was wrong:** **Started: no Phase 3 dependency (depends on P2-T15 only, done).** Migration 0028 adds the `embeddings` table with optional pgvector support: the extension is created when available, otherwise the vector column stays text and retrieval falls back to full-text search. Schema in `packages/db/src/schema/embeddings.ts`. The chunker (`packages/core/src/embeddings/chunker.ts`) splits content at paragraph, sentence, then word boundaries with configurable overlap. The embedding service (`packages/core/src/embeddings/service.ts`) handles index, remove and hybrid retrieve, with content-hash dedup to skip re-embedding unchanged content. Chunker unit test written. **Not yet verified against a running database** (the test harness OOMed on this machine). **Reviewed by the other lane after the merge, and it does not hold. Four findings, none fixed here because the rewrite is this task's own:** (1) the migration is **0031**, not 0028; 0028 is P3-T14's KPI trees, and this row named a file that belongs to somebody else. (2) `embeddings` shipped without `force row level security`, so the owner bypassed the tenant floor, and without `with check`, so a write could carry another workspace's id. Both are corrected in 0031 with the human's approval, along with an `openokr:hard-delete` marker stating why a derived chunk is not soft-deleted: a soft-deleted row would still sit in the vector index and still come back from a nearest-neighbour search. (3) **`embeddings/service.ts` issued raw `client.query` SQL against a pool it held itself**, which broke three hard rules at once: the database reached outside Drizzle, writes outside the Operation pipeline (four boundary violations), and no workspace on the connection. `index()` did call `set_config('app.workspace_id', $1, true)` first, but **`true` means transaction-local and there was no transaction**, so the setting was discarded the instant that statement committed and every query after it ran unscoped. `remove()` did not set it at all. (4) With the floor forced, those queries would have matched nothing: they only worked while it was open. **All of (3) and (4) are fixed here.** Both write paths now run through `withWorkspace(drizzle(pool), …)`, which opens a real transaction and sets the tenant for its whole length, and use Drizzle's query builder rather than SQL strings. The three writes carry `openokr:allow-mutation` markers stating why an embedding is derived rather than a domain change: nobody decided it, and an audit row per chunk would bury the one the author's own edit produced. `pnpm check:boundaries` passes, 363 files. **A fifth finding, and it explains the note above about the harness OOMing: the chunker could not terminate.** `chunkText`'s loop guard compared the next start against `end - maxSize`, which reduces to "only when the overlap is at least the chunk size" and misses the case that actually happens: a paragraph or sentence boundary found close to `start` pulls `end` back, `end - overlap` lands at or behind `start`, and the loop stops advancing while the chunk array grows until the process dies. It was not the machine. The guard now says what it means, that the next start must be strictly greater than this one, and **`embeddings-chunker.test.ts` runs 10/10 in one second where it previously killed its worker after 34.** **Still to do:** integration tests against a real database (only the chunker has a unit test), the outbox-driven worker that triggers embedding on entity writes, local embedding support, and the access-filtered reload after retrieval. The retrieve path still carries a non-null assertion on the embed function. **`knip.json` carries `src/embeddings/service.ts` in the `packages/core` ignore list, and that entry is temporary.** Nothing imports the service because the worker that would call it is the outstanding item above; the ignore keeps CI honest about everything else rather than letting one known gap mask the rest. **Delete the entry in the same change as the worker.** If it is still there when P4-T13 closes, the gate has been quietly weakened rather than satisfied |
| P4-T13b | Access-filtered retrieval | in_review | agung | 2026-08-26 | **A defect found and fixed, and it was total rather than partial.** The three retrieval queries ran straight on the pool with no `app.workspace_id`. Row-level security is forced on `embeddings`, so retrieval returned an empty list for every caller no matter what was indexed. It is the exact shape `EmbeddingService.index()`'s own comment records having hit once already, and nothing noticed because nothing called retrieval yet. All three now run with the workspace applied for their transaction. **Retrieval is filtered by asking the same getter every other read asks.** `governingResource` maps each embeddable type onto one of the five subject types the access getter resolves, and `getAccessScoped` decides. The alternative was a `context_id` column on `embeddings` with a SQL filter, which would be faster and would be a second implementation of the access model. Asking the getter means retrieval cannot drift: a member who loses access to a space stops seeing its chunks on the next query, with no reindex and no backfill. A test asserts exactly that, and asserts the index is untouched by it. **A gap in my own first pass, caught by a test I wrote for it.** The `goal` and `comment` cases returned the id without reading the row, because the id was already the answer. An access context row survives a soft delete, so `getAccessScoped` said yes for a deleted goal and its chunk came back. Both cases read their row now and `activeOnly` does the work every other case was already getting for free. **Candidates are over-fetched four to one, and the limitation is stated rather than hidden.** Up to `limit` readable hits come out of the first `limit * 4` candidates, so a member with narrow access can get fewer than `limit` even when more readable chunks exist further down the ranking. Four is a guess with a reason: most readers can see most of their workspace, and a factor that multiplied the work by ten to serve the narrowest reader would slow every query for the common case. **The honest fix is the `context_id` column**, and it is recorded here rather than pretending the over-fetch is free. **`memberId` is required on the input.** Retrieval without a requester is retrieval that cannot be filtered, and an optional field would make the leak a default rather than a mistake. **Which path was proved, and which was not.** This machine has no pgvector, so every query in the test file takes the full-text path, which is the degradation this row asks for and the second test-plan line. The hybrid vector path is written, is reached when the extension is present, and is **not exercised here**. The access filter sits above both, so it holds either way, but a run with pgvector is what would prove the ranking. **The refusal tested is suspension, not membership.** P3-T01's `workspace_standard` binding gives every active member `edit` across the workspace, so "a member outside the space" is not a refusal this product has, and a test asserting it would pass for the wrong reason. Session content is also tested through its space, because a retro note's own row names no space and its access has to walk through the session. **Verified:** embeddings-retrieval 7/7, embeddings-worker 10/10, the full `packages/core` suite, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 449 files, dead-code, both database linters. |

| P4-T01f | Session stages, process health and the rhythm diagnostic | done | agung | 2026-08-18 | **§9's facilitator guidance was already in `guidance.ts` from an earlier task, so this is the rest of §7 and §8.** `sessions.ts` carries §7.1's three rituals, §7.2's four weekly steps, §8.1's eleven review stages with the document's own minutes, §8.5's five process-health statements, §8.7's four management-retro questions and §8.6's diagnostic. **The eleven stages sum to exactly the sixty minutes §7.1 gives the review, and a test asserts it**, which is the kind of agreement two documents can lose without anybody noticing. **The statement order is load-bearing and now says so.** §8.6 averages statements 2 and 5 into the rhythm score, so renumbering §8.5 silently changes the diagnostic. `RHYTHM_STATEMENTS` names those positions rather than leaving two indexes written down somewhere else, and a test pins them to the cadence statement and the say-it-early one by their words. **Durations are not §11 parameters, deliberately.** §11 holds numbers the practice fires on; a stage length is pacing a facilitator overruns on purpose, and §8.1 says so in as many words. The two numbers the diagnostic does fire on, 0.7 and 3.5, were already in §11 and are read from there. **Two refusals rather than guesses.** `rhythmScore` returns null when either statement went unanswered, because a diagnostic built on a missing answer is worse than none: it reads as evidence. `lowestProcessHealthStatement` keeps the earlier statement on a tie, so next cycle's process OKR does not depend on iteration order. The weekly steps carry no minutes, because §7.1 gives the check-in a range and inventing four numbers that sum to it would put a figure in the product the method never wrote. **Verified:** `packages/method` 189/189 (18 new, with every statement, question, verdict and prescription compared against METHOD.md at run time), typecheck 10/10, biome and knip clean |
| P4-T01g | The conformance suite, `pnpm method:check` | done | agung | 2026-08-18 | **The gate found three real defects on its first run, two of them mine.** It checks three things from the design's §16: every rule key the documents cite resolves inside the package; every parameter METHOD.md §11 lists exists in the registry and the registry invents none the document does not carry; every approved corpus entry is exercised by a test. It reads the documents rather than a transcription of them. **Defect one, a naming conflict in the approved design.** The coach watch list gave seven situations its own key names, `COACH-CONFLICT` and `RHYTHM-CHECKIN-OVERDUE` among them, for rules that already had keys in AI-NATIVE-PLAN.md §6.4. Both arguments read well and both were wrong: METHOD.md decides what §11 carries. Both wrong comments are replaced by what actually happened. **One parameter resolves by union rather than replacement**, and it is the only one: §11 words the word lists as "a workspace may add terms; the canon terms remain", so an override adds. Replacing would let a workspace switch a canon rule off by overriding it with a short list, which is a change to the practice and not a setting. **Two guards sit in front of the parse**, because a regex that matches nothing agrees with everything: the trigger parse must find at least forty keys and the §11 parse at least forty parameters before their contents are compared. | P4-T03 | Publish gates | done | agung | 2026-08-18 | **Most of this existed from P3-T03**: `publishGates`, the recompute on the publish path, the refusal naming the gate, and the checklist with its honest third state. The refusal names the check, the offending key result and its objective. **Every unmet gate links at what would fix it.** A checklist saying "gate 4 is red" and stopping has told a facilitator the one thing the dot already told them. The destinations are phases and screens rather than fields, because a gate is a property of the whole set. **The override, and everything about it is deliberately uncomfortable.** §4.5's gates are hard, and a product with no way past a hard refusal is one people leave. So: the same `full` access publishing needs, a reason of at least twenty characters validated at the boundary, and an audit row under **a different action name**, `workflow.override`, so the log can be read for overrides without parsing a JSON column. The payload carries the reason and every gate that was red with its own missing list. An override against a green set is **refused**, because one recorded against nothing teaches the reader of the audit log that overrides are routine. **Four things the tests found rather than the code.** The audit chain orders by `seq` and not by a timestamp, because time is ambiguous under concurrency and the hash chain has exactly one order. A goal with no key results makes gate 2 red on KR-1 and KR-4, so the green case needs two of them, one leading and one lagging. Gate 5 wants a capacity note, which is rich text and not a string. Gate 6 wants a publication deadline the fixture leaves null. | P4-T04a | The nudge table and the due engine | done | agung | 2026-08-18 | **Migration 0033 with its tenant policy, and `notifications.nudge_id` finally has a target**: that column has carried since migration 0013 pointing at nothing, because nudges did not exist. **Two documents disagree about this table and the higher one won.** TECHNICAL-PLAN.md §4 line 220 specifies `kind`, `recipient_member_id`, `agent_id`, `scheduled_for`, `sent_at`, `acted_at` and a single `suppressed_reason`. The P4-T00 design document's §7 names the recipient `member_id`, has no `kind`, no `agent_id`, no `scheduled_for` and no `acted_at`, and splits suppression across two columns. The plan outranks a design document in CLAUDE.md's order, so the plan's columns shipped, and the human chose on 2026-08-18 to correct the design document to match. Its §7 now carries the shipped columns with the reasoning for each, and says it was corrected, so somebody reading only that section is not told to build the wrong table. **`kind` was the one column neither document defined a value set for.** Derived rather than invented: AI-NATIVE-PLAN.md §6.4 splits the catalogue into exactly two tables, rhythm and quality, and `trigger()` already carries that owner. The volume dashboard reads by it, because "the Champion is noisy this week" is a different finding from "the Coach is". **`scheduled_for` and `sent_at` are two columns because quiet hours move delivery without changing the decision.** One timestamp could not tell "held until morning" from "never sent". `acted_at` is what makes a nudge measurable rather than merely countable: a rule that fires often and is never acted on is noise. **The ladder is not in this code.** `escalation` in `packages/method` decides which step fires and which roles it reaches, golden-master tested with no clock and no rows; this resolves a role to a member and writes the row. A scheduler carrying its own copy of §11's day numbers is how a threshold stops being the threshold. The coordinator resolves through `resolveCoordinator`, so §4.2's manager fallback is not written a second time. **The sponsor step stops short and says so:** a space has no sponsor, the cycle does, and resolving that belongs to P4-T04c rather than escalating to somebody arbitrary now. **`now` is an input to the action.** A run that read the clock could not be driven across the fortnight §11's ladder is written about, and that fortnight is the acceptance criterion. **Two tests were wrong and the code was right.** The grace boundary is exclusive: §3.5 and `escalation` agree that at exactly the grace limit the goal is not yet outdated, and the first test ran at the boundary asserting the opposite. And suspending the *caller* is refused by the Operation pipeline before any of the engine runs, which is correct; the suspended-member test now suspends the reviewer and checks the champion still hears. None of those is an escalation, and deriving it that way would have woken somebody at two in the morning to tell them a check-in was due today. Urgency is now its own field, set by the engine because the engine is what knows who the step reached: the ladder having widened past the champion. The script creates the member, the agent row, its member group and a `view` binding on every space that already exists, with no workspace-wide binding, and is idempotent by predicate rather than by the ledger. Applied to the development database and verified on `/admin/agents`. **Five existing tests had to change, and each one is worth naming, because a seeded agent is a second member and a second member group**: provisioning's "one workspace, one member" count is now scoped to humans, its tenant-isolation case checks every row's workspace rather than counting one, the access model expects four groups instead of three and finds the founding member's group by member rather than by kind, and two agent tests filter to custom agents rather than assuming the list holds only what they created. | P4-T05b | The daily sweep and the cycle countdown | done | agung | 2026-08-20 | **Three of the Champion's four cadences, and the readers are the only thing that differs between them.** `runDueNudgesInTx` gained a `cadence`, and the suppression decision, the active-member filter, the row writing and the inbox insert stayed one path for all four. A cadence holding its own copy of any of that is how a morning summary ends up ignoring quiet hours. `hourly` is the default, so `nudges.run` and every P4-T05a caller behave exactly as before. **Each cadence has its own trigger string and its own `agent_runs` row**: `schedule.daily`, `schedule.weekly`, `schedule.cycle`. AI-NATIVE-PLAN §6.2 puts the check-in nudge in the **hourly** queue and gives the daily run the staleness sweep, which flips health rather than sending a message; §6.2 outranks IMPLEMENTATION-PLAN, so §6.2 is what was built and the card now says so. A daily run that also chased check-ins would mean two cadences reading the same rows. Confirmed with Agung before writing it. **The staleness sweep is P3-T06's function, not a second one.** The daily run calls `sweepStaleness`, the same code `pnpm cadence:sweep` calls, on its own transaction, so a health flip and this run's audit row commit together and mean the same thing whether a human typed the command or the agent reached it. It is reported as `staleFlipped` rather than counted into `recorded`, because flipping a goal's health is a write to the domain and not a message to a person. **Blocker aging reads P4-T07c's table and defines nothing.** The plan was to declare it and wait, on PHASE-4-SPLIT's rule that the table is P4-T07c's; Obed landed `blockers` while this was being written, so the reader is here instead. `blockerEscalation` has been golden-master tested since P3-T06 with nothing to read, and it is still the only thing deciding which step fires. **The recipient at step 1 is the blocker's own named owner, not the goal's champion**: the ladder calls that position `champion` because it means "whose problem this is", and for a blocker that is the member who agreed to the next action. **Nothing stamps `escalated_at` or `escalated_to_id`**: those are P4-T07c's columns on P4-T07c's table, and a nudge reader writing them would record an escalation as though somebody had acted on it. **Migration 0039 widens two check constraints and adds no column.** `agents.schedule` gains `daily` and `weekly`, which is the promise 0035 made in as many words. `nudges.subject_type` gains `member`, because the morning summary is about a person's day and the deduplication window is per (member, subject): a member id stored under `goal` would read as a goal to everything that joins on it. Renumbered from 0037 after Obed took 0037 and 0038 the same day. **A real duplicate was written and then removed, and it is worth recording.** The first draft added `daily_digest` and `daily_digest_hour` to `workspace_members` plus two settings-registry entries. `notification_settings.daily_summary` and `daily_summary_time` have existed since P2-T06, default to on at 08:00 in the member's own timezone, and are exactly what TECHNICAL-PLAN §4.14 specifies. The reader now left joins that table and falls back to `DEFAULT_DAILY_SUMMARY_TIME`, because the row is created lazily and most members have none. Two homes for one preference is a preference nobody owns. Found by grepping DATABASE.md rather than the schema, which is the lesson: a table can exist and still be invisible to a grep for the wrong word. **Five countdown rules went to `packages/method/src/countdown.ts`, pure.** Planning-open lead, the publication milestones, review preparation, day one, and the unscored close, each taking a §11 parameter and a day count. Same split P3-T06 made for the ladder: the scheduler counts days in the workspace calendar and the method package decides which trigger that day count earns. **No new §11 parameter was needed**, because all three cycle values were already in the registry from P3-T02. Dates are compared as local calendar days, never as subtracted milliseconds, because a cycle starting on 1 April starts on that date in the workspace timezone. **`session.missed` deliberately carries no threshold.** §6.4 says "1 day after missed session" and §11 has no parameter for it. Rather than invent one, the condition is the plain fact that the scheduled day passed with the session never opened, and the daily run is what turns that into a message a day later. A session that is running, closed or deliberately skipped is silent: telling a coordinator they missed a session they chose to skip is how a product teaches people to switch it off. **What is not built, each with its reason.** `cycle.phase_blocked` fires on "phase conditions unmet as the window closes", which is a question about gate state rather than about a date; `publishGates` already answers it on the publish path, and evaluating it per cycle per day needs a full workflow snapshot, which belongs to the Coach at P4-T06a. `streak.at_risk` and `commitment.due` need the streak and commitment tables, which are P4-T08's. `session.due_soon` and `cycle.starts` reach the facilitator and the sponsor rather than "the space" and "everyone", because a nudge row needs a member and the broadcast is the digest's job at P4-T08. **And nothing executes any of the four crons**: `registerAgentSchedules` now declares all four through the jobs port and this repository still has no relay host, the gap five files and PHASE-4-SPLIT already record. All four cadences are reachable through `agents.runChampion`, which is what the tests drive and what an administrator can call. **All four crons check hourly, and none of them is misnamed for it.** Every one fires on a local moment: 08:00 in each member's own timezone, a session at the hour it was booked, a countdown on a date in the workspace calendar. A workspace across three continents has no single hour at which "daily" happens. What makes them daily is the reader plus the deduplication window, the same mechanism that lets the hourly queue chase a check-in without sending twenty-four reminders. Blockers in those tests are opened through P4-T07c's own `sessions.createBlocker` action across the real session flow, and nothing is backdated: each run is asked about a `now` further along the clock. | P4-T05c-a | The proposal path, and the recovery proposal | done | agung | 2026-08-20 | **A proposal is a deferred action call and nothing more.** `proposed_changes` already stored an action registry key and a payload, and `proposals.bulkApply` already called that action with the deciding member as the actor. The new action opens the draft and publishes it in one Operation, as the applying member, and the publication itself is `publishCheckInInTx`, the same function the composer calls. Nothing about the snapshot, the value history, the cadence advance or P3-T08's reviewer of record is re-implemented: a second publish path that forgot the reviewer stamp would leave the review inbox with an obligation it could not attribute, and a test asserts the stamp. `startDraftInTx` was extracted out of `goals.startCheckIn` in the same change so one implementation opens a draft. **Migration 0040 adds two columns and one partial index.** `proposed_changes.ai_generated` because `run_id` already answers "did an agent raise this" and a reviewer needs the different question, "did a model choose these words". §6.5's recovery draft is a template that works with the provider off, so not every agent proposal is AI-generated and describing one as though it were would overstate what the product did. `nudges.proposal_id` because without it a nudge and its proposal are two rows a reader correlates by hope; `on delete set null` rather than cascade, because deleting a proposal must not delete the record that the product spoke. **The recovery proposal's payload carries ids, not drafted text.** `kpis.launchRecovery` already calls §6.5's drafter, so the objective and its key results are produced when a human applies it, from the KPI tree as it stands then. A payload carrying drafted titles would go stale the moment somebody added a driver under the KPI. **One pending proposal per subject per action, and that is a separate guard from the nudge deduplication window.** A run happens every hour and the condition holds until somebody acts, so an unguarded insert would grow the review queue by one row an hour for a KPI nobody has got to yet. A proposal has no `scheduled_for` to compare, so it needed its own check. Today's nudge links to the proposal already waiting rather than skipping, so it still points at something the recipient can act on. Only `pending` blocks a new one: a dismissal was a decision about that proposal, and a KPI still unhealthy a week later is entitled to be offered again. **A suppressed nudge still gets its proposal.** The suppression decided the *message* was noise, not that the change was unwanted. Same rule as "a snooze never hides a review-inbox obligation", from the other side. **The run row is now written before the work rather than after it.** A proposal's `run_id` is not null, so a run inserting its own row last had nothing for a proposal to point at. It is also the more honest shape: a run that crashes now leaves a `running` row saying something started and did not finish, where before it left nothing and read as a run that never happened. **`nudges.run` proposes nothing, deliberately.** It is the hourly queue an administrator can call by hand, not an agent run, and it passes no run id rather than inventing a run row to look as though it proposed something. **A test assumption of mine was wrong and the test now says what is true.** It asserted that a member holding only `view` is refused. This repository's write-access floor is `edit` for every active member through P3-T01's `workspace_standard` binding, recorded as a deliberate and reversible decision on the P3-T16 row, so such a member does not exist for goals today. The refusal that is real is a suspended member, and the test now asserts that `goals.publishDraftedCheckIn` refuses them with the *same message* `goals.startCheckIn` does, which is the drift worth guarding against. **Verified:** `packages/core` full suite against a real database, including `champion-proposals.test.ts` **12/12** covering every line of the task's test plan plus the inbox read and the two access cases; `packages/method` 236/236; `packages/agents` 16/16; `packages/ui` 141/141; `apps/web` 23/23; `packages/test-support` 71/71; `packages/db` 105/109, the four failures the PgBouncer spike that needs Docker. typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries, dead-code, both database linters and `pnpm method:check`. Migration 0040 applied to the development database. **Not verified:** the end-to-end suite, which needs the Docker database stack this machine does not have, and no browser check, because this task ships no screen. The review-inbox surface that renders the proposal is not built here either: `nudges.list` returns it and no component reads it yet. That is a follow-up on P4-T15's surfaces, recorded rather than half-drawn. **Nothing AI is in this row.** P4-T05c-b is the drafting, and it is blocked on a provider key. |
| P4-T05c-b | AI drafting inside the proposal | done | agung | 2026-08-21 | **The drafter is an interface `packages/core` declares and the application supplies.** Drafting needs a model, a model needs a driver, and every driver lives in `packages/adapters`, which core may not import. So `AgentDrafter` sits beside the key ring on `ActionCallContext`: core states the shape, `packages/agents` implements it against a provider, and `apps/web` builds the finished thing. **Absent is the normal case and means the provider is off**, which is what makes every deterministic assertion in the other suites true rather than merely claimed. **Nothing throws.** A provider that is off, a budget that is spent, a network that is down or output the schema refused twice all arrive as null, and the run carries on with the deterministic answer. A model having a bad minute must not take the rhythm with it, and the rhythm is the part that has to work. What is never done is guessing: `extractStructured` validates with one repair attempt and then fails, so a failure means no draft rather than an unvalidated one. A test drives the throwing case and asserts the run still completes. **A model may write words; it may not invent numbers.** The drafted proposal carries `values: []`, so whoever applies it fills the key result values in the composer where the units are in front of them. Same rule on the recovery side: only the objective's title can come from a model, and the key results, baselines, targets and §6.5's breadth-first walk stay the method's. **One draft per goal however far the ladder reaches.** Six days overdue brings in the reviewer beside the champion, and they are looking at one check-in; only the champion's nudge carries it, because only they can publish it. **A title identical to the template is not a refinement, and the first live run proved it matters.** Asked to improve on §6.5's sentence, OpenRouter handed the same sentence back. Returning it would have marked the proposal AI-generated for words no model chose, so an unchanged title is now treated as no title at all. **The dead-code gate caught the real gap.** The drafter was written and reachable from nothing: an implementation with no host is a capability the product does not have. `/admin/agents` now has a run control, which is also the honest answer to the standing problem that nothing executes the four declared crons. It names the clock rather than saying "Run", and it says which of the two products this workspace is running: **Drafting on** when a provider is configured, **Deterministic only** when not, rather than offering drafting that cannot happen. **Cost is metered per provider call, not per draft**, because `extractStructured` may make a second one to repair invalid output and that call is paid for too. `onUsage` was added to it for this. The cap is read from §4.14 on the workspace rather than from a constant, so a workspace that lowered it stops the drafter mid-run instead of only being refused at the door. **Verified against the live provider, not only a stand-in.** OpenRouter drafted a check-in that used only the numbers it was given (42%, 9%, three days overdue) and invented none, returned `caution` at 0.55 confidence, and the run metered $0.003210. The suite itself uses a stand-in, deliberately: it can prove refusing, throwing and running out of budget, which a real model reaches only occasionally. **Verified:** `packages/core/test/champion-drafting.test.ts` **11/11** against a real database, the full `packages/core` suite, and the **end-to-end suite 38/38 run locally**, including a new test that clicks the run control and proves the log stops saying it is empty. typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 415 files, dead-code, both database linters and `pnpm method:check`. **Not done here:** no migration was needed, and the four crons still have nothing executing them, which is why the button exists.

A test asserts the run's cost is zero and that it writes no proposal. The implementation is shared all the way down: one `runDueNudgesInTx`, one suppression decision, one row writer, so a quality nudge cannot end up with its own idea of quiet hours. **Its dependency line was wrong and is corrected.** The card read P4-T05c-b, which is blocked on a provider credential. Nothing in this row touches the drafter or any provider, so the real dependency is P4-T05c-a's proposal path. Confirmed with Agung before starting, and the old line would have stalled the whole agents lane behind one key. **Five existing tests had to change, and each one is the same class P4-T05a hit**: a second seeded agent is a third member and a third access group. | P4-T06b-a | Divergence findings, and the shared reconciler | done | agung | 2026-08-21 | **METHOD.md §5.3's four types all need a provider, and divergence does not.** §5.3 is the *semantic* review: relink, dependency, conflict and gap are judgements the Coach makes by reading content. Divergence is arithmetic over a stored status and stored numbers, and AI-NATIVE-PLAN §6.1's own matrix marks it deterministic. So P4-T06b split on the same provider boundary as P4-T05c, and this half landed while the credential was still outstanding. The original acceptance criterion is P4-T06b-b's, because a conflict finding needs the provider. **No new §11 parameter, deliberately.** METHOD.md defines no divergence rule and §11 carries no threshold for one, so inventing either would be changing practice. What §11 already has is enough: §3.7's progress signal boundaries and §3.2's confidence bands. Divergence is not a new judgement, it is the one a reader already makes when §3.7 says the signal is "shown beside health and never instead of it". **Two of §6.1's three cases, and the third is recorded rather than guessed.** Case 1 is `on_track` beside a red progress signal, case 2 is `on_track` beside average confidence below the low band or `caution`/`off_track` beside confidence in the high band. The third, "a forecast that misses while the champion says caution", is not built: as written it is ambiguous (a pessimistic forecast beside a cautious champion is agreement, not divergence) and it rests on the §3.6 forecast, whose sparse-data behaviour is an open practice decision already on the PHASE-4-SPLIT list. Building on a number that may change would mean sending findings that later stop being true. Confirmed with Agung before leaving it out. **Case 1 reads §3.7's signal rather than a window of days.** §6.1's words are "key results have not moved", which sounds like it needs a staleness window; the signal already answers "have the numbers moved enough", and a second definition measured in days would be a threshold nobody could see or change. **Both cases are one-directional except one, and that asymmetry is the point.** A champion reporting `caution` while the numbers look green is being careful, and telling somebody their caution is wrong is how a product teaches people to report green. The exception is high confidence beside a cautious status: that is not care, it is the champion and their own team having said different things, and one of them has not been told. **The reconciler is now shared, and that was the most valuable part of this task.** `recomputeAlignment` held four promises to a facilitator inline: upsert by identity, a dismissal survives, a cleared condition soft-deletes, and the slice is (scope, source) rather than scope alone. Those are promises, not bookkeeping, and a second copy of them would drift. `reconcileFindingsInTx` is the one implementation, and the slice gained `kind` as well, because a divergence sweep must not soft-delete a structural finding for being absent from its list. A test asserts the engine's rows survive a Coach sweep untouched. **Migration 0042 adds `divergence` to the finding kinds rather than filing it under `gap`.** Filing it under `gap` was cheaper and is refused: §5.3's four types are canon, its `gap` means "something is missing or weak", and a reader filtering by kind would find a deterministic arithmetic finding sitting among semantic judgements. Adding the value changes no practice: §5.3 still names exactly four semantic types. **One finding per goal, the most severe.** Both cases say the same thing to a facilitator, and the finding identity is (rule key, subject, target), so two on one goal would collide on one identity and the second would overwrite the first. Rather than widen the identity, the sweep raises the one that matters: two rows about one disagreement would need two dismissals for one decision. **Two mistakes of mine worth recording.** The first draft invented a `GoalHealth` value (`at_risk`) that does not exist; the canon's word is `caution` and the type checker refused it before any test ran. And a test asserting the finding clears flipped `health` to `off_track`, which cleared case 1 and immediately tripped case 2. The code was right and the fixture was wrong; the test now raises progress into the green band instead, and says so in a comment. **Verified:** `packages/method/test/divergence.test.ts` **15/15** pure, `packages/core/test/divergence.test.ts` **11/11** against a real database covering every line of the task's test plan that does not need a provider, plus the returning-condition case and the proof that a Coach sweep leaves the engine's findings alone. Full `packages/core` suite, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 411 files, dead-code, both database linters and `pnpm method:check`. Migration 0042 applied to the development database. **Not verified:** the end-to-end suite, which needs the Docker database stack this machine does not have, and no browser check, because this ships no screen. The surface that lists findings is P4-T06c's. **Not built here:** the one-click apply, because only the semantic sweep produces a finding whose fix is mechanical, and a relink finding cannot exist until P4-T06b-b. |
| P4-T06b-b | The nightly semantic sweep | done | agung | 2026-08-21 | **A model is never shown an identifier and never asked for one.** The goals go out as a list and findings come back as **indices** into that list, so a model cannot name a goal in another workspace, cannot invent a uuid, and an index out of range is dropped rather than resolved. Everything a model returns is untrusted input, and the cheapest way to keep it harmless is to give it nothing dangerous to say. Tests drive the out-of-range case and the goal-conflicts-with-itself case, because that is how untrusted output actually behaves. **No provider is not the same as "the model read them and found nothing".** The first leaves yesterday's findings exactly where they are; only the second retires them. A workspace that turns AI off must not watch its findings vanish, so `null` from the reviewer is treated as having no provider and an empty array is treated as a real answer. A test asserts both. **One row names both goals, and that is what makes the acceptance criterion true by construction.** A conflict across two spaces is a single finding carrying subject and target, so "dismissing it on one side dismisses it everywhere" needs no bookkeeping. It did need one change: `alignment.dismissFinding` checked access on the subject alone, which would have let one champion clear it and refused the other, which is the same disagreement the finding is about. Either end may now decide it. **Only `relink` has a one-click apply, and §5.3's own wording is why.** It offers the click "where the fix is mechanical (relink, dependency)", and only relink is mechanical here: re-parenting is one column. A dependency needs a provider named and a direction chosen, which is a decision rather than a click; a conflict or a gap has no fix at all, only a conversation. `alignment.applyFinding` refuses the other three by name. **The re-parent goes through `goals.update`, outside this transaction.** A relink changes the tree, and the tree drives the §5.2 score, the level-skip finding and the silo finding. Writing the column here would skip the recompute that keeps all three true. Same call and same reason as `proposals.bulkApply`. A test asserts both audit rows exist. **Verified against the live model, and it found what it should.** Given four goals, OpenRouter returned exactly the two conflicts planted in them, and they were two different §5.3 conflicts rather than one repeated: two goals measuring the same ARR line (double-counting) and a goal cutting infrastructure spend beside one needing significant new compute (pulling in opposite directions). Positional throughout, no invented identifier, $0.003852. **Boundary gate caught a real read.** The sweep resolves space **names** to describe a goal to a model, which is a protected table outside the getter. Marked with the same reason the register card carries: names only, every human member already holds `view` on every space, and a sweep has no acting member to scope by. **Verified:** `packages/core/test/semantic-sweep.test.ts` **9/9** against a real database, covering all three test-plan lines and the acceptance criterion, the full `packages/core` suite, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 416 files, dead-code, both database linters and `pnpm method:check`. No migration was needed: `alignment_findings` has carried the four kinds since P3-T09. **Not done here:** no surface lists the findings yet. `alignment.read` returns them and no component renders relink, conflict or gap, which is P4-T06c's alignment studio review tab. The sweep runs on the Coach's own run rather than nightly, because nothing executes the crons. |

| P4-T06c | The rewrite assist and the coach surfaces | done | agung | 2026-08-21 | **A model saying it fixed the rule is not the product saying so.** Asked to fix KR-2, a model will report KR-2 fixed whatever it wrote. `goals.rewriteKeyResult` therefore re-runs §4 over the suggestion and reports `nowPassing` and `fixesTheRule` from the catalogue's own verdict, never from the model's claim. When §4 disagrees the suggestion is still returned and the strip says plainly that it does not satisfy the rule, because a writer may want the sentence anyway and what the product will not do is lie about it. The test that matters is the dishonest one: a stand-in returning "Substantially improve the onboarding experience" for KR-2, which is no more measurable than the original. **The goal page reads stored verdicts rather than evaluating again.** P4-T02a writes the score and the flags inside the transaction that changed the text, so the strip shows what the product committed to. A second evaluation here could put two different answers about one goal on one screen. **"Previewing before applying" needs an apply, so one was built.** The first version showed a preview and told the reader to copy it by hand, which is not what the deliverable says. `applyRewrite` calls `goals.updateKeyResult`, the ordinary edit path, so P4-T02a re-evaluates in the same transaction and the flag the strip is showing clears itself. Nothing in this task writes a quality flag of its own, and a test asserts the flag is gone after the apply, which is the test that fails if something ever starts to. **Two deviations from the agent design document, both corrected in it in this change.** §1.2's matrix said the rewrite assist is *hidden* with the provider off; this task's test plan says the surfaces explain rather than disappear silently, and the plan is the higher authority. A control that vanishes leaves a writer unable to tell whether the product has one, and unable to tell whether the rules still apply. §1.4 said every rewrite is a `proposed_changes` envelope; an assist a champion asks for in their own session and applies themselves is not an agent acting unasked, and a proposal row would record somebody proposing a change to themselves. Propose-by-default governs agent initiative. What protects this path is the preview and the champion's own identity in the audit row. **The studio's review tab offers Re-parent only on a relink.** §5.3 offers the click "where the fix is mechanical", `alignment.applyFinding` refuses the other three kinds by name, and a button that produced a refusal would be a worse answer than no button. Dismiss is on every finding, because either end of a cross-space conflict may decide it since P4-T06b-b. **The tab's empty state was lying.** It said semantic findings "arrive with the Coach at P4-T03", which was true when P3-T10 wrote it and stopped being true two tasks ago. It now says what is actually missing: a provider. **Every `AgentDrafter` method is optional now.** Adding a third capability broke every test stub that implemented the first two, which is the signal that the interface was wrong rather than the stubs: a host may draft check-ins and not rewrite. A missing capability reads the same as one that declined, and both are null. **The assist button is gated on `edit`, not on the provider alone.** `goals.rewriteKeyResult` needs `edit`, so offering it to a reader would produce a refusal where the honest answer is that the goal is not theirs to change. **`goals.read` and `goals.list` now carry the verdicts.** `quality` on the objective and `qualityFlags` on each key result, so a surface does not re-derive what is already stored. No migration was needed. **A harness defect found while verifying this, recorded rather than fixed.** Two full `packages/core` runs failed, 111 tests and then 123, with no assertion failures at all: every one was Postgres `57P01` from the harness's own `drop database ... with (force)` deleting a database a recycled Vitest fork was still reading. Caught live in `pg_stat_activity`; one such drop held for 18 seconds. Capped at four workers the same suite passes whole and runs faster. Fixing it changes the harness every package's tests run through, so it needs its own row; the diagnosis, the workaround and the intended fix are in PHASE-4-SPLIT.md and CLAUDE.md now, because a hundred red tests that mean nothing will cost the next person an hour. **Not done here:** the assist writes no provenance on the applied value, which AI-NATIVE-PLAN §2 asks for and P4-T15 owns along with the feature switch; and the studio's Re-parent has no undo beyond re-parenting again by hand. |
