| P4-T09 | Monthly review and decision log | done | agung | 2026-08-21 | **TECHNICAL-PLAN §4.7 already specified both tables, and the shape I wrote first was wrong in two ways.** The migration was rewritten before it was committed, and the two differences are the most useful part of this row. **A trend belongs to a month, not to a meeting.** My first `session_trends` keyed on the session. A space that reschedules and ends up holding two reviews in one March would then store two March opinions per objective with no way to say which one the room meant, and the quarterly review would have to join sessions to read three months of trend. `objective_trends` is keyed on `(goal_id, month)`, as the plan says, and a test drives the second-review-in-one-month case from both sides: same month reads the first review's answer back and corrects it, a fortnight that crosses into the next month starts empty. That branch exists because a test that only holds on most days of the year is a test that fails on the others. **A decision carries its own `cycle_id`.** Deriving it by joining through the goal looks equivalent and is not: `goals.moveToCycle` is a real action, so a goal moved into the next quarter would drag every past decision with it and a Q1 decision would start reading as a Q2 one. The test for this **failed first**, because the query really was joining through the goal, which is the only reason to believe the column earns its place. **The trend is never pre-filled, and Agung confirmed that before it was built.** §3.7's progress signal sits beside each objective as evidence and no button starts selected. §7.5 records a trend as a judgement, and a judgement that arrives pre-answered is a judgement most rooms stop making. A test asserts the recorded trend and the computed signal are allowed to disagree: red numbers with an improving trend is a legitimate thing for a room to conclude, and the product records what they said. **Two of §7.5's four items are not tables.** The dependency and risk log is a read of P3-T09's alignment register, because a second copy would give a facilitator two answers about one dependency; the screen also names the unconfirmed-and-unowned case, which is what holds publish gate 4 red (§5.4). Resource or priority shifts are one free-text note, so they are a column on the session, and deliberately not a key inside `okr_sessions.notes`: that column holds the facilitator's private per-stage notes and P4-T10a keeps them private, so a shared record living there is one refactor away from being published. **A decision names something or it is refused**, at the boundary and again in a table constraint, so the caller gets a sentence rather than a database error. §7.5's wording is the reason: a decision with no subject is a meeting note, and the log is not a notepad. When a key result is named, the goal is stored alongside it, so the goal page finds the decision with one index rather than a join, and the acceptance criterion is true by construction. A test drives exactly that path, because a query matching only `goal_id` would pass every other test in the file and fail the one that matters. **A monthly review has no stages, and the screen had to be taught that.** The session screen computed its controls from `WEEKLY_STAGE_KEYS`, so a monthly session showed the facilitator "Continue to next step", which cannot work: `stageKey` is null and there is no next stage. Closing is the only move and now the only button. **Closed objectives are left out of the trend list.** A review asks where the work is going, and a finished objective is not going anywhere. Found by the end-to-end run rather than by reading. **A member simply outside the space is refused nothing here**, and the tests say so rather than pretending otherwise. P3-T01's `workspace_standard` binding gives every active member `edit` across the workspace, recorded on the P3-T16 row, so the refusal that is real is a suspended member and that is what the access tests drive. Same correction as P4-T05c-a, for the same reason. **A timezone bug of my own, caught before it was committed.** Both dates were read with `toISOString().slice(0, 10)`, which answers in UTC: a review held at six in the morning in Jakarta records on the previous day, and on the first of a month it lands in the wrong month, which is precisely the column the trend is keyed on. `localDateIn` is the shared answer the cycle engine already uses and the only thing that knows what the offset was on that date, so both the trend's month and the decision's date go through the workspace timezone now. The same UTC pattern is in P4-T08's `week_start` and `last_session_week` and is left alone: it changes how a streak is counted, which is not this task's to move. Recorded as a follow-up. **Two defects the end-to-end run found that reading did not.** The objective list had no `ORDER BY`, so the panel reshuffled between loads and a facilitator working down the screen would lose their place; and closed objectives were being asked for a trend, when a review asks where the work is going and a finished objective is not going anywhere. Both fixed, the second with its own test. **Verified:** `packages/core/test/monthly-review.test.ts` **16/16** against a real database, covering the deliverables, the acceptance criterion, both plan deviations, the closed objective and the two access cases. Full `packages/core` suite **61/61 files and 1201/1201 tests**, end-to-end **40/40** three times in a row. typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 420 files, dead-code, both database linters and `pnpm method:check`. Migration 0043 applied to the development database. **The new end-to-end test failed once out of five runs on code that passed the other four, and I could not explain it.** Two earlier failures of the same assertion were mine and are explained: a bare `getByText(NAME)` matched five elements on the goal page, and the objective list had no `ORDER BY` so the decision landed on whichever objective sorted first while the test then opened a different one. The remaining one is not explained. What was ruled out with evidence: the row was in the database with the right `goal_id`, tenant and no `deleted_at`; the application role could read it under `app.workspace_id`; both new tables had their grants; the card and the action were in the built server; the page rendered every other card. The read returned an empty list at render time and did so once. **One product change came out of chasing it**, and it is right whatever the cause: `sessions.recordDecision` now returns the goal it landed on, and the web action revalidates that concrete path instead of the `/goals/[id]` route pattern, which depended on matching Next's own route key. The test keeps a diagnostic that dumps the stored rows, the regions and the headings on failure, so the next occurrence names the cause rather than starting another round of theories. Three consecutive full runs pass since that change, which is evidence and not proof: the failure was one in five before it. **My own three-run flake measurement measured nothing**, and that is worth recording too: the diagnostic I added had a literal newline inside a string, so the spec would not parse and all three runs failed at compile time. The error output echoed the source lines and I read them as results. A `NEWLINE` constant now, the same fix and the same reason as `packages/agents/src/drafter.ts`. **The harness defect recorded at P4-T06c bit twice more here**, in its second form: `database "openokr_test_core_wN" does not exist` rather than `57P01`, same root cause, 17 tests and no assertions. Four workers passed once and then failed; two workers passed whole. PHASE-4-SPLIT.md and CLAUDE.md now name both forms and say two. **Documents updated in the same change:** TECHNICAL-PLAN §4.7 gains `shifts` on the session row, §7.2 marks `objective_trends` and `decisions` as having no legacy source with the reason, DATABASE.md matches, and the session design document §3.2 now records both deviations rather than leaving them in a commit message. **Not done here:** the missing test plan line on the card was written in the same change, because the next reader should meet the corrected card rather than the original omission; no reference mockup exists for S-23, so the screen follows the specification alone; nothing schedules a monthly review, which is the same missing scheduler every other cadence waits on; and a decision cannot be edited or withdrawn once recorded, which §7.5 does not ask for and a later task can add if a real room needs it. |

The write path is complete and correct; no event reaches a browser, because nothing drains the outbox. Agung chose this over publishing directly from the server action, which would have made the test green by going around the outbox rule. The test is `test.fixme` rather than deleted or left passing, so the claim stays visible and unmistakably unproven, and the quarterly spec reloads the second client and says why. **A real leak, closed here.** `sessions.read` returned the whole `notes` map to every caller from P4-T07a onward. Section 8.1 calls those notes private and nothing enforced it. `toOutput` now takes the reading member and hands the map to the facilitator alone; the activity payload names the stage and never the note, because an activity row is read by everybody who can see the space. Nothing had written a note in between, so nothing leaked, and the shape was wrong for four tasks. **The stage machine is one list per ritual behind one lookup.** `stageKeysFor` replaces the weekly-only branches, because the weekly and quarterly machines are the same walk over two lists and a monthly review has no stages at all. The screen had the same bug the monthly review found last task: `isOnLastStage` was computed from `WEEKLY_STAGE_KEYS`, so a quarterly review's stage was never found in it, the index stayed at -1, and the facilitator was offered "Continue to next step" on stage eleven where the action throws. **`REVIEW_STAGE_KEYS` is its own list, not slugs of the titles.** It is what `stage_key`, `elapsed`, `notes` and `added_minutes` are keyed by, and a slug generated from canon text would change the moment somebody reworded a stage, orphaning every stored note. A test pairs the keys against `REVIEW_STAGES` position for position rather than trusting the order. **Migration 0044 adds `added_minutes`, keyed by stage like `elapsed`.** Section 11's `sessions.quarterlyStageMinutes` is the workspace's standing agenda, so a room that runs long on one day must not retune every future review. The rail marks an extended stage with an asterisk and says the workspace's agenda is unchanged. `elapsed` holds seconds because it is measured; this holds whole minutes because it is chosen. **Both new controls are the facilitator's by name, not by screen.** The write-access floor here is `edit` for every active member (P3-T16), so `ACCESS_LEVELS.edit` alone would hand the add-a-minute control and the private notes to the whole room. `requireFacilitator` refuses anybody else, and tests drive both refusals. **The timer counts past its budget rather than stopping.** Section 8.1 says going over is normal and visible. A readout that froze at zero would hide exactly the number a facilitator needs. It ticks in the browser from `stageStartedAt` and the stage's budget, so a client that reconnects lands on the right second without asking, and nothing about elapsed time is stored until the stage ends. **The note draft resets when the stage does, adjusted during render rather than in an effect.** An effect keyed on the note alone would not fire when two stages hold the same text, so unsaved typing from stage one would follow the facilitator into stage two and be written there. **Three tests in the end-to-end spec passed without proving anything**, all found here, and the class is recorded in PHASE-4-SPLIT.md: the decision assertion matched the textarea it had just been typed into and went green on the click; the live-sync assertion matched a title rendered at every stage; and the closing test's fixed count of two advances was correct only because the test before it advanced once, so marking that one `fixme` left it a stage short. **Two of my own tools failed silently while chasing this**, which is why the flake took five runs. A diagnostic with a literal newline in a string stopped the spec parsing, and three measurement runs failed at compile time while I read the echoed source lines as results. Then a Python edit script asserted after its first replacement and before its single write, so a later assertion failure discarded every edit and three more runs tested an unchanged file. Edits go through the Edit tool now, which fails loudly. **Verified:** `packages/core/test/quarterly-shell.test.ts` **12/12** against a real database, covering both test-plan lines, the eleven-stage walk, the refusal past the last stage, the elapsed record, both facilitator-only refusals, one note per stage, and that the weekly machine is untouched. `packages/method` **252/252** including the new key-pairing test. Full `packages/core` suite. End-to-end **40 passed, 1 skipped** three runs in a row, the skip being the `fixme`. typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 421 files, dead-code, both database linters and `pnpm method:check`. Migration 0044 applied to the development database. **Not done here:** the live half of the acceptance criterion is blocked on the relay host and recorded as such, not worked around; the centre panel of each stage names the task that fills it rather than rendering nothing or faking content; and the room pulse is P4-T10a-b, which is why `session_participants` is not in this row. |

| P4-T10b-b | Quarterly review: the reveal | in_review | agung | 2026-08-26 | **A contradiction inside one design table, and the decision that closed it.** p4-t00-session-design.md §4.3 said the objective score is hidden until the room reveals it and, two lines down, that the running cycle score updates live as key results are scored. Those cannot both hold: the cycle score is §3.4's plain average over key results and the objective score is §3.2's weighted average over the same rows, so on a review with one objective whose key results weigh the same, the two numbers are one number. A cycle score moving on every grade was the hidden figure with a different label on it. Agung decided on 2026-08-26 that the cycle score runs through the reveals, and the design document is corrected in this change rather than left to disagree with the code. It also makes the acceptance criterion literal: revealing is the thing that moves the average. **The hole this closes was real and it was in the action, not the screen.** `sessions.scoringStatus` returned `objective.score`, `cycleScore` and `verdict` to every caller with view. P4-T10b-a kept those off the grading screen and wrote an end-to-end assertion about their absence, so the screen was honest and the contract was not: a second surface, the public REST projection or the agent tool catalogue read what the room had not seen. The read now returns `score: null` and `revealed: false` until the reveal, and the screen has nothing left to keep back. **No migration.** `review_scores.revealed_at` shipped with 0046 at P4-T10b-a precisely so the reveal would be a write and not a schema change, and it is. **The reveal animates in CSS on purpose.** `packages/ui/src/styles/tokens.css` §2 collapses every CSS animation and transition to nothing under `prefers-reduced-motion`, so the existing `.animate-pop` is instant for a reader who asked for that and nothing here checks a media query. A JavaScript count-up would have counted straight through that override, which is the whole reason the note is in the code. **A redundant reveal answers with nought rather than refusing**, the shape `sessions.revealVotes` set at P3-T07, and enqueues no outbox row: there is no second reveal to push and the idempotency key would collide with the first. That collision is not theoretical, it failed the idempotence test before the guard existed. **Facilitator only, and the core suite is what proves it.** The write-access floor here is `edit` for every active member (P3-T16), so `edit` alone would let any participant pre-empt the room, the same reasoning §8.1's add-a-minute control needed. The end-to-end spec cannot show that: its second browser context reuses the first one's storage state, so both clients are the same signed-in facilitator and there is no participant there to refuse. An assertion written there would have passed for the wrong reason, so it is recorded in the spec as not covered and covered against a real second member in `packages/core`. **A P4-T10b-a end-to-end defect found by this task, fixed here.** That spec filled `getByLabel("Score")`, which matched the facilitator's private note first, because on this stage the note is labelled "Private note for Score the key results" and its panel renders above the grading panel. So the slider was never moved, "0.6" landed in the note, the grade stored as nought, and the next assertion, `getByText("0.6")`, found the note and reported success. Four assertions passed while nothing under test had happened. It surfaced the instant the reveal showed the honest 0.00. Both locators are scoped to the row now, and the row carries "0.6" twice so the chip is taken by position. **Still not live, and said plainly.** `session.scoresRevealed` is declared, enqueued as an outbox row and listened for by the client, and no relay drains the outbox, exactly as P4-T10a-a recorded for `session.stageChanged`. The write is one write and every client that re-reads gets the same answer; the end-to-end spec reloads the second client rather than claiming a rail that does not run. **Verified:** review-scoring 24/24 with ten new reveal tests, `packages/core` 64/64 files and 1250/1250 tests, end-to-end 41 passed and 1 skipped on three of five runs, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 426 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check and the licence gate. **`e2e/sessions.spec.ts:242` failed two of those five runs and it is not this task's:** the snapshot is the error boundary, "We could not load your workspace", so `requireWorkspace()` threw during a server render rather than a locator racing a refresh. P4-T10b-a called it a known flake and it is worse than that word suggests at two in five. The shape worth chasing is the SSE route holding a LISTEN connection per client while the page calls `router.refresh()` on every event, which is a pool question rather than a test question. Recorded rather than patched here, and it wants its own row. |
| P4-T10c | Quarterly review: narratives and recognition | in_review | agung | 2026-08-26 | **Migration 0047: `review_narratives`, `kudos` and `okr_sessions.mic_goal_id`.** TECHNICAL-PLAN §4 already specified both tables, so this is mostly building what was written down, with three shapes settled here. **The mic is a column, not a row per turn.** Exactly one objective holds it, which is the property the stage exists to enforce, and a single pointer is the only shape that cannot represent two holders. Every client agrees who is speaking because there is one write and they all read it. The panel keeps no holder of its own, because a second copy of that answer is a second answer. **The pass is what marks an objective spoken for**, rather than a second control beside it. In a real room the facilitator says thank you and turns to the next owner, and a separate tick is a second thing to forget. That makes putting the mic down a real act: nothing takes the mic after the last owner, so without a null pass that owner is never marked. A mic that comes back to an objective does not un-speak it, because `spoken_at` is stamped once. **The narrative body is nullable and usually null**, which is a shape the plan row did not carry and now does. §8.1 gives the stage nine minutes of talking rather than writing, so a row appears when the mic moves on with no body and no author. An empty document would put "somebody wrote nothing" in the same shape as "nobody wrote", and the minutes at P4-T12 have to tell those apart. Two check constraints hold it: a body always has its version, and an author only exists with a body, so the facilitator who marked a turn over cannot be recorded as the author of a story they never wrote. **One recorded deviation from TECHNICAL-PLAN §4, and it needs Agung's word before this row is accepted:** that row said `kudos.session_id?` and this ships it not null. REQUIREMENTS §105 puts kudos inside the quarterly session and nowhere else, so every v1 writer sets it and a nullable column would be one nothing can produce and nothing can read, which is the `notifications.nudge_id` shape P4-T04a complained about. Relaxing it later is a single forward migration dropping the constraint, with no rolling-upgrade hazard, so the invariant is the cheap direction to be wrong in. The plan row is corrected in this change with that reasoning on it. **Recognition aggregates nothing.** Two entries naming the same person are two things they did, and `kudos` is unique on nothing so the second cannot overwrite the first. Who may be named comes from the read rather than the screen: `sessions.recognition` returns every active human member except the reader, which is the same decision `sessions.giveKudos` enforces, and the seeded Coach and Champion are excluded because recognition names a person's effort rather than a scheduler's. **Two defects found on the way, neither belonging to this task.** The stage rail is a landmark that lists every stage by name, so an end-to-end locator filtering a region on the words "Objective narratives" matched the rail and asserted against the agenda rather than the panel; the P4-T10a-b room-pulse assertions are scoped the same wide way and still pass, which is worth knowing before trusting them. And `Card` is a plain div with no role, so a panel wrapped in one is unreachable by name: both new panels carry `role="region"` with their heading as the accessible name, which is what a screen reader needs to skip to them and the only unambiguous target for anything else. **The quarterly placeholder card stopped lying.** It rendered the running stage's title as a second level-two heading beside the real panel's, and told the reader the panel "arrives with its task" for stages that now have one. It reads "This stage" when a panel is present, and `page.tsx` decides that because `page.tsx` is what renders the panels: a list of built stages kept inside the rail component is a second place to forget. **Still not live**, the same position P4-T10a-a and P4-T10b-b recorded: `session.micPassed` is enqueued as an outbox row and no relay drains it, so the end-to-end spec reloads the second client rather than claiming a rail that does not run. **The end-to-end spec inserts a second human for stage four and removes it again**, because this workspace has one human member plus two seeded agents and recognition names somebody else. Recorded rather than left implicit: `registration-to-dashboard.spec.ts` counts members and has already been broken once by a member it did not expect. **Verified:** review-narratives 20/20 and review-scoring 26/26, `packages/core` in full, end-to-end 41 passed and 1 skipped, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries, dead-code, both database linters, method:check and the licence gate. |
| P4-T11a | Quarterly review: the retros | in_review | agung | 2026-08-26 | **Migration 0048: `retro_notes`, `retro_votes` and `management_answers`, plus one new METHOD.md §11 parameter.** TECHNICAL-PLAN §4 already specified all three tables. **Two decisions from Agung on 2026-08-26, both asked before code and both recorded.** First, the dot cap is `Retro dots per member` in METHOD.md §11 with a canon default of 3. §11 states it plainly, that every numeric value the product enforces is a parameter in that registry and a value not in the registry is not a setting, so the alternative homes were closed by the document rather than by preference; three because the stage has seven minutes and more turns the vote into a full ranking. It lands in `rhythm_settings` like every other §11 parameter, so it is already tunable from the rhythm card on S-36 with no new surface. Second, the management retro is read by a space's managers and its coordinator. §8.7 says leadership answers out loud, plural, those are the leadership roles this product has, and the write-access floor here is `edit` for every active member (P3-T16), so `edit` alone would have shown it to the whole room. **One deliverable deliberately not built, and Agung chose that too.** The design doc's §4.6 line said "configurable starting prompts" and no document carries the prompt text: METHOD.md has none and §4.14 has no home for a list like it. Writing them would be inventing practice. The two canon columns ship without chips and the gap is recorded in the design document with what reopening it would take. **Two caps, holding different things.** One dot per member per note, held by a unique index. `Retro dots per member` in total across notes, held by the action because it counts across rows no index can see. A second cast on the same note withdraws the first rather than erroring: a room pressing a toggle twice should not meet a refusal, and spending two dots on one note is how three dots become one loud opinion when §8.1's vote is about spread. Removing a note removes the dots spent on it, because a dot spent on something that no longer exists is a dot its owner cannot get back and the cap would silently shrink for the rest of the stage. **`retro_notes.votes` is denormalised and held honest by a test rather than by hope.** TECHNICAL-PLAN §4 specifies the column and the board sorts on it, so it stays, recounted from the rows and rewritten in the same transaction as every vote. A cached total is usually the wrong idea, so the test asserts it equals the count behind it. **Anonymity is per note, not per session**, because §8.1 asks for silent writing and one thing in a retro is usually harder to say than the rest. The board shows "anonymous" rather than an empty author line, so a reader can tell a choice from an absence. An anonymous note has no author to take it back, which is why the facilitator may remove any note and the room may only remove its own. **The management retro's refusal is not-found, not forbidden**, because telling somebody it exists and is closed to them is itself a disclosure about the room. The page catches it and renders one line saying the stage is leadership's, which discloses nothing the stage rail does not already show everybody. A review with no space falls back to workspace administration, there being no space roles to read. **One accessibility fix the end-to-end suite forced and the screen needed anyway.** Both retro columns carry a note field and a "without my name" checkbox, so unscoped those are two identical targets: a screen reader reads the same label twice. Each column is a `fieldset` named by its heading now, which is what the a11y lint asked for when `role="group"` was tried first. **Verified:** review-retros 18/18, `packages/core` in full, end-to-end 41 passed and 1 skipped, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 433 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check at 55 thresholds and the licence gate. |
| P4-T11b | Root cause and the process-health survey | in_review | agung | 2026-08-26 | **Migration 0049: `root_causes`, `process_health_responses` and `okr_sessions.process_health_salt`. METHOD.md §8.4's taxonomy was missing from `packages/method` and is added here.** §11 lists the root-cause taxonomy among the structures a workspace cannot change and CLAUDE.md says every taxonomy is implemented in that package, so its absence was a gap between the document and the code rather than a decision. `ROOT_CAUSES` is now read back out of METHOD.md by two tests, word for word and in the document's order, because a set comparison would pass on a shuffled list and the picker shows the order. **The stage's list comes from `review_scores`, not `key_results.score`.** Grades do not land on the key results until the session closes (P4-T10b-a) and stage seven runs before that, so reading the key result would show an empty list in every live review. Strictly below the threshold: §8.4 says "below 0.7", so a key result that scored exactly it met it, and asking a room to explain a result it did not miss is how a stage loses its credibility. A test holds that boundary. **Anonymity, stated precisely rather than claimed broadly.** `process_health_responses` carries a salted hash where a member id would go, and the salt lives on the session, written when the first response arrives. A hash of the member id alone would be the same string in every review, so somebody holding the table could follow one unnamed person's answers across quarters without learning their name; the per-review salt breaks that. It is a salt rather than an HMAC on the instance root key because a rotation would leave every stored hash unmatchable and silently break the one-response rule mid-review. **What it does not buy is anonymity against somebody holding both the database and the member list**, because a room is small enough to enumerate and no scheme that lets this application recount a member's response could be. The test asserts what is actually guaranteed: the column list has no member id, the hashes are not the member id, and no read hands one back. **The activity payloads matter more here than anywhere else in the phase.** An activity row carries its actor, so a payload with the survey scores in it would attribute an anonymous answer in the one place everybody in the space can read. The root-cause payload carries no cause number either, because §8.4 says look for the system rather than the person and a space-wide entry reading "capacity or resourcing" against a named key result invites the opposite. The audit row records that somebody answered and never what they said. **Two gates caught helpers they could not prove, and both were right to.** The boundary gate flagged a `tx.update()` inside a plain function that generated the salt: it reads the call site, so a write reached through a function call is indistinguishable from one outside the Operation pipeline, which is exactly the shape it exists to catch. The write is spelled out inside the operation now and only the hashing, which touches nothing, is factored out. The a11y lint refused `role="group"` on the score rows and asked for a `fieldset`, which is right: five identical one-to-five rows are five identical button sets, and without a name on each group a screen reader reads the same five numbers five times. **All five statements are answered together and a partial submission is refused twice**, at the boundary and on the screen. §8.6 builds the rhythm score out of statements two and five, so a set missing one of them produces a diagnostic with a hole in it, and a diagnostic built on a missing answer is worse than none because it reads as evidence. **Verified:** review-diagnosis 17/17, method 268/268 with the two new taxonomy tests, end-to-end 41 passed and 1 skipped, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 436 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check at 55 thresholds and the licence gate. **The full `packages/core` suite was not run for this row**, and that is a deliberate change of pace for finishing the phase today: it runs once at the end of the batch rather than before each commit. If it finds anything here it lands as a follow-up commit rather than being folded back into this row silently. |
| P4-T11c-a | The diagnostic and the reset decisions | in_review | agung | 2026-08-26 | **P4-T11c was cut in two before code was written, and IMPLEMENTATION-PLAN is corrected in this change.** It carried five tables, four of §8.1's eleven stages, roughly ten actions and four panels in one [M]. P4-T11a was already at the size limit with three tables and two panels. Split at the line §8 itself draws: §8.6 and §8.8 are the verdict and the close, §8.9 is the feed-forward, which is P4-T11c-b. **Migration 0050: `review_diagnostics` and `review_decisions`.** METHOD.md §8.8's three meanings were missing from `packages/method` and are added here as `CLOSE_DECISION_MEANINGS`, held word for word against the document, the same gap class as §8.4's taxonomy at P4-T11b. The three words themselves already existed as `GOAL_CLOSE_DECISIONS` in `packages/db` and are reused rather than copied, because the two must agree. **Two names for one verdict, resolved toward the canon.** TECHNICAL-PLAN §4 wrote the first verdict as `delivered` while `packages/method` calls it `results_delivered`. A translation layer between the canon and the store has nothing to gain and one place to get backwards, so the stored value is `DiagnosisKind` verbatim and the plan row is corrected with that reasoning on it. **The diagnostic is stored with the numbers it was read against.** §8.6 calls it the review's most valuable output and the minutes have to show what the room was told, so a verdict recomputed later would quietly change as scores were corrected. A test proves that: correcting a score after reading leaves the record alone, and reading again replaces numbers and verdict together so the row stays internally consistent. The sentences are recomputed from the stored numbers rather than stored twice, so a wording correction in METHOD.md reaches old records while the verdict they were read at does not move. **Reading the diagnostic is refused until both numbers exist.** §8.6 combines a cycle score and a rhythm score and neither is optional. A diagnostic built on a missing answer is worse than none, because it reads as evidence. **The cycle score the diagnostic reads is not the reveal-gated one.** P4-T10b-b withholds an objective's roll-up during stage two; by stage seven the room has seen everything, and a diagnostic that ignored unrevealed grades would diagnose a quarter that did not happen. Stated in the code so the two readings are not confused later. **One test-plan line is unmet and the schema is why, not an oversight.** "A keep, modify or abandon decision writes back to the goal on close" was implemented, refused by `goals_close_is_complete` (migration 0022), and then removed: that constraint holds `closed_at`, `success_status` and `close_decision` present together or not at all, so a decision without a close is a contradiction and it was right to refuse. Closing the objective from the review would mean deriving a success status and **inventing a retrospective body for every objective**, because `closeGoalInTx` requires one and stage nine collects none. **Open question for Agung:** should `goals.close` read `review_decisions` as its default, or should the review close objectives outright and collect a retrospective per objective to do it? The decision is recorded and nothing is written to the goal until that is settled. **Two things not asserted end to end, both recorded rather than faked.** Reading the diagnostic once both numbers exist needs a return to stage seven after the stage-eight survey, and the rail advances forward only, so a browser cannot get there; the three §8.6 verdicts, the refusal, and the stored numbers surviving a correction are covered against a real database in `packages/core`. The stage-nine panel is driven in the browser, including the refusal of a decision with no why. **Verified:** review-reset 16/16, method 270/270 with the two new close-decision tests, end-to-end 41 passed and 1 skipped, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 439 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check at 55 thresholds. **The full `packages/core` suite runs once at the end of this batch**, not per commit, which is the pace change recorded on the P4-T11b row. |
| P4-T11c-b | Learnings, next-cycle drafts, decisions and actions | in_review | agung | 2026-08-26 | **Migration 0051: `learnings`, `next_cycle_drafts` and `review_actions`.** **Agung's decision of 2026-08-26 on the close question P4-T11c-a raised, implemented here.** The decision stays in `review_decisions` and `goals.reviewDecision` hands it to the close screen as its default, so closing an objective remains a deliberate act with a retrospective behind it and nobody has to remember or retype what the room already decided. The most recent review wins, because a goal reviewed twice was discussed twice and the later conversation is the one that stands. Nothing writes `goals.close_decision` from a review, which is what `goals_close_is_complete` requires. **One correction to TECHNICAL-PLAN §4, and the canon is why.** That row had `review_actions.owner_id?` and `due_on?` optional while METHOD.md §8.1 stage 11 says "Every action has a name and a date, or it is a wish". The task's own test plan asks for the refusal, so nullable columns would let the product store exactly what the stage exists to prevent. Both are not null and the plan row is corrected. **Promoting the same retro theme twice is refused.** §8.9 promotes the top dot-voted themes, and a second promotion would double a theme's weight in the next cycle, which is the opposite of what the dot vote was for. `learnings.retro_note_id` is unique per note and a check constraint ties it to `source = 'retro_theme'` both ways, so a promoted learning names the note it came from and a typed one cannot claim to. The board arrives most-voted first, because asking a room to remember its own vote is how the top theme stops being the one promoted. **Carry forward is off by default in the column, the action and the checkbox.** §8.9's rule is that carried work re-enters as an issue and has to survive prioritisation on its merits, so a default of true is the free pass that section refuses. **One test-plan line moved rather than quietly dropped.** "The lowest process-health statement becomes an issue in the next cycle" is §8.9's feed-forward, which writes `cycle_issues` rows into the next cycle. P4-T12 is titled "Minutes, exports and review feed-forward" and owns that mapping; this row builds the tables it reads, and IMPLEMENTATION-PLAN is corrected to say so. **The end-to-end spec now walks all eleven stages in one test**, from the room pulse through to ticking an action and reopening it. Both screen-level refusals are driven in the browser: a draft with no why, and an action with no owner or date. **Verified:** review-forward 19/19, end-to-end 41 passed and 1 skipped, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 441 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check at 55 thresholds. **The full `packages/core` suite runs once at the end of this batch**, which is the pace recorded on the P4-T11b row. |
| P4-T12-a | The minutes and their exports | in_review | agung | 2026-08-26 | **P4-T12 was cut in two before code was written.** The minutes are a read across twelve tables, a screen and two export routes; the feed-forward is a change to `cycles.feedForward`, which P3-T15 already built carrying a `waiting` list that names the two §8.9 rows it could not fill. Two separable pieces with separate acceptance criteria. IMPLEMENTATION-PLAN is corrected in this change. **One new runtime dependency, and Agung approved the specific package after I proposed candidates: `@react-pdf/renderer` (MIT).** The measurement, taken rather than estimated: 3.5MB across 15 packages in the traced standalone output, which is what reaches the Docker image. Anything wrapping Chromium was rejected because it would add roughly 300MB to an image P1-T09 worked down from 726MB to 204MB, and the low-level drawing libraries would mean computing coordinates for a document whose length changes with every review. The licence gate is green. **Markdown and PDF are the same document.** One function builds it and three things consume it, so the download, the PDF and the screen cannot drift into three records that disagree slightly. The PDF renderer reads the four Markdown shapes that function produces and nothing else: a general parser would be a second dependency for a document this file already knows the shape of, and anything it failed to render would be a section silently missing from a record. **Two absences are the point of half the tests.** The facilitator's private per-stage notes are out: UIUX-PLAN's S-25 listed "facilitator notes" among the contents, §8.1 makes them private, and the screen that collects them promises nobody else can see them. METHOD.md sits above UIUX-PLAN in the authority order and §8.10's own list does not mention them, so the S-25 line is corrected rather than the promise broken. The management retro is **absent from the payload** for anybody who is not a manager or the space's coordinator, not merely hidden on a screen, so an export cannot carry §8.7's answers past their audience. A test reads the whole payload as text and asserts neither appears. **Nothing in the minutes is recomputed.** The cycle score and the verdict come from `review_diagnostics` as the room was told them. A test corrects a score after the diagnostic was read and asserts the summary does not move, because a document that recalculated would disagree with the meeting it minutes. **The minutes are readable while the review runs, and say so.** Nothing in §8.10 makes the document exist only after the close, and a facilitator checking it mid-review is ordinary. A draft that did not announce itself would get quoted as a record, so both the screen and the Markdown carry that line. **A link from the review to its minutes**, because a document with no way in is the shape this branch already found once on the session screen itself. **Verified:** review-minutes 10/10, end-to-end 41 passed and 1 skipped with the minutes screen and both export routes driven in a browser, including the PDF's magic bytes; typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 446 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check and the licence gate with the new dependency. Two of my own test expectations were wrong and are corrected: 0.65 is below §11's 0.7 floor so the verdict is `strategy_or_quality`, and `sessions.setStageNote` takes `id` rather than `sessionId`. **The full `packages/core` suite ran green at 69 files and 1342 tests before this row**, covering everything committed up to P4-T11c-b; this row's own core test file is verified and the next full run will cover it. |
| P4-T12-b | Review feed-forward into the next cycle | in_review | agung | 2026-08-26 | **§8.9's mapping is complete, and `cycles.feedForward` reports nothing waiting.** P3-T15 built that action with a `waiting` list naming the two rows it could not fill, because the tables did not exist: learnings arrived at P4-T11c-b and the process-health survey at P4-T11b. The field stays rather than being deleted, because it is what made a deliberately partial mapping visible instead of letting it read as complete, and the next row §8.9 grows will use it the same way. **Pulled, not pushed, and §8.10 is why.** "Hold the review before drafting the next cycle's OKRs, never in the same session" means the next cycle usually does not exist when the review closes, so a push on close would be a no-op in the ordinary order. `cycles.feedForward` already took a `fromCycleId` and a `toCycleId`, which is the only shape that works, and the review is found rather than passed in: it is the closed quarterly session on the cycle being left behind. IMPLEMENTATION-PLAN's acceptance criterion said "when the facilitator closes it... the next cycle's Phase 2 already holds the scores" and is corrected. **The lowest process-health statement lands as an issue, not a priority, and that is a reading rather than an oversight.** §8.9's table says "a process priority" while its closing line says carried work re-enters as an issue and does not get a free pass. `cycle_issues.source` has carried a `process_health` value since P3-T03 with nothing writing it, so the schema was built for this reading, and a statement promoted straight to a priority would be exactly the free pass §8.9 refuses. A test asserts no `cycle_priorities` row is written. **If Agung reads §8.9's wording the other way, this is one line to change.** **A carried learning joins carried key results as an issue at impact 4**, because §8.9's row is "every carry-forward item" and a learning marked to carry is one. An unmarked learning reaches the input pack and not the issue list: it is a learning, not a commitment for the next quarter. **The learnings go into §2.6's item two**, "Prior cycle OKRs with scores and retrospective notes", which is where §8.9 sends them. The note is rewritten rather than appended, so a second run leaves one note instead of two copies of it. **Idempotent throughout, and tested as such.** A cycle fed forward twice, by two people or by a retry, holds one set of issues and one input-pack note. Doubling them would inflate a prioritisation list nobody added to. **Two honest empty cases.** A review that never ran hands over nothing and says so. A survey nobody answered has no lowest statement, and inventing one would be the product deciding a team's own process problem for it. **Verified:** review-feed-forward 9/9, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 446 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check at 55 thresholds. One of my own test calls was wrong and is corrected: `cycles.create` takes `on`, a date inside the period to generate, rather than explicit bounds. **This row and P4-T12-a are the two not yet covered by a full `packages/core` run**; the last one was green at 69 files and 1342 tests through P4-T11c-b. |
| P4-T14a-a | Copilot threads and grounded answers | in_review | agung | 2026-08-26 | **Asking and answering are two writes, and that is the whole design.** A single `copilot.answer` action would be one call for every projection, and `defineWriteAction` builds its handler from an operation spec whose `execute` runs *inside* the transaction, so it would hold a connection open for the length of a model call. Ten concurrent questions is ten connections idle in transaction and a pool of ten. So `copilot.ask` records the question, the model is called with nothing open, and `copilot.recordAnswer` records the answer. `answerQuestion` is the orchestration and is a plain export, for the same reason `resolveAICredential` is. The split is also what P4-T14a-b needs: streaming cannot happen inside a transactional write. **The citation guarantee is made twice, and both halves are tested.** By construction, the model is shown passages positionally and never an identifier, so a citation it produces can only name something retrieval already found for this member; an index out of range resolves to nothing. At read time, `readableCitations` asks access again on every read, so a source deleted after the answer stops being shown while the prose stands unchanged. Neither half alone is enough: the first goes stale, and the second cannot stop a model naming a stranger's goal. **Retrieval was requiring every word of the question, and returned nothing for most of them.** `plainto_tsquery` joins terms with `&`, so "What is happening with mid-market activation?" matched no goal, because no goal contains "happening". Nothing before this passed retrieval a sentence, so P4-T13b's tests never saw it. Both query paths now swap the operators for `|` and let `ts_rank` decide, with the hyphenated-compound phrase operator left intact. Three tests in `embeddings-retrieval` cover the sentence, the ranking and the all-stop-words query that must still return nothing. **One answer about who may read what, now that two callers ask it.** `governingResource` plus `getAccessScoped` was inlined in the retrieval loop; it is now `mayRead`, and `governingResource` is private. Two copies of that pair is how a citation and a retrieval hit would come to disagree. **Copilot activity rows are kept out of the feed by a central set.** The Operation pipeline requires an activity, and an activity with no `contextId` is workspace-public by construction, so "Agung asked the copilot something" would have appeared in everybody's feed. `PRIVATE_ACTIVITY_KINDS` is filtered in `queryFeed` rather than at each caller. Neither payload carries the question or the answer. The audit rows carry the cost, which is what an administrator needs. **Both writes sit at `comment`, not `edit` and not `view`.** Not `edit`, because the write is one member's own conversation and a member who may read a space without changing it should be able to ask about it; that is `nudges.snooze`'s reasoning exactly. Not `view`, because `registry.test.ts` holds one absolute line, that no write is reachable at `view`. The cost is that a guest at `view` cannot ask the copilot, which is the narrower grant. The invariant was extended with the reason written next to the other two exceptions. **§7 asks for cost on the message and my first schema had none.** Added `cost numeric(12,6)`, read back as a decimal string so a cost report adds up. `citations` is an addition to §7's column list, recorded in DATABASE.md with its reason. **What is not here.** No interface at all: this row is the contract and the guarantee, and the panel, the streaming, the stop control and the three screen states are P4-T14a-b. `ai_usage_events` gets no row from a copilot turn either; the message carries model, tokens and cost, and the usage event belongs where the provider call is priced, which is the host. Worth a look in P4-T14a-b. **A third copy of `actingMember` now exists**, alongside the private ones in `actions/sessions.ts` and `actions/alignment.ts`. Left local rather than extracted, because moving it means editing two large files this task had no other reason to touch. The extraction is the follow-up. **Verified:** copilot-ask 20/20, embeddings-retrieval 10/10, registry 9/9, activities, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 453 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check. |
| P4-T14a-b | The copilot panel | in_review | agung | 2026-08-26 | **The panel fills the `askAi` slot `Topbar` has been holding open since P2-T10.** That slot was left deliberately empty with a comment saying a button wired to nothing is worse than no button. It is wired now, on ⌘J, from the one place every authenticated page composes its chrome. **Streaming needed a new capability, not a rewrite.** `streamGrounded` sits beside `answerGrounded` on the drafter seam and yields a discriminated union: `text` chunks for the reader, one `done` chunk carrying the assembled `GroundedAnswer`. A host may implement either or both. `copilot.availability` reports which, so the panel does not offer a stop control for something that cannot be stopped. **A stream that stops carries no `done` chunk, and that absence is the signal.** `streamAnswer` records what arrived in a `finally`, which is the only place that runs both when the signal aborts and when the consumer stops pulling (a closed tab). Nothing can be yielded from a `finally`, so the `done` event may not reach the browser on a stop; the reader who stopped it already knows. A stop before the first token records nothing at all, so the question stays askable rather than being answered with silence. **Both ways of stopping are tested, against a scripted provider.** `copilot-stream.test.ts`: signal abort, consumer break, provider throwing mid-answer, and stopped-before-the-first-word. In every case the partial text is stored with `stopped_at` set and **no citations**, because the model never said which passages its unfinished sentence rested on. **The sentinel line is the part of the provider work that needed a test.** A streamed answer cannot report its sources as JSON without the reader watching a string being escaped, so the model ends with a `SOURCES: 1, 3` line the product reads and the reader never sees. A naive implementation emits every piece as it arrives and those words appear on screen and then vanish. `copilot-drafter.test.ts` splits the pieces mid-word and mid-sentinel and asserts each piece individually, not only the join. **A streamed turn records no token count, and that is the port's shape.** `AIProvider.stream` yields text and no usage, so `tokensIn`, `tokensOut` and `cost` are null on a streamed answer; estimating them would put a made-up number in a cost report. The cap is still honoured at the door, so a spent budget cannot start a stream. What it cannot do is stop one part way through on cost. The non-streamed `answerGrounded` reports all three. **Three defects and one bug found, three fixed here and one left where it belongs.** (1) I detached `answerGrounded` from its drafter (`const f = drafter.answerGrounded`), which loses `this`: a working provider read as an absent one, and the catch turned it into "the provider did not answer". Both call sites now go through the drafter, with the reason written next to them. (2) `copilot.spec.ts` sorted before `registration-to-dashboard.spec.ts` and claimed the instance, turning that file's registration test red; the file is `s39-copilot.spec.ts` now and `signIn` refuses to claim, with an error naming the rule. (3) Two test questions were made only of stop words, so retrieval correctly found nothing. **The bug not fixed: `?` cannot open the shortcut overlay.** `ShortcutOverlay` registers `{ key: "?" }` with no `shift`, and `useKeyboardShortcut` reads a missing `shift` as "shift must not be held". On any keyboard where `?` is shifted, the overlay's own shortcut can never fire. That is P2-T10's component and fixing it under a copilot task would be the wrong place, so it is recorded here and in the spec, with the assertion that would have caught it written out as a comment. **A live answer is not reachable in a browser yet, for a reason outside this row.** Nothing constructs `OutboxRelay` anywhere, so no outbox topic is consumed in a running instance and `content.embed` indexes nothing. Even with a key configured, retrieval would find nothing to cite. The e2e spec proves the state a self-hosted instance without a key actually runs in, and says all of this in its own header rather than leaving it to be discovered. **Verified:** copilot-stream 9/9, copilot-drafter 10/10, the full `packages/core` suite 1410/1410, `packages/agents` 26/26, e2e 48 passed 1 skipped with all seven copilot tests green, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 456 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check, licences. |
| P4-T14b-a | Copilot proposals | in_review | agung | 2026-08-26 | **A model authors fields, never a payload and never an identifier.** The catalogue in `copilot/proposals.ts` offers one action today, `goals.create`. The model writes a title, a sentence and a level, and picks a space by number from a list of labels; the cycle, the space's real id, the champion and the reviewer all come from the product and the asking member. Same positional rule as the citations, and for the same reason: a number out of range resolves to nothing rather than to somebody else's space. Three refusals are tested, and they are the tests that matter: an action off the list, an index past the end, and a field the schema will not take. **The list is one entry on purpose.** Least privilege is the rule for agents and it is the rule here: a copilot that may propose any registered write is a copilot that may propose `people.erase`. The shape is what makes the second entry a small change, and a test asserts that a model naming `people.erase` produces nothing at all. **Nothing in this row decides whether the caller may do what the proposal asks.** `copilot.applyProposal` calls the proposed action through `callAction` as the applying member, so the action's own access level and its own authorisation run exactly as they would if they had typed it. The test lowers the member's binding to `comment` and asserts the panel still offers Apply and `goals.create` refuses it, which is the test-plan line word for word: refused by the permission layer, not hidden by the interface. **`goals.delete` is new, and Agung approved it rather than me deciding it.** The acceptance criterion needs a working undo after a created objective, and there was no reverse for `goals.create`: `goals.close` is the end of a cycle with an outcome and a retrospective, so using it as an undo would file a false report about a quarter. The new action is a soft delete at `full`, `destructive` in the registry, taking the key results with it. It is useful well beyond the copilot, which is why it was a product decision and not a mechanism. **Undo is a write, not a rollback.** The reverse runs through the pipeline with its own audit row, so the record says an objective was created and then removed. `undone_at` sits on the applied row rather than becoming a fourth status, because it was applied and then it was undone and both are true. A member at `edit` can apply a proposal that creates an objective and cannot undo it, because `goals.delete` is `full`. That is the access model answering honestly rather than the copilot inventing a right, and it is tested. **`proposed_changes.run_id` became optional.** Every proposal until now came from an agent run. A copilot proposal came from a member's question, and inventing a run to hang it off would put a run in the log that nobody scheduled and nothing executed. A proposal now names its origin, a run or a thread, exactly one, with a check constraint. `result` holds what applying it returned, which is the only place the created entity's identifier exists and therefore the only thing undo can work from. **Copilot proposal activity stays out of the workspace feed; what it does goes in.** The four new kinds joined `PRIVATE_ACTIVITY_KINDS`. The applied action writes its own activity through its own operation, and that one belongs in the feed because the change does. A test asserts both halves. **Two things the browser proves and two it cannot.** The e2e spec inserts the proposal with SQL, because a proposal only exists when a model writes one and this instance has no provider; it then proves the preview, the AI chip, Apply creating the objective with a `goals.create` audit row, the objective's own page rendering, and Undo soft-deleting it. The model-to-proposal path is proved in `packages/core` and `packages/agents` against scripted providers. **A defect of mine, found by the suite.** My first permission test inserted a `workspace_members` row before its `users` row and then leaned on a guest having no bindings. It failed for the wrong reason and left the admin pool wedged, which then read as a hang in every later test. Rewritten to lower the acting member's own binding, which is precise about which layer refuses. **A false alarm worth recording, because it cost an hour.** A run of this file appeared to deadlock on `truncate` after the seventh test. It was my own overlapping background test processes sharing one database, inserting the same users and truncating under each other. Killed, and the file runs 16/16 in 35 seconds. Nothing in the product. **What is not here.** P4-T14b-b, the background runs, is cut out and marked blocked: nothing constructs `OutboxRelay` or a jobs adapter anywhere, so an enqueued job would never be picked up. Recorded as PLAN.md §12's R10 at Agung's direction rather than opened as a Phase 4 row. **Verified:** copilot-proposals 16/16, copilot-drafter 15/15, the full `packages/core` suite 1426/1426, `packages/agents` 31/31, e2e 53 passed 1 skipped with all five proposal tests green, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 458 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check, licences. |
| P4-T14b-b | Copilot background runs | blocked |  |  | Cut out of P4-T14b on 26 August 2026 and blocked on the same day. Nothing in the application constructs `OutboxRelay` or a jobs adapter, so a background job this row enqueues would never be picked up by anything. Recorded as PLAN.md §12's R10, owned by the deployment and operations phase. Do not start this row before a host exists. |
| P4-T15a | Planning and drafting assists | in_review | agung | 2026-08-26 | **Cut into four on 2026-08-26, before any code.** The single row listed thirteen distinct assists across all four groups of AI-NATIVE-PLAN.md §2, each needing a feature switch, provenance and a preview. That is a lane, not a session, and the [M] sizing in this phase has already been wrong three times. Cut along §2's own group boundaries rather than an invented grouping, so each row maps onto one section of the capability catalogue. **Two of the thirteen had already landed and the list restated them:** the rewrite assist is `goals.rewriteKeyResult` from P4-T06c and the overdue check-in draft is `goals.publishDraftedCheckIn` from P4-T05c-b, so both are dropped rather than left in to be built twice. **Three more belong to other phases:** initiatives and tasks are Phase 5, grounded question answering is P4-T14a, and spreadsheet column mapping is P6-T01. **Three read actions, and reads is the guarantee.** `goals.draftObjective`, `goals.suggestMeasure` and `goals.suggestParent` commit nothing, so there is no version of these that quietly creates an objective: the propose-and-approve line is drawn by the shape of the code rather than by remembering to be careful. Three tests count goals and key results before and after, so a future change that starts writing is caught by behaviour and not only by the registry's shape invariant. **The product judges the model's output; the model does not get to claim it.** A drafted objective and a suggested measure are run back through METHOD.md §4's own checks and the response reports which ids actually pass and fail. The surface shows those as chips, so a reader sees "fails OBJ-2" before they decide, never a confident draft with nothing said about it. That is P4-T06c's rule applied to two more assists. **The suggested parent is safe by construction, not by a filter.** Candidates come from `goals.list`, which is access-scoped, and the model answers with an index into that list. There is no index it could return that resolves to a goal the member cannot read, and no filter to forget. A test returns index 9 for a list of one and gets null; another asserts the model is shown titles and levels and never an identifier. **Only objectives above this one are offered as parents.** A sibling is a dependency and §5 has its own register for those, so the candidate list is bounded by level. Tested with an individual-level objective, which is offered the company and the team ones and nothing else. **One feature switch per assist**, `assists.draftObjective`, `assists.suggestMeasure` and `assists.suggestParent`, so an administrator can turn off the one that is not helping and keep the other two. A test switches one off and asserts the next still answers. The keys are exported and the test uses them from the code, so a rename breaks the test rather than quietly making it assert nothing. **Provenance is written for the first time.** `goals.ai_generated` has existed since migration 0022 and nothing ever wrote it, which meant an assisted objective read exactly like a typed one. `goals.create` now takes `aiGenerated`, the apply path sets it, and the ordinary form does not. It is the caller's claim rather than something inferred: only the surface that ran the assist knows whether the reader kept the draft or rewrote every word. **A defect of mine that took thirteen unrelated test files down with it.** `goal-assists.ts` imported `callAction` from `registry.ts`, which imports `goal-assists.ts`. The cycle left TypeScript unable to infer the registry's own type, so every typed `callAction` result across the package became `any` and thirteen test files failed on implicit `any`. Fixed by calling the two read handlers directly, which is what `callAction` does anyway and carries the same access checks. Written down next to the code, because the symptom points nowhere near the cause. **The browser proves the acceptance criterion in full.** With no provider, `/cycle?phase=4` offers none of the three affordances, the create form still creates an objective, and the Draft Coach still renders a §4 check id. That is the criterion word for word, and it is the state every self-hosted install without an API key runs in. What the assists do with a provider is proved in `packages/core` against a scripted drafter. **Key results carry no provenance column.** `key_results` has `quality_flags` and no `ai_generated`, so a measure applied from a draft is recorded only through its objective. Worth a column if the assists get used; not added here, because adding one to a shipped table for a field nothing reads yet is speculative. **Verified:** goal-assists 19/19, the full `packages/core` suite, `packages/agents`, e2e 57 passed with all four assist tests green, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 461 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check. |
| P4-T15b-a | The digest and the trend | in_review | agung | 2026-08-26 | **Half of this row was building the thing the assist assists with.** METHOD.md §7.2 step 4 describes a digest with six parts, and P4-T08 stored five numbers in a `jsonb` column with nothing rendering them: there was no digest template, in words, anywhere. So `weeklyDigestLines` is now in `packages/method`, because the digest is what the product says to a team about their own week and that is canon, and `sessions.digest` assembles it. Three of §7.2's parts the stored row does not hold, the change on last week, the names of what is at risk, and the blocker clocks, are computed at read time from rows that were already there. No schema change, and an old digest still reads correctly after a goal is renamed. **A narrated number the product did not compute is refused, not trusted.** `statesOnlyKnownNumbers` compares every figure in the model's prose against the numbers the product handed it, and one that is not among them drops the whole narration. That is the strongest guarantee available here: a narration cannot be made to state a figure nobody measured, whatever the prompt says. The allowed set for a trend is the series, the targets, the corridor bounds and the differences the product itself computes, so "up 7 points" passes and "recovering from 41%" does not. **The check is generous about what counts as a number and strict about what is allowed.** `62.0` for `62` passes, and a comma decimal passes, because a check nothing can satisfy is a check that gets removed. Tested directly as well as through both actions, because it is the one function standing between a model and a false figure in a digest that goes to leadership. **A real bug, found by a test asserting an order.** `kpis.detail` returns records newest first, which is right for a table. The trend prompt hands the model a list labelled "oldest first", so an unsorted list would have had it read every trend backwards and describe a rise as a fall. Sorted now, with the reason written next to it. **Both narrations are optional and neither is load-bearing.** With the provider off, `sessions.digest` renders the whole digest and the panel shows it; the narration appears above the lines and the lines never go away, because a reader who wants the numbers should not have to trust a paragraph for them. **One switch per assist**, `assists.narrateDigest` and `assists.narrateTrend`, and a test switches one off and asserts nothing reaches the model. **A weekly session had no digest panel at all** before this row, so one is added. It is the first surface for §7.2's step 4. **Verified:** the digest template 14/14 in `packages/method`, rhythm-assists 25/25, the full `packages/core` suite, `packages/method` 284/284, `packages/agents` 31/31, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 464 files, dead-code, method:check. **A second import cycle, and this one cost nineteen tests in a file about something else.** The assist feature keys were exported from the two action modules, `index.ts` re-exported them, and that made `index.ts` load those modules, which load `actions/goals.ts` and `actions/kpis.ts`, which meant `registry.ts` could be evaluated while a module it depends on was still initialising. `ACTION_MAP` then held `undefined` for `goals.create`, and `goal-assists.test.ts` failed nineteen times on "Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'handler')". The keys now live in `ai/assist-keys.ts`, a module that imports nothing, because a module with no imports cannot be part of a cycle. Same root cause as P4-T15a's, one layer up, which is why the fix is structural rather than another careful import. |
| P4-T15b-b | The blocker summary and the KPI suggestion | in_review | agung | 2026-08-27 | **The board did not exist, and the assist could not without it.** The product could read the blockers raised in one session and nothing else: no way to ask what a space is stuck on, which is the "open-blocker board with ages" REQUIREMENTS §7 asks for. `blockers.board` is that read, it needs no provider, and it is on the space page. **§7.3 states no ranking, so the order is derived, and the derivation is written down where changing it will be noticed.** §7.3 gives the five types, the clock and one sentence: "a blocker that ages past it is escalated, not re-discussed". IMPLEMENTATION-PLAN asks for "ranked by age and impact". So: §11's ladder first, because the ladder is the product's own statement of urgency; then §3.2's band for what the blocker holds up, which is "impact" in the only terms this product measures it in; then age, which is the thing §7.3 does name. `rankBlockers` is in `packages/method` with that reasoning at the top, and `packages/method/test/blocker-board.test.ts` pins each step. **If that reading is wrong it is a METHOD.md question, and this is the flag for it.** **The ladder fires at the hour, not after it.** §11's wording is "owner warned at twenty hours", and a ladder firing at twenty and one would warn nobody at twenty. The clock and the coordinator rung are both twenty-four by default and are kept separate, because a workspace can move one without the other. **The summary cannot reorder the board.** The assist is handed the list in the product's order and asked for prose; the response carries the board back unchanged beside the words. A model reordering a queue by how interesting each item reads is the failure this separation prevents. And a summary that *quotes* a next action not on the board is dropped: only quoted spans are checked, because prose about a list naturally reuses its words and a check on loose words would refuse every real summary. **A suggested formula is validated by §6's own parser before it is offered.** `buildFormula` resolves the model's numbered references against the list it was shown, folds them into `formulaNodeSchema`'s binary shape, and runs `validateFormula`. A formula the parser refuses is dropped and the rest of the suggestion stands, because a metric with a bad formula is still a metric somebody wanted. Every other field falls back on its own too: a frequency the product does not have becomes monthly, a corridor outside §6's bounds becomes null, and the title and unit survive both. **The formula shape was not what I first assumed.** I wrote `{operation, operands[]}` with names like "sum" and "ratio"; §6's actual grammar is `{op: add|sub|mul|div, l, r}` over `{k: kpiId}` leaves. Rewritten to §6's spelling, because a second vocabulary for the same four operators is a translation layer that eventually disagrees with the parser. **What the browser proves is the order.** Four blockers inserted deliberately out of age order, ranked by the ladder rather than by age, with the ones past the clock marked and the newest carrying no escalation chip. The summary and its refusals are proved in `packages/core` against a scripted drafter, because this instance has no provider. **Verified:** blocker-board 24/24 in `packages/core` and 8/8 in `packages/method`, the full `packages/core` suite 1504/1504, `packages/method` 292/292, `packages/agents` 31/31, e2e 71 passed with all five board tests green, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 471 files, dead-code, method:check. |
| P4-T15c | Review assists | in_review | agung | 2026-08-27 | **All five of §2.3's assists, over deterministic paths that all already existed.** The retro board, the minutes, §8.6's diagnostic, a goal's check-in history and the learnings marked to carry forward were built in P4-T10 to P4-T12, so this row adds only the language on top of them. Every one is a read: nothing here writes, so there is no version of these that quietly changes a review. **The diagnostic is additive, and the test proves it field by field.** `sessions.narrateDiagnostic` reads `sessions.diagnostic` first and returns that read's verdict, diagnosis and prescription unchanged; only `narrative` comes from a model. The test compares the provider-off answer with the provider-on answer string by string and asserts only the narrative differs, which would fail the day somebody let a model write the verdict. §8.6's own read is asserted untouched afterwards too. The prompt is told the verdict is not its to change, restate or argue with, and the code does not depend on it obeying. **Clustering cannot merge two reviews, by construction.** The notes handed to the model are one session's board, numbered, and the answer is positional over that list. A test gives two reviews notes each and asserts the clustering of one contains three notes and not the other's, even when the model answers with an index past the end. A theme with no title or no surviving notes is dropped, and notes no theme claimed are returned as `unclustered` rather than quietly lost. **Every proposed objective cites its learning or is dropped.** §8.9 hands learnings forward so the next cycle answers them; an objective with no learning behind it is one somebody could have typed unaided, and this is not the place for it. The citation is an index into the carried learnings, resolved to the learning's own id and text, and a response with nothing left resolves to null rather than an empty list dressed as an answer. **The minutes draft is built from the minutes read's own shape.** `minutesSections` walks the read rather than naming its fields, so a section added to the minutes reaches the draft with no change here, and anything that looks like an identifier is dropped on the way. A draft cannot describe a review the minutes do not. **Two things I got wrong, both caught by tests.** A check-in names its subject through `subject_type` and `subject_id`, not a `goal_id` column, and it has no `period_start`: the retrospective assist read the wrong columns until the typechecker said so. And the diagnostic's rhythm score is §8.5's one-to-five survey mean, not a fraction, which the table's own check constraint refused. **Two of the five are on screen.** The diagnostic panel gains "Add the specifics", under the verdict and never instead of it, and the retro board gains "Find the themes", which says on the screen that the vote is still per note. Both are absent with no provider, which is every install without an API key. The minutes draft, the retrospective draft and the next-cycle proposals are registry actions with no surface yet, and that is worth a follow-up row rather than three half-built panels here. **Verified:** review-assists 20/20, the full `packages/core` suite 1524/1524, `packages/agents` 31/31, e2e 71 passed, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 472 files, dead-code, method:check. |
| P4-T15d | The list filter assist | in_review | agung | 2026-08-26 | **The grammar had to grow before the assist could set it.** The explorer filtered on a cycle, a level, a view and closed-or-not, so the row's own acceptance sentence, "my off-track goals this quarter", was unexpressible. `goals.list` gains `health` and `mine`, and the explorer gains both as chips. Both are ordinary filters that work with no provider, and the explorer is better for having them either way. **`mine` rather than an owner id.** A filter naming a member id would let anyone read somebody else's list by editing a URL. This question is always about the person asking, so the filter is a boolean and the server resolves who that is. Champion or reviewer, because both are ways of being accountable for an objective. **Refusing is a first-class answer, and most of the tests are about it.** §2.4 asks for a filter "refused rather than approximated", so the drafter answers `expressible` as a field: a model handed only a filter shape fills it in, and one asked whether the sentence fits the grammar answers that question instead. Then the product re-checks every field: a level it does not have, a band it does not have, a cycle number past the end of the list, or a filter that would filter nothing all become refusals with the reason. An assist that narrowed "goals blocked on legal" to "off track" would hand somebody a list they believe is one thing and is another, and they would act on it. **What comes back is the explorer's own filter state, not a result set of its own.** The panel navigates to the URL a reader would have reached by clicking the chips, so the answer is editable with the controls they already know: click Any on Health and the assist's answer is gone. That is why the component navigates instead of rendering a list. **A defect the e2e caught, and it was silent.** The explorer's `href` helper rebuilds the query from a fixed list of filters, and the two new ones were not in it, so clicking any chip quietly cleared them. The assertion that the other half of the filter survived a click is what found it. **The filter groups are named regions now.** "Any" and "All" both appear more than once on the bar, so a test clicking `getByRole("link", { name: "Any" }).first()` passes for the wrong reason. Each group is a labelled `fieldset`, which is what the a11y lint asks for and what a screen reader wants: the chips mean nothing without knowing which filter they belong to. **One `signIn` fix that helps every spec.** `waitForURL` resolves while the sign-in navigation is still finishing, so a `goto` on the next line was superseded and failed with `net::ERR_ABORTED`. Two specs hit it on separate runs. `signIn` now waits for the load, and `goTo` retries once. **Verified:** filter-assist 10/10, the full `packages/core` suite 1480/1480, e2e 66 passed with all five filter tests green, typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 468 files, dead-code, method:check. |

## Phase 5: Reach: channels, agents, work
