| P5-T00 | Reach design gate | in_review | agung | 2026-08-27 | **Three design documents, written 27 August 2026, awaiting an explicit approval.** `p5-t00-channel-design.md`: the three channel tables, the routing order, the capability matrix as `capabilities()` returns it, identity linking, the eight-step inbound security order, the command surface mapped to registry actions, and the conversational state machine for WhatsApp and Telegram. `p5-t00-agent-surface-design.md`: the drift check as the design, the three token audiences and why they cannot be one, the REST and command-line mapping, the six authorisation tables with the four places the flow is easy to get wrong, and the tool catalogue with its invariant test. `p5-t00-work-layer-design.md`: the seven work tables, board ordering under concurrency, and the one decision the whole layer turns on. **Each document names what already exists before what it adds.** The `Channel` port and the `none` driver, the nudge engine with its ladder and quiet hours, the 250-action registry with `can()` and the pipeline invariants, `blobs`, `comments` and the embeddings worker are all there and are cited by the task that built them. What does not exist is listed as plainly: no channel tables and no driver but `none`, no REST surface or OAuth tables, no initiatives, tasks, documents or search table. **One decision runs through the whole work layer and it is stated once, at the top.** Completing linked work does not move a key result. TECHNICAL-PLAN §4.9 says so and the design takes it literally: measured progress is the number, the ratio of finished tasks is a second signal beside it, and the gap between them is the divergence the Coach already reports. A product that let a full board turn a key result green would teach the exact practice it exists to correct. **The channel design says out loud that it delivers nothing until a relay exists.** Every table, driver, route and command is testable without one and none of them sends a message in a running instance with one absent. That is PLAN.md §12's R10, and naming it in the design means P5-T01 is planned around it rather than surprised by it. **Fourteen open questions are listed with my position on each**, four on channels, four on the public surfaces, five on the work layer, plus the relay question. Each is a place where the authority documents do not settle it and I chose; they are the items to read first. **What the approval would rest on, stated plainly.** The documents are checked structurally against their authorities: §5.2's capability matrix, §5.3's seven commands, §5.4's routing rules, §8.1's shape table, §8.2's authorisation requirements, §8.3's three exposures, TECHNICAL-PLAN §4.9's and §4.11's table lists and §14's projection rule are each reflected line for line, and every registry action named was checked against `ACTION_MAP`. What a structural check cannot settle is the judgement in the ordering scheme, the conversational question order, the REST path heuristic and the fourteen open questions. Needs an explicit design approval before any Phase 5 implementation row starts. **Approval, recorded as it actually happened.** On 27 August 2026 the instruction was "lanjut kerjakan phase 5, auto continue", not a line-by-line reading of the three documents. That is a human telling the phase to start, and Phase 5 implementation began on it, so it is written down here rather than reported as a design review that did not take place. The fourteen open questions are still open and each is answered by my own position in the documents. |
| P5-T01a | The outbox relay host | in_review | agung | 2026-08-27 | **Cut out of P5-T01 before any code, because P5-T01 assumed a relay host that has never existed.** `OutboxRelay` landed in `packages/adapters` at P1-T07 and nothing has ever constructed one. Six of the product's months of outbox rows are still in the table: no invitation email has been sent, no session event has reached a second browser, nothing has been indexed. Routing nudges through a queue nobody drains would have stacked a second layer of undelivered work on the first. The cut and its reason are in IMPLEMENTATION-PLAN.md; P5-T01b is the channel row. **The relay runs in the web process, and the risk register's fallback turned out to be the right answer.** R10 called an in-process relay wrong "the moment there are two" replicas. It is not: rows are claimed with `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` under a lease, which is the mechanism that makes concurrent relays safe, and P1-T04's own test drains one queue from several relays without a double delivery. What is left is a little wasted polling. Against that, a separate container needs the whole adapter layer resolvable outside the Next.js bundle, and Next traces only `pg` into the standalone output; the alternative was shipping the AI, mail and socket dependencies a second time, which is the same weight the Dockerfile already refused once for the migration runner. `OPENOKR_RELAY=off` leaves a dedicated drainer available to anybody who wants one. **The dispatch table lives in `packages/core` and takes its dependencies as plain functions.** Core may not import adapters, so `OutboxHandlerDeps` declares what a handler needs (a pool, and optionally an embed function, a publish function, a mailer) and the host supplies it, the same shape `AgentDrafter` already uses. **A missing dependency skips, a missing handler fails.** An instance with no SMTP skips its invitation rows with a logged reason rather than accumulating dead letters for every invite. A topic nothing handles is a producer that shipped ahead of its consumer, so `PermanentDispatchError` dead-letters it on the first attempt instead of retrying ten times over an hour. That error is declared twice, in core and in the relay, matched by `name`, because neither package may import the other; the trade is written at the top of both files. **The test that stops this happening again reads the source.** `handlers.test.ts` scans every `topic:` literal in the action and operation sources and fails if one has no handler, with a floor on the count so a broken regex cannot pass by finding nothing. A list would have been the thing somebody forgets to update. **And one test runs write-to-delivery end to end**: the real `invitations.createPersonalLink` action writes the row, the handler turns it into the email, and the assertion is that the link carries the same token the action returned to the inviter, which is the one value the database only holds as a hash. A second test dispatches every row a fresh workspace produces with no mail, no realtime and no embedding configured, which is a plain self-hosted install on day one, and requires that none of them throw. **`getPool` moved out of `lib/auth.ts` into `lib/pool.ts`.** A pool has nothing to do with authentication; while every caller was a request handler that also needed a session, nobody noticed. It is re-exported from the old path, so the sixty-eight importers are untouched. **A build worker does not start a relay**, guarded on `NEXT_PHASE`: `next build` runs `register()` in workers that have the placeholder `DATABASE_URL` and must exit, and a chained polling timer would keep them alive. **PLAN.md §12 R10 is closed in this change, and P4-T14b-b is unblocked by it.** **An acceptance criterion that has been unproven since P4-T07a is now proven.** `sessions.spec.ts`'s two-context test was `test.fixme` with a note saying to remove it when a relay exists. Both halves are fixed here: the relay delivers `session.stageChanged`, and the assertion was rewritten, because the old one waited for a weekly step title that the rail renders at every stage and so held before the advance as well as after it. It now reads `aria-current="step"`, which names one step out of four. The rail carried the current step in colour alone, which a screen reader cannot hear and a test cannot read without asserting on a class name, so `aria-current` was added to the list item. The test checks where the second client starts, advances the first, and requires the second to move with no reload, no click and no navigation in that context at all. **Verified:** `handlers.test.ts` 11/11, `outbox-delivery.test.ts` 2/2 against a real database, `relay.test.ts` (adapters) 13/13 including the new permanent-failure pair, web instrumentation 5/5, the new relay toggle 4/4, the boundary suite 80/80 with two new cases for the delivery exemption, and the full `packages/core` suite at 1537/1537. End to end **72 passed, nothing skipped**, which is one more than any run before this: the two-context test had been skipped since P4-T07a. typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 476 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check, licences. |
| P5-T01b-a | Channel connections, the email driver and the message log | in_review | agung | 2026-08-27 | **Cut out of P5-T01b before any code**, at the seam the design already draws: what a channel *is* (three tables, one driver, the log) before what the product *decides* (routing, quiet hours, degradation), which is P5-T01b-b and owns the original acceptance criterion. **Three tables, migration 0054, forced row-level security on each.** `channel_connections` carries the envelope trio rather than TECHNICAL-PLAN §4.11’s single `credentials_ciphertext`: envelope encryption needs the wrapped data key and the root key’s fingerprint beside the ciphertext, which is the shape `ai_credentials` already stores and `pnpm keys:rotate` already knows how to re-wrap. One column would have meant a second encryption scheme in the same instance. The plan row and DATABASE.md are both corrected in this change. **Email has no connection row**, because it is the instance’s own mail settings. That is what lets routing always fall back to it, and it is why the provider check constraint lists four values and the message log lists five. **An identity is unique in both directions.** Without the external-id index two members can claim the same chat account; without the member index one member holds two identities that inbound resolution would then have to choose between. Two tests insert straight through the actions to prove the database refuses both, by index name. **The message row is written before the send, not after it.** The unique key on `(workspace_id, idempotency_key)` is checked inside the same transaction as the write that asked for the message, so a caller asking twice gets one row and one outbox job. A log written after a successful send would record what happened and prevent nothing. That index is deliberately **not** partial on `deleted_at`: soft-deleting the record of a send must not let the send happen again, and a test inserts a deleted row and then a live one to prove it. **The acceptance criterion is one test.** Two identical asks, one row, one outbox job, one email. Delivering that job twice sends once more, because the handler finds the row already `sent` and stops before calling the driver. **Suppressed, failed and sent are three different outcomes and the log says which.** A member with no address is suppressed, not failed: an instance where that becomes a dead letter is an instance whose operators stop reading dead letters. A driver that throws is recorded and rethrown, so the relay’s own backoff decides whether to try again. **`channels.linkIdentity` and `channels.unlinkIdentity` sit at `comment`, and the registry invariant test names them as a fourth exception** beside `comments.*`, `nudges.snooze` and `copilot.*`. Both touch one row that is about the member and nobody else, and both resolve the member from the caller’s own session rather than from an input, so there is no identifier a caller could pass to reach somebody else’s. A member at `view` still receives nudges, and telling the product where to send them cannot be a privilege only editors have. `connect`, `disconnect` and `send` are workspace-wide and stay at `full`. **Connecting does not claim to have verified.** `last_verified_at` stays null until something calls the provider. A card that says verified because a string was pasted is worse than one that says nothing. **The relay wires the email driver directly rather than a router**, so the one thing this task does not build is visible as one line to replace rather than hidden inside a default. `channels.send`’s output names `email` for the same reason. **Nothing here is user-visible yet**, so there is no end-to-end test and no screen: S-36’s connection card and the member’s own channel settings arrive with the routing that gives them something to show. **A local flake that was not one.** The first end-to-end run after a fresh build timed out twice at the 120-second webServer limit and passed on the retry. The cause is `start-server.sh` rewriting eighty traced pnpm symlinks as junctions on Windows before the server binds, which only happens on the first start after a build. Raised to 240 seconds with the reason written beside it, because two wasted runs reading as flakiness is how a real failure gets ignored later. **Verified:** `channels.test.ts` **23/23** against a real database, covering both unique directions, the tenant floor with a second workspace under a different owner (provisioning is idempotent per user, so the same owner would have compared a workspace with itself), the acceptance criterion, and every delivery outcome. `channel-email.test.ts` 9/9. Full `packages/core` **84 files, 1560/1560**. adapters 134/134, config 80/80. End to end **72 passed**. typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 479 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check. **One pre-existing failure, not from this change:** `packages/db/test/pooling-spike.test.ts` 4/4 fail with `ECONNREFUSED` on port 56432, which is the PgBouncer container this machine does not run. |
| P5-T01b-b | Routing, quiet hours and the message builder | in_review | agung | 2026-08-27 | **A defect found while reading, and Agung approved fixing it before any code was written.** AI-NATIVE-PLAN §5.4 says a nudge inside quiet hours “queues to the next open window” and §11’s own test list says quiet hours *defer*. What shipped did the opposite: `suppressionFor` returned `quiet_hours`, the row was written with a reason, and nothing ever sent it. A member whose night covered the sweep hour never heard about their overdue check-in at all. Member quiet hours now defer through `deferralFor`; workspace quiet mode still suppresses, because an organisation that switched the product off for a week is not asking to be told everything at once when it comes back. Seven assertions across two files were written against the old behaviour and are corrected here with the reason beside them. **A nudge row is now the delivery queue.** `sent_at is null`, no suppression reason, and a `scheduled_for` that has passed means owed and undelivered. The run that decides *whether* the product speaks stopped stamping `sent_at`; the pass that decides *where* stamps it along with the channel. Before this, `nudges.channel` was written as the literal `in_app` by the run and resolved nowhere, with a comment saying it would be resolved at delivery. This is that delivery. **`resolveDelivery` is pure**, so “an unlinked member falls back to email” is three lines and no fixture. It names *which* of the two problems applies, because they are different: the settings screen tells a member to link their account and tells an administrator to connect the workspace, and one reason string could not do both. The channel is chosen before the delay is applied, so a member asleep with a broken Slack gets an email at seven rather than a Slack message at seven that would fail the same way. **In-app is written whatever the channel decides.** §5.4’s last line is that a snooze never silences a review-inbox obligation, and the same reasoning applies to routing. The inbox row moved into the delivery pass, so the row that says what was sent is written where the channel was chosen rather than in two places that could disagree. **The reconnect notice is a new proactive message kind, which Agung approved on 27 August 2026.** `channel.reconnect_needed` is in AI-NATIVE-PLAN §6.4’s catalogue, in `packages/method`’s trigger data and in the P4-T00 design document’s table, because the conformance suite compares all three. It is raised *before* suppression is decided, so “told once” falls out of §11’s own deduplication rather than a counter in the channel code: three runs the same day write three rows, one sent and two held with `dedup` on them. A silence the product can explain is the point of writing a row for a message it decided not to send. **The loop closes at the relay.** A channel send that fails marks its connection `error`, and routing reads `connected` only, so the next nudge for anybody on that provider goes to email and its owner is told once. Without that the same send would fail every hour and nobody would ever learn why. **The capability matrix has two homes and a test that fails when they drift.** `packages/core` holds it as data because the message builder runs on the deterministic path and must work with no driver installed; each driver reports its own row because a driver is what actually knows. Neither package may import the other, so the comparison lives in `apps/web`, the one place that legitimately depends on both. **What this task deliberately does not do: write the message.** Every channel message carries one line and its rule key. Per-rule wording is coaching copy and METHOD.md owns coaching copy; writing forty-five recipient-facing sentences inside a channel task would put the product’s voice outside the one document allowed to hold it. Named in the code and here rather than quietly shipped, and it needs a row of its own before any of this reads well to a person. **Verified:** `channel-routing.test.ts` 19/19 (pure), `nudge-delivery.test.ts` 9/9 against a real database including the acceptance criterion, `nudges.test.ts` 23/23 with the two rewritten assertions, `packages/method` 293/293 with the deferral tests, `apps/web` 31/31 including the capability drift test. Full `packages/core` **86 files, 1588/1588**. End to end **72 passed**. typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 485 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check at 45 trigger keys. **One flake, not from this change:** running `method`, `adapters` and `web` in one command failed two `PostgresSearch` tests; adapters alone passes 40/40. That is the known harness defect where parallel workers share one database. |
| P5-T01c | The session entry point | in_review | agung | 2026-08-27 | **Raised twice during Phase 4 and answered on 27 August 2026.** S-22 to S-25 were built across P4-T07 to P4-T10 and nothing in the interface linked to any of them, so `/session/<id>` was reachable only by typing it. Every session feature in the product was invisible to the people it was built for. Three doors now: a sidebar item, a card on the space home (which is the door S-22 itself describes), and a link on the front door. **The access rule was wrong in my first version, and the test that caught it is the one that matters.** I scoped `sessions.mine` with `accessScopeFilter` at `view`, the same filter the space list uses. Every space is readable at `view` on purpose, so that somebody can find one to join, which meant the list showed sessions the reader would then be refused when they clicked. `sessions.read` requires **space membership**, because a session is a room you are in rather than a page you can look at. The read now uses an inner join on `space_members`, which is that same rule in list form: a space the member is not in contributes no row at all, rather than a row something later has to remember to drop. A list that offers rows which refuse to open is worse than a shorter list. **Running first, then soonest.** A session in progress is the only row somebody is already waiting in, so it sorts above everything whatever the calendar says, and the row says which step the room is on and offers Rejoin rather than Open. Proved in a test that opens the *later* session and asserts it comes first. **Finished sessions are off by default**, behind a link. The front door is about what is next; what happened belongs to the scorecard and the digest. **A screen this specification did not number.** UIUX-PLAN’s S-22 puts the session’s door on a space home, which only helps a member who already knows which of their spaces has a session in it. The workspace-wide list is an addition, and it is recorded in UIUX-PLAN §6 beside S-22 as one rather than left undocumented. Worth a human deciding whether it earns a number of its own. **No create-session control**, and the end-to-end spec says so where it writes its fixture straight to the database. This task is the door, not the scheduler; a session is still created by the seeded cadence or by hand. That is the next gap on this screen. **Verified:** `sessions-mine.test.ts` **8/8** against a real database, covering the membership boundary in both directions, the running-first order, and the finished filter. Full `packages/core` **87 files, 1596/1596**. End to end **78 passed**, six of them new, including the acceptance criterion driven as clicks: `/` then Sessions then the row, with nothing typing an identifier. typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 486 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check. |
| P5-T02a | The Slack driver and its inbound door | in_review | agung | 2026-08-27 | **Cut out of P5-T02, and the cut exposed an ordering defect in the plan.** P5-T02's acceptance criterion is a check-in completed in a Slack modal, which needs the command router, and the router is P5-T06's own deliverable. The plan schedules P5-T06 *after* P5-T02, so P5-T02 as written could not be finished without building P5-T06 inside it, and a Slack-only router is the wrong shape for four providers. Recorded in IMPLEMENTATION-PLAN; P5-T02b now depends on both. **Agung approved building against fixtures on 27 August 2026**, with no Slack workspace and no app credentials. Every outbound call in the tests goes through a stubbed `fetch`. What that proves is that the driver sends the right body to the right method and reads the answer correctly; what it cannot prove is that Slack accepts it. The signature tests are different in kind: Slack's scheme is fully specified, so a forged header is refused in the test for exactly the reason it would be refused in production. **No SDK.** Slack's Web API is JSON over HTTPS and its signature is HMAC-SHA256 over three concatenated strings, so `fetch` and `node:crypto` cover it and no runtime dependency was added. **A defect this task created and its own test caught, before it shipped.** The inbound endpoint has to answer "which workspace is this?" before any of §6's eight steps make sense, and the first version asked `channel_connections` through the ordinary application role with no tenant setting. Forced row-level security answers that with nothing, every time, so no inbound message could ever have been accepted. The fix keeps the floor rather than lifting it: `channel_installations` (migration 0056) admits a row through `app.workspace_id` **or** through `app.channel_team_id` matching its own `external_team_id`, which is the arrangement `app.user_id` already has on `workspace_members` for the "which workspaces are mine" lookup. `withProviderTeam` is the only wrapper that sets it, the `with check` clause takes the tenant setting alone so nothing can be written through the second key, and a caller reaches exactly the row for a team id they already hold. §6's own table in the design document now has a step zero, because it was silent on this. **Steps three to six live in `packages/core`, not in the driver.** They are the same four questions for every provider, and a copy per driver is how they come to disagree. Steps one and two stay in the driver because the algorithm is the provider's own, and steps seven and eight are absent on purpose: this endpoint accepts and records, and does not act. **Every answer is an empty 200 except a failed signature.** §5.3's reason is that a helpful error confirms the workspace exists. A duplicate, an unknown sender, an unverified identity, a suspended member and a rate limit are indistinguishable from outside, and each is asserted separately so a handler that started replying "no such member" would fail rather than pass. **The order of the four checks is load-bearing and tested as such.** Deduplication is first, so a retry from an unlinked sender costs one indexed read. Identity resolution comes before the rate limit, so an unknown sender cannot consume a known member's budget: a test counts the limiter calls and requires zero. **Linking is a six-digit code, hashed, expiring and single-use** (migration 0055). Tests prove the code is never stored in the clear, that the same code cannot be used twice, that an expired one is refused, that asking again replaces the last one, and that a message which merely contains six digits links nothing. A second member sending their own code from the first member's Slack account is refused by the unique index, which is the database saying one account is one person rather than a check somebody could forget. **Identity resolution reads the provider's id and never the handle**, and there is a test that sends the handle as the sender id and gets silence. A handle is changeable, reusable and sometimes shared. **Slack errors are sorted into permanent and retryable.** `account_inactive` and six others carry `PermanentDispatchError`'s name, so the relay dead-letters them on the first attempt rather than proving the same thing ten times over an hour; a rate limit stays retryable. **The relay picks the driver from the provider the routing already chose**, and a provider with no driver suppresses with a reason rather than failing. Bot tokens are decrypted per delivery, not held: a process keeping every workspace's token resident is a process whose heap dump is a breach. **The capability matrix drift test now covers Slack as well as email.** **Not built, and this is the gap to decide next: there is no surface for connecting Slack.** `channels.connect` takes the credentials and the team id, and nothing in the interface calls it, so today Slack is connected by an action call. The self-serve OAuth install in this row's deliverables is not here either: it needs two instance settings and a callback route, and neither could be exercised at all without a Slack app, so it would have been unverifiable code standing between an administrator and a screen that also does not exist. The mockup for it is `09-channels`. Named here rather than quietly shipped. **Verified:** `channel-slack.test.ts` **27/27** against a stubbed `fetch`, `channel-inbound.test.ts` **23/23** against a real database, `apps/web` 32/32 including both capability drift tests, `packages/adapters` 161/161. Full `packages/core` **88 files, 1619/1619** (the registry invariant needed `channels.startLink` named as a fourth own-identity exception beside the other three channel actions). End to end **78 passed**. typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 491 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check, licences. **One pre-existing failure, not from this change:** `packages/db/test/pooling-spike.test.ts` 4/4 fail with `ECONNREFUSED` on port 56432, the PgBouncer container this machine does not run. |
| P5-T02c | The channel settings surface | in_review | agung | 2026-08-27 | **Cut out of P5-T02a, whose deliverables named a connection health card that was not built.** The mechanism was real and nothing in the interface reached it: Slack was connectable only through an action call. A mechanism nobody can reach is not shipped. **Two screens.** The workspace card at `/admin/channels` lists each provider with its state, when it last verified and the provider's own last complaint, and offers connect, disconnect and a test send. The member's own page at `/account/channels` carries the primary channel, quiet hours and the short-code linking flow, at `comment` level, because telling the product where to send its reminders cannot be a privilege only editors have. **No credential is ever rendered, and the form is never pre-filled.** The end-to-end spec asserts the bot token and the signing secret are absent from the page source, which is the one thing only a browser can prove. Replacing a credential means typing the new one. **Connected is not verified, and the card says which.** A provider with no driver yet says so instead of taking a credential, because storing a token nothing can use is worse than refusing it. **A defect in how confirmations were shown, found by the spec and fixed properly.** Connecting revalidates the page, which moves the form into a disclosure and unmounts it, so the success message rendered from `useActionState` could never be read. Rather than keep a message nobody sees, the confirmation now comes from server state: the card itself says the connection is stored and unverified while `last_verified_at` is null. The same reasoning was applied to saving quiet hours, where the saved values coming back on the inputs are the durable confirmation. The transient message field is for refusals, which do not revalidate, and both form components now say so. **Two more real defects the tests caught.** First, `people.updateOwnProfile`'s new quiet-hours regex lost its backslashes when it was written, so it matched the literal letter `d` and refused every real time; the browser only showed a value that never changed, which is the shape of a write that silently did nothing, and an isolated core test named the cause in seconds. `own-delivery.test.ts` now keeps a case for it. Second, the delivery form posted an empty `primaryChannel` whenever the member's stored channel had become unreachable, because a disabled radio submits nothing and the enum then refused the whole write, taking the quiet hours with it. It now sends only what the form actually submitted. **A three-month-old intermittent is diagnosed, and it was never flakiness.** `sessions.spec.ts`'s two-context test has failed about one run in five since P4-T07a and failed four of five once this task put ten more specs ahead of it. The cause is P2-T09's authentication rate limit, which is per address: by that point in the run the same account has signed in several times over, and the spec was spending the limit on setup. The second context now reuses the first one's session, which is what the acceptance criterion actually needs, two independent clients watching one session. The end-to-end suite is **88 passed** with nothing skipped and nothing intermittent. **A hydration race the spec would otherwise have blamed on the product.** Filling the quiet-hours inputs and clicking Save immediately raced hydration, which reset the uncontrolled inputs and posted the old window back. The spec asserts the filled value before submitting, which is what tells a fill that did not stick apart from a write that did not land. **Verified:** `own-delivery.test.ts` 4/4, full `packages/core` **89 files, 1623/1623**, `apps/web` 32/32, end to end **88 passed** including ten new channel specs. typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 499 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check. |
| P5-T02b | Slack buttons and the modal check-in | in_review | agung | 2026-08-27 | **Smaller than the row said, because most of it had already shipped.** P5-T06a delivered the slash command rendered from the router, §6's steps seven and eight, the refusal being the browser's own sentence, the unknown-command reply and the audit entry naming the channel; P5-T06b delivered a check-in across turns, which already worked on Slack. The row was re-sized to [S] and its deliverables corrected to the two things only a provider with a modal can do. **A modal is a nicer way to ask the same questions, not a second way to write a check-in**, and the acceptance test is a comparison rather than a fresh set of assertions: the conversational path and the form both end in `goals.startCheckIn` and `goals.publishCheckIn`, and the test asserts the same status, the same confidence, rich-text narrative on both, and the channel on every publication's audit row. If the two paths ever diverge, that test is what says so. **`triggerId` is on the port, not only on the Slack driver.** Teams has the same idea under another name, and the endpoint that decides "form or conversation" should not branch on which provider it is talking to. Its absence is what makes the conversation the fallback rather than an error: a trigger expires in about three seconds, and Slack having a bad second is not a reason a member cannot check in. There is a test for each of the three shapes that carries one and for the one that does not. **`openView` is on the driver and deliberately not on the port.** A port method every driver must implement and two of the four cannot is a port that lies. **A submitted form is not a message**, so it has its own type: nobody typed it, it has no text, and it carries one answer per field. A driver that flattened it into `text` would be inventing a sentence the member never said. Slack stores a select's answer under `selected_option.value` and a text input's under `value`; handling only one of the two is how a form silently loses a field, and there is a test for both. **Buttons that run a command are now Slack actions rather than links.** A button whose url is `okr:checkin <goal>` is posted back as a value, so pressing "Check in" on a nudge starts the flow in the conversation it arrived in; every other button is a real URL and stays one, because a link into the product is the right answer for a link into the product. The email driver turns the same button into a labelled link, which is why the scheme is a url rather than a second field on the message. **The key results are not on the form, and that is stated rather than hidden.** A form that silently wrote a number nobody typed would be worse than one that never asked, so a field the form did not carry is left absent and the action's own schema decides whether it was required. A test asserts the key result's `current_value` does not move. The values stay with the conversational path and the browser until the form grows the fields. **Verified:** `channel-slack.test.ts` **38/38** against a stubbed `fetch`, `chat-check-in.test.ts` **18/18** against a real database, full `packages/core` **91 files, 1660/1660**, `packages/adapters` 172/172, `apps/web` 32/32, end to end **88 passed**. typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 503 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check. **Still never spoken to Slack.** Every outbound call goes through a stubbed `fetch`, including `views.open`. The signature and payload-shape tests are exact; what no test here can prove is that Slack accepts the view. |
| P5-T03a | The Teams driver and its inbound door | in_review | agung | 2026-08-29 | **Cut in two along the same seam Slack was.** P5-T02a built a driver and an inbound door and P5-T02b built what only a rich surface can do. Teams divides the same way, and the acceptance criterion (an adaptive card carrying a blocker's age and a reassign action) belongs to the second because it is a card. IMPLEMENTATION-PLAN.md is corrected in this change; 117 tasks. **Nothing in packages/core changed to add a third provider, and that is the finding.** The command catalogue, the router, section 6's inbound steps three to six, the identity linking and the conversational check-in are the same code Slack and Telegram reach. A provider that had needed its own copy of any of them would have meant the design did not hold. **Teams is the first provider with real asymmetry between its two directions.** Slack signs inbound with a shared secret and authenticates outbound with a bot token; Telegram echoes a secret one way and puts a token in a URL the other. Teams does neither: outbound needs an OAuth2 client-credentials token fetched per hour, and inbound arrives as a token Microsoft signed which has to be verified against keys Microsoft publishes. No SDK: the wire protocol is JSON over HTTPS and the token is an ordinary RS256 JWT, so fetch and node:crypto cover both. **The service URL is the one genuinely new problem, and it shaped three things.** There is no fixed address to send a Teams message to: every inbound activity carries the serviceUrl for its own region and outbound has to go back to that one, with no way to look it up. So a workspace whose bot has never been messaged cannot be messaged, the driver suppresses with exactly that reason rather than failing, the inbound door records the URL on the connection (which is what rememberConnectionConfig exists for), and "say something to it" is a numbered step in the runbook rather than a footnote. **The token binds the service URL, and checking that is load-bearing.** Microsoft signs a serviceUrl claim into every inbound token. Comparing it against the activity's own is what stops a caller holding a valid token for some other bot from pointing this instance's replies at a host they control. It is the easiest of the five checks to leave out and the one whose absence is worst. The other four are the signature against a published key, the issuer, the audience and the expiry, and each has its own test naming its own reason. **The verification tests sign real tokens with a real key pair.** Microsoft's scheme is fully specified, so a token these tests forge is refused for exactly the reason a forged one would be refused in production: there is no stand-in anywhere in the signature path. A `none` algorithm, a key that is not published, an unreachable key endpoint and a service URL that differs only by a trailing slash are each covered. **One endpoint for every tenant, unlike Telegram.** Teams carries the Azure directory tenant on every activity, which is the same shape as Slack's team id, so the installation lookup is the existing one with a different provider and there is no path segment per workspace. **The connection form is the one every provider shares.** Teams calls its three values an Application (client) ID, a client secret and a Directory (tenant) ID; the boxes are still named for Slack and the card's hint does the translating, exactly as Telegram's already does. The manifest template and the runbook are in deploy/teams. **Not built, and named rather than quietly shipped: adaptive cards.** P5-T03a sends text, and a nudge's actions arrive as links under the message. A command button is left out of the text entirely rather than rendered as a link that does nothing when clicked. The driver already reads a card action's `value.command` as its text, so P5-T03b is the cards and not the routing. **Not verified against Microsoft, and cannot be here.** Every outbound call in the tests goes through a stubbed fetch, so what is proved is that the driver sends the right body to the right endpoint and reads the answer correctly. Whether Microsoft accepts it needs an Azure application registration, which this machine does not have. The signature tests are different in kind, for the reason above. **Verified:** packages/adapters channel-teams.test.ts **30/30**, packages/core channel-teams.test.ts **9/9** against a real database including the acceptance criterion (a command acted on and audited with the channel named), the capability drift test extended to a third driver, full packages/core **97 files, 1748/1748**, packages/adapters **230/230**, end to end **110 passed**. typecheck 11/11, biome, boundaries 539 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check, check:contract over both artifacts. **Not verified:** packages/db/test/pooling-spike.test.ts, which needs the PgBouncer container this machine does not run. |
| P5-T03b | Adaptive cards and card actions | in_review | agung | 2026-08-29 | **Two kinds of button, two kinds of action, and the difference is who acts.** A link button is somewhere to go, so it is an Action.OpenUrl and the browser does the work. A command button is something to run, so it is an Action.Submit carrying the command in its data, which arrives back on the activity's value and is read as the message's text. That is the same okr: scheme the Slack blocks and the Telegram keyboard already read, so a message is written once and rendered three ways with no branch on the provider anywhere. **A card only when there is something for a card to hold.** A plain reminder with no actions and no blocks stays a plain message: wrapping every sentence in a card would make an ordinary reply look like a form. The text is set either way, because it is what a notification preview and an accessibility reader use and what a client that cannot render the card falls back to. **The escalation card carries the blocker's own words and a computed age, not new coaching copy.** METHOD.md owns what the product says and per-rule wording is still a row of its own; what is added is the subject's own data, which is the difference between "you have a reminder waiting" and a message somebody can act on without opening anything. **sessions.reassignBlocker did not exist, and the acceptance criterion could not have been met without it.** The ladder ends at a coordinator, and the two useful answers are "it is done" and "I will take it". Closing had an action; taking on had none. Built here, with the new owner named rather than chosen inside it: the chat command passes the sender, a board would pass a third person, and neither is the action's decision. A suspended member cannot be handed work, and an already-closed blocker cannot be handed at all. **Two new chat commands, resolve and take, and a second tiny router table.** SESSION_BOUND is for commands that need a lookup; take needs no lookup at all, only the sender's member id in a named field. A command that took a member id from the line would be a command for handing work to other people from a phone, which is a board's job. **A latent defect found and fixed while wiring this: a command button printed as a link.** The builder's degradation for a provider with no buttons rendered every button as "Label: url", which for okr:resolve abc is a line somebody clicks and nothing happens. Nothing passed a command button through the builder before this task, so no message was ever broken by it; the escalation card reaching email would have been the first. A command button now degrades into the words to type, which every provider that can receive a message can receive. **The card is dropped and the facts survive.** For Telegram the blocks go and the same age, type, next action and rule key are still in the text; for a provider with no buttons the two actions become two lines saying what to reply. Three assertions cover the three shapes. **One test-only mistake worth recording, because it is the same shape as the one in the device login.** The test dated the blocker from the moment the file captured rather than from the row's own opened_at, which createBlocker stamps milliseconds later, so a twenty-six hour age floored to twenty-five. The row's moment is the only one the product will ever compare against. **Verified:** blocker-card.test.ts **15/15** against a real database including both halves of the acceptance criterion (resolve and take, each reaching the registry with the channel audited), packages/adapters channel-teams.test.ts **33/33** covering the card shape, the two action types and the plain-message case, full packages/core **98 files, 1763/1763**, adapters **233/233**, cli 59/59, apps/web 35/35, end to end **110 passed**. typecheck 11/11, biome, boundaries 540 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check, check:contract over both artifacts. **Not verified:** whether Teams renders the card as intended, which needs an Azure application registration; and packages/db/test/pooling-spike.test.ts, which needs the PgBouncer container this machine does not run. |
| P5-T04a | The WhatsApp driver and its inbound door | in_review | agung | 2026-08-29 | **Cut in two along a line this provider draws itself.** Sending and receiving a WhatsApp message is one session. The conversation window is a different problem: it decides what may be sent at all rather than how it looks, it needs the last inbound moment recorded per member, and it needs a registry of approved templates whose home is still an open question. The acceptance criterion belongs to the window because a template is what it is about. IMPLEMENTATION-PLAN.md is corrected in this change; 118 tasks. **The fourth provider, and again nothing in packages/core changed to add it.** The installation lookup, the router, the access check and the audit row are the code the other three reach. **The constraint is a new kind.** Slack, Teams and Telegram limit how a message looks; WhatsApp limits whether it may be sent at all. Outside a twenty-four hour window opened by the member's own last message, Meta carries only templates it approved in advance. The builder has known how to answer that since P5-T01b-b and the matrix has carried templateOnlyOutbound since then; what is missing is which side of the window a given member is on, which is P5-T04b. **The verification handshake is a GET, and it is the first inbound path in this product that is not a message.** Meta proves the endpoint belongs to whoever configured it by asking it to echo a challenge, with no body and no signature to verify, so it cannot go through section 6's order. It is answered by the endpoint itself against a token the administrator chose, compared in constant time. A wrong token and a number this instance does not know get the same refusal, and an instance with no token configured echoes nothing: otherwise anybody who guessed the URL could confirm the endpoint exists. **No buttons, and the degradation built at P5-T03b turns out to be this provider's native shape.** A command button folds into the text as `Resolve: reply "resolve abc"`, which on WhatsApp is the literal instruction. What was written last task to stop a broken link is what this provider wanted all along. **Three credentials in a form with two secret boxes.** The access token, the app secret and a verify token the administrator invents. The second box holds the last two separated by a space, because a third box for a value only one provider has would be a box every other provider's card had to explain away. The runbook in deploy/whatsapp says which is which, and says plainly that the verify token is not issued by Meta. **A defect caught by reading the file back rather than by a test.** The split regex was written through a shell heredoc as /s+/ instead of /\s+/, which would have split every secret on the letter s. Same trap as the quiet-hours regex at P5-T02c, and the same fix: write the file with an editor rather than through a heredoc. **Not verified against Meta, and cannot be here.** Every outbound call goes through a stubbed fetch, so what is proved is the body sent and the answer read. Whether Meta accepts it needs a Business account with a verified number. The signature and handshake tests are different in kind: the scheme is fully specified, so a body these forge is refused for exactly the reason a forged one would be refused in production. **A gap the runbook names rather than hides.** Until P5-T04b, this connection sends free-form messages, which Meta accepts inside the window and refuses outside it. That refusal is classified permanent so the message is dead-lettered rather than retried ten times, but a reminder to somebody who has not written in a day will not arrive. Written into deploy/whatsapp/README.md under its own heading. **Verified:** packages/adapters channel-whatsapp.test.ts **22/22**, packages/core channel-whatsapp.test.ts **9/9** against a real database including the acceptance criterion, the capability drift test extended to a fourth driver, packages/adapters **255/255**, apps/web **36/36**. typecheck 11/11, biome, boundaries 542 files, dead-code, both database linters, check:contract. **Not verified:** packages/db/test/pooling-spike.test.ts, which needs the PgBouncer container this machine does not run. |
| P5-T04b-a | Syncing Meta's approved templates | in_review | agung | 2026-08-30 | **Design C3 is corrected, and the correction came from Agung on 29 August 2026.** C3 put a per-nudge-kind template registry in packages/method, "because a template is a coaching message and §11 already owns those". Two things are wrong with that. §11 is the *threshold* registry, which holds numbers rather than words. And a template is not canon at all: it is registered and approved inside one customer's own Meta Business account, so two workspaces cannot share one and no document could name them for everybody. Agung's answer went further than the question and is what this row builds: sync the templates from Meta, let an administrator choose which to use, and support filling their variables from the product's own data. The first of those three is here; the other two are P5-T04b-b. **A mirror, replaced wholesale on every sync.** Nothing here is authored in this product, so a sync has no local edit to overwrite: it records what Meta says now and soft-deletes what Meta no longer lists. Soft rather than hard, and the unique index deliberately not partial on deleted_at, so one template is one row for the life of the workspace and a mapping that referred to a withdrawn one can still say which it meant. A template Meta lists again is the same row, back. **The business account is learned, not configured.** Meta puts it on entry[].id of every webhook body, so the inbound door records it the way the Teams service URL is recorded, after verification. Asking an administrator to find it in a console when the product is already told it every time somebody writes would be a form field for nothing. A workspace whose number has never received a message is told exactly that rather than shown an empty list that looks like an answer. **The fetch is in apps/web and the write is in the registry.** packages/core may not call a provider, so the settings screen asks the driver for the list and hands it to channels.syncTemplates, which is one Operation. The same division the relay already uses, and the reason a half-recorded list cannot leave a workspace offering templates that no longer exist beside ones that do. **listTemplates is on the driver and not on the Channel port.** No other provider has anything like it, and a port method three drivers answer "not applicable" to is a port that has stopped describing what a channel is. The one caller already knows it is looking at WhatsApp. **Variables are counted from the body, as the highest placeholder rather than the number of occurrences.** Meta numbers them from one with no gaps, and a body saying {{1}} twice still takes one parameter. Counting at sync time is what will let the mapping screen refuse a bad binding when it is saved rather than when a nudge is due at seven in the morning. **Paging is bounded at twenty pages.** Meta pages everything, and a workspace with sixty templates would otherwise silently see the first twenty-five. A cursor that never ends is a loop this process would not come back from, so a truncated list somebody can see is truncated is the better failure. **Templates that are not approved are kept and labelled.** An administrator wants to know their submission is pending rather than that it does not exist. Only approved ones will be offered for a mapping, because a pending one is a refusal moved to seven in the morning. **A pre-existing test finally broke on its own, and it is worth recording.** api-tokens.test.ts compared a token's expiry against two dates written out in full. It passed for two days and then started failing, because the world moved past them: the action computes the expiry from the real clock, as it must. Both moments now come from the row. This is the third time this shape has appeared on this branch (the device login, the blocker card, and now this), and the rule each time is the same: a test about before and after a stored moment must ask the row when that is. **Verified:** whatsapp-templates.test.ts **8/8** against a real database including the acceptance criterion, the withdraw-and-restore cycle and the tenant floor; packages/adapters channel-whatsapp.test.ts **29/29** including Meta's paging, the endless-cursor bound and the variable counting; full packages/core **100 files, 1780/1780**; end to end **109 passed**. typecheck 11/11, biome, boundaries 544 files, dead-code, both database linters, check:contract over both artifacts. **Not verified:** whether Meta returns what the parser expects, which needs a Business account; and packages/db/test/pooling-spike.test.ts, which needs the PgBouncer container this machine does not run. |
| P5-T04b-b | The mapping, the variables and the window | in_review | agung | 2026-08-30 | **The second half of Agung's answer to C3, and it changed one command for every provider.** `checkin` no longer requires a goal identifier. One check-in due starts it; none says so; two or more lists the titles and asks which, because picking the older one would be the product deciding what somebody meant about the thing it is asking them to be honest about. **Agung asked whether the specs require variable substitution at all, and they do not.** REQUIREMENTS line 137 says "template-based nudges out, conversational check-in and blocker capture in", and AI-NATIVE-PLAN line 156 says templates are pre-registered per nudge kind. Neither mentions variables. It is built anyway, and the reason is Meta rather than the plan: Meta refuses a send that omits parameters for a template that declares them, so a customer whose approved template already contains `{{1}}` would otherwise be told their own template is unusable. A closed five-source vocabulary checked when the mapping is saved is a better answer than that. **Five sources, not an expression language.** `member.name`, `workspace.name`, `subject.title`, `rule.key` and `reply.command`. An administrator picks one per placeholder from a select. Anything evaluated at send time would be a second query language living in a settings screen, and every value it could reach is already in that list. Every source resolves to a non-empty string, because Meta refuses a blank parameter and losing a whole reminder over an unnamed member is the wrong trade. **`reply.command` is the source that makes the flow work.** WhatsApp has no buttons, so a template that wants an answer has to say what to type. It resolves to the command with the identifier in it, which is what turns "your check-in is due" into something answerable in one reply, and it is why the bare `checkin` verb had to exist as well. **Every check happens when the mapping is saved, not when a nudge is due.** The approval, the parameter count and the source names. The two moments a check could happen are "an administrator is looking at the screen" and "it is seven in the morning and a reminder did not arrive". The screen makes all three refusals unreachable in the ordinary case, which is why they come back as `not_found` carrying the sentence that says which: a refusal here means the form is stale. **Two defects found on the way, both older than this task and both fatal to it.** The outbox relay dropped `templateKey` and refused any message with no text, so a template message would have been permanently dead-lettered with "the message row has no text to send". And `queueChannelMessageInTx` did not carry `templateParameters` onto the log row. Neither had ever fired, because nothing before this ever set `insideConversationWindow: false`: the builder had known how to answer the window since P5-T01b-b and nothing had ever asked it. **A rule with no template mapped queues nothing and counts the member as unreachable.** The inbox row is still written, because the obligation lives in the product and the channel is only where the product goes to find somebody. Sending the body anyway would be a message Meta bounces; sending it as a second free-form message would be one the member never agreed to receive. **`last_inbound_at` is stamped for every provider and read by one.** The column is on the identity, and one code path is cheaper to keep right than four. Deriving the window from the message log instead would be the same fact read the slower way, once per outbound message. **Verified:** whatsapp-mappings.test.ts **12/12** against a real database; nudge-delivery.test.ts **13/13** including the acceptance criterion end to end (the template is sent, and its own `reply.command` parameter fed back through the router starts the conversation); chat-check-in.test.ts **22/22** including the four bare-`checkin` outcomes; channel-inbound.test.ts **25/25** including the window's clock; packages/adapters channel-whatsapp.test.ts **31/31** including the components payload and the empty-parameters case.
