| P5-T05 | Telegram driver | in_review | agung | 2026-08-27 | **The second provider, and the first real test of "one definition, four renderings".** Nothing in `packages/core` changed to add it. The command catalogue, the router, §6's checks three to six, the conversational check-in and the routing layer are the same code Slack reaches; what is new is one driver and one endpoint. That is the claim the design made in P5-T00 and this is the first evidence for it. **Three things are genuinely different from Slack, and each is named rather than papered over.** The bot token goes in the URL rather than a header, so it is a credential in a path and the error messages name the method and never the URL: there is a test that asserts a refusal message does not contain the token. Inbound is verified by a shared secret rather than a signature, because Telegram does not sign the body at all, and a test states the consequence out loud: a tampered body under a valid secret passes here and fails on Slack. `callback_data` is capped at 64 bytes, so a button carrying a command too long to fit is dropped rather than truncated, because half a command is a command that would run the wrong thing. All three are recorded in the design document's own §6 table and capability matrix. **The workspace comes from the URL, because Telegram never says which bot an update was for.** Slack puts a team id on every payload; a Telegram update carries the chat and the sender and nothing about the receiving bot. So each workspace registers its webhook at `/api/channels/telegram/<bot id>` and the segment resolves through the same `channel_installations` row Slack uses. The shared secret then proves the caller knows what only Telegram was told. **The endpoint shape was extracted at the second copy, not the first.** The Slack endpoint was written whole, because one copy of something is not a pattern. `lib/channel-inbound.ts` now holds every step that is not a provider's own: read the bytes once, resolve the workspace, open the connection, verify before parsing, run §6's steps three to six, answer. Two more providers are coming, and two places where a security step could be reordered is one too many. What stays per endpoint is how the workspace is identified, how the secret is shaped, which driver is built, and whether the provider offers a form. **Telegram has no modal, so a check-in there is the conversational path**, which is the same code a member on Slack reaches when their trigger has expired. Nothing was written for it. **The admin card offers it, and one field means two things.** Slack signs the body and Telegram echoes a secret it was given, so the second field is labelled "signing or webhook secret" and each driver parses its own shape out of one stored string. The end-to-end spec now scopes every locator to a provider's own form, because two of the four have drivers and two forms are on the page. **Verified:** `channel-telegram.test.ts` **28/28** against a stubbed `fetch`, `packages/adapters` **200/200**, `apps/web` 33/33 including a third capability drift test, full `packages/core` **91 files, 1660/1660**, end to end **89 passed**. typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 506 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check. **Never spoken to Telegram either.** Every outbound call goes through a stubbed `fetch`. The secret comparison and the payload shapes are exact; what no test here can prove is that Telegram accepts a `sendMessage` body. A bot token is easy to obtain, so this is the provider worth trying against the real thing first. |
| P5-T06a | The command router and the one-line commands | in_review | agung | 2026-08-27 | **Cut out of P5-T06, and the cut corrected a dependency that was backwards.** The row read "depends on P5-T02, P5-T03, P5-T04, P5-T05", all four drivers, while P5-T02's own acceptance criterion needed the router. The design document says the opposite of that line, "one definition, four renderings", so the router is what a driver consumes and it needs exactly one driver to be provable. Each later driver inherits the surface rather than extending it. **The seam is a command that is one line against a command that is a conversation.** Status, ask, ack and snooze carry everything they need in the text somebody typed and are built. Check in, blocker and commit collect fields, which is a modal on Slack and Teams and §8's state machine on the other two, and they are P5-T06b. **The refusal is the browser's own sentence because it is the browser's own code path.** Nothing in the router checks access: the command names a registry action, `callAction` runs it, `can()` decides. The acceptance test proves this the only way worth proving it, by calling the action directly as the same member, catching the refusal, and asserting the chat reply is that string **word for word**. A router with wording of its own would be a second answer to "who may do this" and the two would drift within a release. **The channel goes on the audit row in one place, not forty.** `ActionCallContext` carries it, the Operation pipeline merges it into the audit payload, and every action is answerable a quarter later without knowing channels exist. The payload is inside the hash chain, so it is as tamper-evident as the rest of the row and needed no migration to an append-only table. A browser write leaves the field absent rather than saying "browser", because inventing a value for the ordinary case would make every row look like it had a channel; both directions are asserted. **Two corrections to the design document's own command table, made here.** Its access column was a second home for a number the registry already declares, so it is now documentation asserted by nothing. And `status` and `ask` each listed several actions, while the invariant is exactly one per command; a test walks the catalogue against `ACTION_MAP` so a renamed action breaks the build rather than the product, at the keystroke rather than in a chat window. **The catalogue was wrong about `nudges.snooze` and a test caught it.** It takes `{nudgeId, until}`, not the `{id, hours}` the first version sent, so the command would have failed schema validation the first time anybody typed it. Fixing it raised the real question: `until` is arithmetic on a moment, and nothing in this product reads a clock inside its own logic. `toInput` and the router now take `now` from the caller, and a test asserts the stored `snoozed_until` is exactly forty-eight hours after the moment supplied rather than after whenever the test ran. **A test of mine passed vacuously and was replaced.** The audit assertion was guarded on "if anything was audited", which passes for a router that does nothing at all. It now runs a real nudge sweep, snoozes a real nudge from chat, and asserts one audit row with the channel on it, unconditionally. **Silence is still for strangers, and a member always gets a sentence.** A duplicate, an unknown sender and a suspended member stay silent, which is §6's rule and §5.3's reason. A rate limit and any command answer, because by then the sender is somebody the product knows. An unknown verb names what is available instead of being a dead end, and a missing argument says which one. **Verified:** `chat-commands.test.ts` **19/19** against a real database, full `packages/core` **90 files, 1642/1642**. typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 501 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check. **One thing to know about the harness, not about this change.** An earlier full `packages/core` run reported three failures in `check-ins.test.ts` and `channels.test.ts` with per-test durations of thirty to sixty seconds. All three pass in isolation and the suite ran clean at 1642/1642 on the next attempt in a third of the wall time: it was contention from overlapping runs on one database, which is the harness defect recorded at P4-T06c rather than anything in this change. Named here because three red tests that mean nothing cost the next person an hour. |
| P5-T06b | The conversational check-in | in_review | agung | 2026-08-27 | **Narrowed from three commands to one, and the reason is in the actions.** `sessions.createBlocker` needs a session, a key result, a type, an owner and a next action; `sessions.setCommitments` needs a session and a list. Both are bound to a running session, and nothing in the design says how a chat message finds one: the sender names a key result, not a session. A check-in needs a goal and three answers, all of which somebody can give from a phone. The other two are now P5-T06c, with the missing lookup named as its first deliverable. **The state is a row, which is the whole reason `channel_conversations` exists.** Either the web process or the relay can restart between two messages, so a half-finished check-in held in memory is lost by a deploy. `collected` holds the answers and nothing else in the product reads it; the two registry actions run once, when every field is in. **Nothing partial is ever stored, and four tests hold that.** An abandoned conversation leaves no check-in, the goal's `next_check_in_at` does not move, an expired one is not a conversation at all, and a reply that is not an answer ends it with "Nothing was saved" rather than re-asking, because a machine that kept re-asking would trap somebody in a loop they did not start. **The expiry is a §4.14 setting, not a §11 threshold, and the design document is corrected.** It said METHOD.md §11's registry. §11 is the OKR practice canon, and how long a chat window stays open is an interaction timeout rather than a practice rule, so it is `chatConversationMinutes` with a thirty-minute default that a workspace can still change. The clock restarts on every answer rather than running for the whole conversation: somebody answering slowly is still answering, and a test proves twenty minutes per answer is fine. **A real interaction defect, found by a test and fixed at the cause.** The first build read replies before commands, so a member who typed `checkin` while one was already half finished had it abandoned *and* nothing started, which is the worst of both. A message that parses as a known command now ends the conversation and then runs; anything else is read as an answer, which is why "8" and "skip" are never met with "I do not have an 8 command". Recorded in the design's own abandoning row. **Access is checked before a single question is asked.** `goals.startCheckIn` runs at the start rather than at the end, so a member who may not check a goal in is refused immediately. Asking three questions and then refusing would waste somebody's time and teach them the product does not know its own rules. **A person answers in a person's units.** Confidence is 0 to 10 and stored as the 0 to 1 the action takes; status accepts "on track", "On Track", "on_track" and "off-track"; a key-result value takes "skip" for somebody who cannot look one up, because §3.2 puts the values last exactly so that a member can still finish. The narrative goes through the shared rich-text module rather than hand-built editor JSON. **The acceptance criterion is proved against the database, not against the reply.** One published check-in with the status and confidence on it, the key result's `current_value` moved, one `check_in_snapshots` entry naming that key result, the cadence advanced, the conversation row gone, and the audit row carrying `channel: slack`. **Verified:** `chat-check-in.test.ts` **14/14**, full `packages/core` **91 files, 1656/1656**, `packages/adapters` 161/161, `apps/web` 32/32, end to end **88 passed**. typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 503 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check. **A note on how the suite was run, because it cost time.** Two full `packages/core` runs reported failures, forty-one of them at worst, with per-test durations of thirty to sixty seconds; both were runs I had started while typecheck, lint and dead-code were also going, all against one database. With nothing else running the same suite passes 1656/1656 in a third of the wall time. That is the contention recorded at P4-T06c and it is self-inflicted, not a defect in the work. |
| P5-T06c | The session-bound chat commands | in_review | agung | 2026-08-27 | **The lookup the design left open is now stated once, in code and in the document.** §7 listed both commands and said nothing about how a chat message finds a session, which is why P5-T06b left them out rather than guessing inside another task. The rule: a sender who names a key result gets the running session in that key result's goal's space; a sender who names nothing gets the running session in the one space they are in that has one. **Only a running session.** A blocker raised into a scheduled session is a blocker nobody is in the room for, and a commitment made into a closed one is against a week that has finished. Both refuse, and the refusal says which of the two reasons it is. **Two running sessions refuse rather than pick.** The product cannot ask Telegram which one somebody meant, and choosing would put a blocker on the wrong team's board. The reply says to name a key result, which is the thing that disambiguates it. **Both commands are one line, and that is a decision rather than a shortcut.** The machine for a conversation exists and was used for `checkin`. A blocker's type is one word out of five and its next action is a sentence, and somebody raising a blocker is doing it during a session, on a phone, while a room waits: three exchanges to say one thing is worse there than one line that needs its words in order. Recorded in the design document beside the seam it changed. **The session lookup lives in the router, not in the catalogue.** `toInput` is pure by design, parsed arguments and a moment, and a session is a query. A table maps each session-bound verb to what the sender names and what the action needs beyond it, so a third one is a row rather than a third branch. **Both put the sender down as the owner**, because a blocker somebody raises is theirs and a commitment somebody makes is theirs. **`setCommitments` appends rather than replacing**, despite the name, so one commitment from chat adds one row and a second does not wipe the first. There is a test for that, because the name says otherwise. **Found and not fixed, recorded rather than quietly left.** `sessions.setCommitments` computes `weekStart` from `new Date()` inside the action. Nothing in this product reads a clock inside its own logic, and here it means a commitment made late on a Sunday evening in Jakarta lands in the wrong week: the server's date is not the workspace's. It is P4-T08's code and fixing it changes the action's input shape, so it belongs to its own row rather than to a channel task. **Verified:** `chat-session-commands.test.ts` **12/12** against a real database, including the acceptance criterion read back through `blockers.board` (the board the space page shows) as well as off the row. Full `packages/core` **92 files, 1672/1672**, end to end **89 passed**. typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 507 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check. |
| P5-T07a | The REST surface and its tokens | in_review | agung | 2026-08-28 | **Cut in three, along what each part can be used without.** This row listed a REST surface, scoped tokens, a generated OpenAPI document, a generated command line and a drift check. A surface with tokens is usable the day it lands, with curl and nothing else. A generated document needs a surface to describe, and the drift check is this task's acceptance criterion, so the generator and the check that proves it belong together. A command line needs both and adds a device login, which is a flow rather than a projection. IMPLEMENTATION-PLAN.md is corrected in this change; 115 tasks. **Every route is a projection of the registry, including the parts that usually get hand-written.** The path is the action's name with dots as slashes, the method comes from the safety class, the scope is the same word again, and the parameter list is the input schema's own keys. A resource-shaped surface would have needed a hand-written mapping from three hundred actions onto nouns and verbs, and that mapping is the thing that goes stale with no test able to prove it complete. **The method is a safety property, not a style.** A GET is retried by proxies and prefetched by browsers, so deriving it from the safety class means a write can never be reachable by GET. A mismatch is 405. **A token is a person, narrowed.** It carries the authority of the member who minted it and its scopes narrow that further; can() still decides. A write-scoped token in a view-level member's hands writes nothing. That is what makes it safe for any member to mint one without an administrator, and it is why there is no service account and no access level on a token. **The audience is a column, not a prefix.** Section 14 asks for REST and MCP tokens to be separated. The token's text carries okr_rest_ or okr_mcp_ so a person can tell two of their own apart and a wrong-door request can be refused without a query, but a string a caller presents is not evidence, so the stored audience is the answer. A REST token is refused at the agent endpoint before P5-T08 exists to have one. **api_tokens is the second table in the product read before a tenant is known.** A request arrives with a bearer token and nothing else, so forced row-level security would answer the lookup with nothing. Its policy takes a second key, app.api_token_hash, set only by withApiToken, admitting exactly the row whose digest the caller already holds. Same arrangement as channel_installations at P5-T02a, and the with-check clause takes the tenant setting alone so nothing can be written through the second key. **A suspended member's tokens stop working with nothing revoked.** Membership is resolved on every call, so the hard rule about excluding suspended members holds here without a revocation sweep having to remember. **Filtering is the action's own input, and that is a deliberate reading of section 14 rather than an omission.** Section 14 asks for a filter grammar matching the list contracts. The list contracts already express their filters as declared, typed, individually documented input fields: goals.list takes cycleId, includeClosed, level, spaceId, health and mine, each enforced by the action. A ?filter=field:op:value syntax on top would have exactly one operator with a consumer, because no list action accepts a range or a set, and would give a caller two ways to ask one question with two chances to disagree. What makes the declared input a contract rather than a coincidence is the refusal: ?spaceID= is answered by name with the list of what the action does take, rather than dropped. Recorded as a reading in docs/design/p5-t07-api-design.md section 2, and worth a human's confirmation. **Paging is declared per action and never faked in the transport.** An action that takes a cursor declares which fields of its last item form the next one; an action that does not is answered unsupported_parameter on ?cursor=. Loading everything and slicing it here would cost the same query and tell the caller it had not. Only activities.workspaceFeed declares it today, because it is the only registry action that already takes a cursor. Adding paging to another list is a change to that list's query and belongs to the task that needs it. **Forbidden has already collapsed before the transport sees it.** Section 14 says forbidden collapses to not-found for invisible resources, and that happens in can() and the access getter, so this layer never holds the information that could leak. What reaches it as forbidden is the other case, visible at an insufficient level, and flattening that would be a lie in the other direction. **The order of a request is not rearrangeable, and one test proves the scope gate runs early.** Authenticate, resolve, rate limit, route, method, scope, input, act. Routing before authenticating would let anyone with a network path enumerate an instance's actions one 404 at a time. The acceptance test sends a write whose body is deliberately incomplete: a scope check that ran second would answer 422 about the fields instead of 403 about the scope, so the assertion that there are no fields on the error is the assertion that the gate is where it says it is. **Renamed apiTokens.* to tokens.* before shipping.** The registry invariant test requires a lowercase domain, and it caught the camelCase one immediately. Worth noting because the projections all derive their paths from that name. **A real defect found while making this task's first HTTP request, and fixed here.** apps/web/proxy.ts redirects any request with no session cookie to /sign-in. Two surfaces authenticate themselves instead: the REST surface resolves a bearer token, and the inbound channel webhooks verify a provider signature over the raw bytes. Neither sends a cookie. So before this change every inbound Slack and Telegram request was answered with a 307 to the sign-in page, and nothing the channel layer does could ever have run in a deployed instance. The unit tests call the handler function directly and could not see it. Both prefixes are on the public list now, with a test, and the reason is written on the list. **A second real defect, in the end-to-end harness.** Better Auth allows ten sign-ins per address per minute (P2-T09), the suite runs single-worker as one account, and this task's spec was the tenth file to sign in. The refusal lands on whichever file is running when the limit trips, which is how it read as flakiness in sessions.spec.ts twice. e2e/instance-account.ts now authenticates once and restores the cookies for every later spec, which is what a browser does anyway, and leaves the limit to the specs that are about it. **Both account pages were unreachable from the interface.** /account/channels shipped at P5-T02c with no link to it from anywhere, and this task's page would have been the second. The avatar menu now takes a list of account pages rather than one href named after its own destination, which is how a menu ends up with pages that exist and cannot be reached. **Verified:** api-tokens.test.ts **17/17** and api-surface.test.ts **16/16**; full packages/core **94 files, 1705/1705**; e2e/s37-api-tokens.spec.ts **12/12** including the acceptance criterion over real HTTP with the absence of the write asserted afterwards, and the whole end-to-end suite **101 passed**; packages/adapters 200/200, packages/method 293/293, apps/web including the new proxy case. typecheck 10/10, biome, boundaries 517 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check, check:licences. **Not verified:** packages/db/test/pooling-spike.test.ts (4 tests), which needs the PgBouncer port from the Docker stack this machine does not run. Unrelated to this change and failing the same way before it. |
| P5-T07b | The generated OpenAPI document and the drift check | in_review | agung | 2026-08-28 | **The document is built from the same routes the transport serves**, so it cannot describe an action the surface does not have or miss one it does. The path, the method, the operation id, the scope and the safety class all come from the route; the query parameters, the request body and the response shape come from the action's own Zod schemas through z.toJSONSchema, which emits JSON Schema 2020-12, the dialect OpenAPI 3.1 uses. No shape is described twice anywhere in the repository. **One script in two modes, and that is the point.** gen:contract writes contract/openapi.json and check:contract compares a fresh document against it. A separate generator and checker are two programs that can disagree about what the artifact should be, and then the check passes on a file the generator would never write. **The failure names the action.** A byte difference is the trigger and the message is a per-action diff, so a stale document reports "changed: goals.create" rather than a JSON path. A change outside paths is reported against the document itself, which is rare and worth saying plainly rather than hiding among 265 actions. **The served document is generated on request, not read from the artifact.** GET /api/v1/openapi.json calls the same builder, so it describes the running instance whatever version it is on, and the committed copy is kept honest by the gate rather than by being the source. **The error responses are shared through $ref**, which took half a megabyte of repeated boilerplate out of the committed file. The artifact exists so a change to the public surface shows up in a diff a person reads; nine identical response blocks repeated 265 times work against exactly that. **A real defect the generator found, fixed here.** comments.list declared editedAt and createdAt as z.date(). No JSON response can carry a Date: the REST surface has always sent a string there and the schema said otherwise, and the goal page was calling .toISOString() on the way into its own component. They are ISO strings now, declared where the contract is declared rather than converted past it. It is the only schema in the registry that resisted conversion, and it resisted because it was wrong. **Not done, and deliberately.** The document does not carry examples, and no list action other than activities.workspaceFeed declares paging. Both are per-action work rather than generator work: an example belongs beside the schema that needs one, and paging a list is a change to that list's query. P5-T07c adds the command line's own artifact to the same run and the same check. **Two mistakes of mine in the CI work that ran alongside this, both recorded rather than smoothed over.** The per-shard ports were written as an arithmetic expression, and GitHub Actions expressions have no arithmetic operators, so the workflow file was rejected whole and zero jobs ran until it was fixed; the ports are written out in the matrix now, and both workflow files plus the compose file were parsed and inspected before the second push. Then the contract drift gate landed in that workflow-fix commit by accident, one commit ahead of the script it calls, so that single commit's run is red for a command that does not exist yet. The branch head is green; the intermediate commit is not, and rewriting a pushed history behind an open pull request would have been the worse trade. **Verified:** openapi.test.ts **17/17**, including the acceptance criterion (a staged stale document, and the check naming goals.create), plus added, removed, whole-document and multiple-action cases and a determinism check. Full packages/core **95 files, 1722/1722**. End to end **102 passed**, including a new case that reads the served document and asserts it describes this instance. typecheck 10/10, biome (13 warnings, 0 errors), boundaries 519 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check, and check:contract itself: 265 actions described, committed document agrees. **Not verified:** packages/db/test/pooling-spike.test.ts, which needs the PgBouncer container this machine does not run. |
| P5-T07c-a | The generated command line | in_review | agung | 2026-08-28 | **Cut in two, because the login is a protocol and the rest is a tool.** The row listed a generated command line and a browser device login. The command line is an artifact, a flag parser and an HTTP client, usable the day it lands with a token from the account screen. A device login is a device code table with its own row-level security, two endpoints, an approval screen and a polling loop. IMPLEMENTATION-PLAN.md is corrected in this change; 116 tasks. **The tool reads contract/cli.json and nothing else.** @openokr/core appears in exactly one type-only import, which TypeScript erases, so the types are declared once in the generator and the terminal tool carries no Drizzle, no Postgres driver and no domain code. packages/cli has no runtime dependencies at all. That is also what makes the drift check mean something: a tool that imported the registry would have nothing to drift from. **Both artifacts are generated in the same run**, so they cannot describe different registries, and check:contract compares both and names what moved in each: an action for the document, a command for the command list. **Everything the tool can be sure about is decided before a socket opens.** An unknown flag, a value the type refuses, an enum value not in the list, a required flag absent: each is exit 2 with the flag named and what the command actually takes. Two end-to-end assertions count the requests the stub server saw and require zero. What the generator could not read arrives as a string and is passed through, because a command line that refuses what the API would accept is worse than one that lets the server answer. **Three exit codes that mean different things.** 0 is the answer, 2 is a usage error decided locally, 1 is the instance refusing or unreachable. A script that retries on 1 and gives up on 2 is doing the right thing, which is the point of separating them. **The instance's own sentence is what a person reads.** A revoked token says it was revoked. A tool that classified the status itself would say "authentication failed" and send somebody hunting for a typo in a token that is perfectly well formed; there is a test asserting the word "authentication" does not appear. **Nothing prints a token back, including the command that was just given one.** login answers with a sixteen-character hint. The config file is owner-readable only where the platform has mode bits, and the comment says plainly that Windows has none that chmod reaches rather than pretending otherwise. **Clean JSON on stdout**, so a pipe into jq works. The one thing said alongside an answer, the next cursor, goes to stderr for exactly that reason. **A file input, because a shell cannot carry a JSON document.** --flag @path reads it, and @@ escapes a value that really starts with an at sign. **Verified:** packages/cli **44/44**, including a real node:http server rather than a stubbed fetch, because what is being tested is the request itself and a stub would be the test asserting its own expectations. End to end **104 passed**, with two new cases that mint a token through the browser and then spawn the bin as a process against the running instance: the acceptance criterion, and the instance's refusal carried through. Full packages/core 95 files 1722/1722 unchanged by this task. typecheck 11/11 with the new package, biome, boundaries 527 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check, and check:contract: 265 actions and 265 commands, both committed files agreeing. **Not verified:** packages/db/test/pooling-spike.test.ts, which needs the PgBouncer container this machine does not run. **One assumption corrected by its own test.** The first refusal case used goals.create, which has more required flags than the line supplied, so the tool refused it locally with exit 2 and never reached the server the test was about. Swapped for people.updateOwnProfile, whose flags are all satisfiable, so the assertion is about the server's answer rather than about the parser. |
| P5-T07c-b | The browser device login | in_review | agung | 2026-08-29 | **RFC 8628's device authorization grant, narrowed.** A terminal cannot open a browser and a browser cannot write to a terminal, so the two meet through a row: the terminal creates a request and polls it, a person approves it in a browser they are already signed in to, and the terminal never sees a password. **The token is minted at poll time and never stored.** Approval records who and when. The poll claims the row and mints the token in one transaction, with consumed_at is null in the where clause, so a code approved twice grants once and no granted credential ever waits in a short-lived table to be collected. **What is granted is what was asked, because approval takes no scopes.** The approve action's input is a code and one bit. There is no field through which a grant could be widened, which is a stronger guarantee than checking afterwards that none was. The end-to-end test asks for read only and then asserts a write is refused for scope. **The workspace is learned rather than given, and that shaped three things.** A terminal has not chosen a workspace; the approver does, by being in it. So the row starts with workspace_id null, the table takes app.device_code_hash as a second policy key (the third table in the product read before a tenant is known, after channel_installations and api_tokens), and OperationSpec gained a deviceCodeHash field because the approving write puts a workspace onto a row the tenant setting cannot yet see. Starting a request does not go through the pipeline and is marked as an allowed exception with its reason: there is no actor and no workspace for a pipeline to authorise against, and nothing is granted by it. **Both codes are hashed, and neither is stored in the clear.** The long one stays in the terminal, the short one goes in the URL. The alphabet has no 0/O and no 1/I/L, and the hash normalises case and whitespace, because a person retypes the short one. **The refusals are the protocol's own words.** authorization_pending, slow_down, expired_token and access_denied come back as the error.code this surface uses everywhere else, so a client that already speaks RFC 8628 needs no special case and one that speaks only this API's error shape needs none either. slow_down doubles the wait rather than incrementing it, because a client that is too fast is usually much too fast. **The default is read and write, never destructive.** A login should not quietly acquire the scope that removes things other people can see. **Three defects of mine, each found by a test rather than by reading.** The tests pinned a fixed clock while the approve action reads the real one, as it must, so every request in the file was already expired: ten failures that all said the same thing. The poll set the tenant setting *after* the claim update, and the database refused the update, which is the row-level security floor doing exactly its job. And the token hint printed the first sixteen characters unconditionally, which for a sixteen-character token is the whole token; it is bounded at half the length now, because a rule that holds only for values of the expected length is not a rule. **A fourth, in the approval screen, and it is the same one this branch has now hit three times.** The decide action revalidated the page, the pending read then found nothing, the card holding the form was replaced, and the form was unmounted along with the only confirmation the flow has. Unlike the channel and token screens there is nowhere else for that message to live: the request is gone once it is answered. So this action deliberately does not revalidate, and the comment says why. **Verified:** device-login.test.ts **17/17** against a real database, covering the three test-plan lines (the scopes asked for and no more, an expired code refused, a code approved twice granting once) plus slow_down, denial, the audit payload and the tenant floor. packages/cli **59/59**, with the clock and the browser passed in so the poll loop is tested without waiting or opening anything. End to end **110 passed**, including the acceptance criterion: the login spawned as a real process whose link is read from its own output, approved in a real browser, and the next command run from the profile it wrote. typecheck 11/11, biome, boundaries 537 files, dead-code, both database linters, method:check, check:contract over both artifacts. **Not verified:** packages/db/test/pooling-spike.test.ts, which needs the PgBouncer container this machine does not run. |
| P5-T08a | The grant, the codes and the token endpoint | in_review | agung | 2026-08-30 | **P5-T08 was cut in three, along what a client can complete without.** One [L] row held four separable things: the token machinery, the documents that let a client find it, the screen a person approves it on, and a client registry. Nothing in the second or third can be built before the first, and the first is provable on its own against a hand-registered client, which is exactly what the static allow-list is for. IMPLEMENTATION-PLAN.md is corrected in this change; 121 tasks. **Five tables, because the flow has five lifetimes.** A client lives for years, a grant until somebody ends it, a code for a minute, an access token for an hour, and a refresh token until its first use. Collapsing any two puts two lifetimes in one row. **A fourth pre-tenant key, and the first one three tables share.** The token endpoint receives a secret and nothing else: which workspace it belongs to is the question. Codes, access tokens and refresh tokens each admit the row whose own hash equals `app.oauth_secret_hash`, the same shape `channel_installations`, `api_tokens` and `device_authorisations` already use. Sharing the setting name widens nothing, because each policy still compares against its own column. **Every dangerous moment in this protocol is a race, so every one of them is a claim inside a transaction.** A code is consumed in the same transaction that mints the tokens and conditioned on still being unconsumed; a refresh token is claimed before anything is minted and conditioned on still being unused. Two concurrent redemptions cannot both pass, and neither can two concurrent refreshes. **A replayed secret is evidence, not an error, and that applies to codes as well as refresh tokens.** The specification only insists on it for refresh tokens. A code redeemed twice means the tokens the first redemption produced are already in somebody else's hands, so the grant ends there too. Revoking one link and leaving the chain would refuse one request and change nothing an attacker cares about. **An API token is not an MCP token, and the rule is not a comparison anybody could forget to write.** They live in different tables with different prefixes, so presenting one where the other belongs finds nothing at all. **The allow-list is a fallback in the lookup rather than a seeding step.** A seed has to run somewhere, and the only place that runs once per instance is the first-run wizard, which an instance upgraded from an earlier release never runs again. The row is written the first time somebody actually uses the client: no configuration, correct on a fresh install and on an upgrade, and an operator's edit to an allow-listed client survives because an existing row wins. **Loopback ports are ignored, and only on loopback.** RFC 8252 §7.3: a native application binds an ephemeral port, so it cannot be registered in advance. Everything else is compared whole, because a prefix match on a redirect is how authorisation codes leak. **`/api/mcp/token` is on the proxy's public list; the consent screen deliberately will not be.** A client redeeming a code has no session by definition. A person approving one is in a browser and must be signed in. **Verified:** oauth-flow.test.ts **15/15** against a real database, including the acceptance criterion (a rotated-away refresh token revokes every link in the lineage, the newest included) and the four resource-binding refusals; oauth-clients.test.ts **11/11** for the redirect and PKCE rules. Migration linter, soft-delete linter, boundaries 558 files, typecheck 11/11, biome, dead-code, check:contract. **Not verified:** no client has driven the real HTTP endpoint yet, because there is no consent screen to issue a code from a browser until P5-T08c. The token endpoint is exercised through the same functions the route calls, which is every line of it but the form parsing. |
| P5-T08b | Discovery, registration and the outbound-request rules | in_review | agung | 2026-08-30 | **The outbound-request rules land here and are not only this task's.** TECHNICAL-PLAN §11 has required them since it was written, and nothing had needed one: every channel driver calls a fixed provider host. A client metadata document is the first fetch of an address a stranger chose, so `outboundFetch` is built in `packages/adapters` and the AI base URL and webhook paths can adopt it. **Four rules, and each closes a way around the others.** The literal host is checked, then *every* resolved address rather than the first, no redirect is followed, and size and time are capped. Checking the name alone is defeated by DNS; checking only the first address is defeated by a name that answers with one public and one private address; one redirect reaches anything the address checks refused; and the size cap is enforced on what arrives rather than on `content-length`, which a hostile server is free to understate. The cloud metadata address is why it matters: a plain GET of 169.254.169.254 returns credentials on most hosting. **Not a driver, and deliberately so.** There is no vendor here, only the platform `fetch` with the rules a hostile URL requires. It lives in `packages/adapters` because that is where code touching the network lives, not because a package needs wrapping. **Three documents from one builder, served at six paths.** RFC 9728 puts the resource path after the well-known segment, so `/api/mcp` has its document at `/.well-known/oauth-protected-resource/api/mcp`. Clients differ on which they try first, and serving one is how a connection fails with nothing in a log to explain it. Two builders would be two answers to where the token endpoint lives. **Only `S256` is advertised, because only `S256` is accepted.** Advertising `plain` and then refusing it would be a downgrade any attacker could ask for and a support question for everybody else. **Open registration, which is not a hole.** Registering records a name and a set of addresses and grants nothing: every authority still comes from a person approving a specific client in a specific workspace on the consent screen. The identifier is issued by this server rather than chosen by the client, because a client that picked its own could claim another's. No `client_secret` is returned at all: every client here is public, and emitting an empty one would be worse than omitting it. **`withInstanceAdmin` for the registration write, and the choice is stated in the code.** A client registers with the instance, so there is no tenant setting to apply and `withContext` refuses an empty context. That wrapper carries one power this handler does not use; the alternative was a new wrapper opening a transaction with no tenant setting at all, which would reach the auth tables. One unused power inside a function that writes one table is the smaller hole. **A migration was almost rewritten and was not.** Marking `oauth_clients` as `instance-scope` with a declared open policy would read better than the `not-tenant-scoped` marker it shipped with at P5-T08a. Migrations here are forward-only and 0062 is already pushed, so the edit was reverted rather than made. Worth a follow-up migration if the declared-policy form is preferred. **Verified:** outbound-guard.test.ts **17/17**, oauth-discovery.test.ts **23/23**, oauth-flow 15/15 and oauth-clients 11/11 still green, packages/adapters **281/281**, end to end **117 passed** including eight new specs that drive the real unauthenticated HTTP surface: every document without a session, preflight, registration by a stranger, a dangerous redirect refused, and a client document pointed at 169.254.169.254 refused by name. typecheck 11/11, biome, boundaries 563 files, dead-code, migration and soft-delete linters. **Not verified:** no real external agent has driven the flow, because the consent screen that issues a code from a browser is P5-T08c. |
